Spin characters of the symmetric group which are proportional to linear characters in characteristic 2 2
Matthew Fayers

Abstract
For a finite group, it is interesting to determine when two ordinary irreducible representations have the same p p -modular reduction; that is, when two rows of the decomposition matrix in characteristic p p are equal, or equivalently when the corresponding p p -modular Brauer characters are the same. We complete this task for the double covers of the symmetric group when p = 2 p=2 , by determining when the 2 2 -modular reduction of an irreducible spin representation coincides with a 2 2 -modular Specht module. In fact, we obtain a more general result: we determine when an irreducible spin representation has 2 2 -modular Brauer character proportional to that of a Specht module. In the course of the proof, we use induction and restriction functors to construct a function on generalised characters which has the effect of swapping runners in abacus displays for the labelling partitions.



2020 Mathematics subject classification: 20C30, 20C20, 05E10

This is the accepted manuscript for an article published in
Annals of Representation Theory
2(1) (2025), pp. 37–83,
doi:10.5802/art.21
.

1
Introduction

When an irreducible spin representation of the symmetric group is reduced modulo
2
, it becomes a representation of the symmetric group in the usual sense, and it is an interesting problem to say exactly which representation is obtained.
In this paper, we determine when the result is “a multiple of a Specht module”, in the sense of their images in the Grothendieck group or equivalently of their Brauer characters.
That is, we determine when the
2
-modular reduction of an irreducible spin representation has Brauer character which is a multiple of that of a Specht module.

We show that the following pair of conditions on the labelling partition are necessary and sufficient for a spin representation to have a
2
-modular Brauer character proportional to that of a Specht module.
For a strict partition
\alpha
, we say that:

\diamond

\alpha
is
4
-stepped
if for every part
\alpha_{r}>4
the integer
\alpha_{r}-4
is also a part of
\alpha
; and

\diamond

\alpha
is
4
-semicongruent
if the odd parts of
\alpha
are congruent modulo
4
.

If
\alpha
satisfies both conditions, we abbreviate this by saying that
\alpha
is
4
-stepped-and-semicongruent.

To obtain the partition labelling the corresponding Specht module, observe that if
\alpha
is
4
-stepped-and-semicongruent then there are unique (up to reordering)
2
-core partitions
\sigma
and
\tau
such that the even parts of
\alpha
comprise the partition
2(\sigma+\tau)
(see
Section

1.2
below for details).
We define
{\alpha}_{\circ}
to be the partition of
|\alpha|
(unique up to conjugation) whose
2
-quotient has the form
(\sigma,\tau)
.

We denote by
\chi(\lambda)
the character of the Specht module labelled by a partition
\lambda
, and denote by
\langle\alpha\rangle
the character of an irreducible spin representation labelled by a strict partition
\alpha
(when
\alpha
has an odd number of even parts, there is actually an associate pair of characters
\langle\alpha\rangle_{+}
and
\langle\alpha\rangle_{-}
labelled by
\alpha
, but they have the same
2
-modular reduction, so we write
\langle\alpha\rangle
to denote either of them).
For any ordinary character
\varphi
, we write
\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{}
for the Brauer character of the
2
-modular reduction of the corresponding representation.

Our main result is the following.

Theorem 1.1
.

Let
\lambda
be a partition of
n
and let
\alpha
be a strict partition of
n
.
Then
\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{}
is proportional to
\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{}
if and only if
\alpha
is
4
-stepped-and-semicongruent and
\lambda\in\{{\alpha}_{\circ},{\alpha}_{\circ}^{\prime}\}
.
In this case,
\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{}=2^{\lfloor e/2\rfloor}\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{}
, where
e
is the number of even parts of
\alpha
.

Equality of Brauer characters occurs when
e\in\left\{0,1\right\}
, giving the following corollary.
The
double
of a strict partition
\alpha
, denoted
\mathrm{dbl}(\alpha)
, is the partition obtained by replacing every odd part
2k+1
with parts
k+1
and
k
, and every even part
2k
with two parts equal to
k
. Cores and quotients are defined in
Sections

2.5
and
2.6
.
Given partitions
\mu
and
\nu
, we write
\mu\sqcup\nu
for the partition obtained by combining all parts of
\mu
and
\nu
and reordering as necessary.

Corollary 1.2
.

Let
\lambda
be a partition of
n
and let
\alpha
be a strict partition of
n
.
Then
\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{}=\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{}
if and only if:

\diamond

\alpha=\kappa\sqcup\eta
, where
\kappa
is of the form
(\ldots,9,5,1)
or
(\ldots,11,7,3)
and
\eta\in\left\{\varnothing,(2),(4)\right\}
, and

\diamond

\lambda
has
2
-core
\mathrm{dbl}(\kappa)
and
2
-quotient
(\sigma,\tau)
, where
\sigma,\tau\in\left\{\varnothing,(1)\right\}
and
2(\sigma_{1}+\tau_{1})=\eta_{1}
.

1.2
implies in particular that equality of Brauer characters can occur only when the characters lie in blocks of weight
0
,
1
or
2
. In the cases of weights
0
and
1
, the equality of Brauer characters in fact implies isomorphism of representations, since in this case the modular reductions remain simple.

We wish to highlight the “runner-swapping function” (
5.4
) we introduce in the course of the proof as being of independent interest.
This function acts (up to a sign) on Specht modules by swapping a pair of runners in an abacus display for the labelling partition, or equivalently by adding all addable nodes and removing all removable nodes of a particular residue in the Young diagram (see
5.7
,
6.1
and
6.7
).
This action can be understood as the affine Weyl group acting on the set of partitions (see
[
Fay14
]
). Our approach exhibits this action as a combination of induction and restriction functors. Our expression is new; although expressing the action via induction and restriction functors has been achieved before by
[
CR08
]
, we believe our expression is simpler, and allows us to also compute its action also on spin representations.

1.1
Discussion on main problem

Fix a prime
p
.
For an ordinary character
\chi
of a double cover
\hat{\mathfrak{S}}_{n}
of the symmetric group, the Brauer character
\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{}
of the
p
-modular reduction of the representation affording
\chi
can be found by restricting
\chi
to the
p
-regular conjugacy classes of
\hat{\mathfrak{S}}_{n}
.
We are interested in the question of when two ordinary irreducible characters
\chi\neq\psi
of
\hat{\mathfrak{S}}_{n}
can give
\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{}=\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{}
, or equivalently whether an ordinary irreducible character is determined by its values on
p
-regular conjugacy classes.

Our question naturally splits into three cases, two of which are (almost) already answered.
We call a representation or character of
\hat{\mathfrak{S}}_{n}

spin
if the central element of order
2
acts non-trivially, and call it
linear
otherwise (see
Section

2.3
) (unfortunately the term
linear character
can also be used to mean a character of degree
1
; we will not use it in that sense in this paper).

\diamond

The case where
\chi
and
\psi
are both linear characters reduces to the corresponding question for the symmetric group, which was answered by Wildon
[
Wil08
, Theorem 1.1.1]
: if
\lambda,\mu\in\mathscr{P}(n)
are distinct, then
\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{}=\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{}
if and only if
p=2
and
\mu=\lambda^{\prime}
is the conjugate of
\lambda
(see
Section

2.1.2
).

\diamond

In the case where
\chi
and
\psi
are both spin characters and
p\neq 3
, this question has been answered by the second author
[
McD24
, Theorem 1.2]
:
\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{}
and
\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{}
are equal if and only if
\chi
and
\psi
are an associate pair
\langle\alpha\rangle_{+}
and
\langle\alpha\rangle_{-}
, where
\alpha
is a strict partition with an odd number of even parts and a part divisible by
p
.

It remains to consider the case where exactly one of
\chi
and
\psi
is a spin character. In this case, if
p
is odd, it is never the case that
\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{}=\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{}
: the composition factors of a
p
-modular reduction of an ordinary spin representation are always spin representations, so (the modules affording)
\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{}
and
\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{}
will have no composition factors in common. Thus we restrict attention to the case
p=2
from now on.

It will be helpful to interpret our main theorem also in terms of decomposition numbers (see
Section

2.3
for details).
It is a standard fact from modular representation theory that if
\chi
and
\psi
are ordinary characters, then
\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{}=\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{}
if and only if
[\chi:\varphi]=[\psi:\varphi]
for all modular irreducible characters
\varphi
.
Thus our question can be phrased as asking when two rows of the decomposition matrix are equal.

Example 1.3
.

The decomposition matrix of
\hat{\mathfrak{S}}_{4}
in characteristic
2
is given as follows, with rows corresponding to ordinary characters and columns to
2
-modular irreducibles.

\begin{array}[]{r|cc|}\\
&\rotatebox{90.0}{$(4)$}&\rotatebox{90.0}{$(3,1)$}\\
\hline\cr\chi(4)&1&\cdot\\
\chi(3,1)&1&1\\
\chi(2^{2})&\cdot&1\\
\chi(2,1^{2})&1&1\\
\chi(1^{4})&1&\cdot\\
\langle 4\rangle_{+}&\cdot&1\\
\ \langle 4\rangle_{-}&\cdot&1\\
\ \langle 3,1\rangle&2&1\\
\hline\cr\end{array}

We can see that if
\chi
and
\psi
are distinct ordinary irreducible characters, then
\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{}=\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{}
if and only if
\{\chi,\psi\}=\{\chi(4),\chi(1^{4})\}
,
\{\chi,\psi\}=\{\chi(3,1),\chi(2,1^{2})\}
, or
\{\chi,\psi\}\subset\{\chi(2^{2}),\langle 4\rangle_{+},\langle 4\rangle_{-}\}
.
Of these:

1.

the pairs of linear characters
\{\chi(4),\chi(1^{4})\}
and
\{\chi(3,1),\chi(2,1^{2})\}
are conjugate pairs, as predicted by
[
Wil08
]
;

2.

the pair of spin characters
\{\langle 4\rangle_{+},\langle 4\rangle_{-}\}
is an associate pair labelled by a partition with a part divisible by
2
, as predicted by
[
McD24
]
;

3.

the equality of
\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{}
with
\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{}
and
\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{}
is predicted by our main theorem.

In fact, it turns out to be rare to have
\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{}=\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{}
when only one of
\chi
and
\psi
is a spin character, because the spin characters typically have much larger degrees than the characters of the Specht modules. So we address a more general question in this paper: when are
\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{}
and
\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{}
proportional to each other? In other words, when is one row of the decomposition matrix proportional to another? Given two characters
\chi
and
\psi
whose
2
-modular reductions are proportional, we can easily find the constant of proportionality using the regularisation theorems (see
Section

2.5.4
and
Lemma

3.1
), and therefore extract the answer to our original question. Working with proportionality will actually make many of our calculations easier, because we will able to neglect constants occurring.

Having broadened our scope to proportionality, one may ask whether
p
-modular reductions of pairs of linear characters, or of pairs of spin characters, can be proportional.
The answer, except for pairs of spin characters when
p=3
, is “no”: by a straightforward extension of their arguments, the results cited above that classify equality between such pairs also rule out non-trivial proportionality. Thus our result is in fact a characterisation of all proportional pairs of
p
-modular characters of
\hat{\mathfrak{S}}_{n}
for
p\neq 3
. For
p=3
, it is possible for the
p
-modular reductions of irreducible spin characters to be proportional but unequal: for example,
\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{}=2\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{}
.

1.2
Description of the labelling partitions

We elaborate on the characterisation of a
4
-stepped-and-semicongruent strict partition
\alpha
and the definition of the corresponding
{\alpha}_{\circ}
given at the beginning of the introduction.
We use the notions of
2
-core,
2
-quotient and
4
-bar-core defined in
Sections

2.5
and
2.6
, as well as the notation
\kappa_{a}
for the
2
-core partition with largest part
a
, and
\bar{\kappa}_{a}
for the
4
-bar-core strict partition with largest part
2a-1
, satisfying
\mathrm{dbl}(\bar{\kappa}_{a})=\kappa_{a}
.
We also use the operations defined on partitions
\sqcup
,
+
, and integer multiplication on partitions, as defined in
Section

2.1.1
.

Suppose
\alpha
is
4
-stepped-and-semicongruent, and write
\alpha=\gamma\sqcup 2\beta
, where
\gamma
is the strict partition comprising the odd parts of
\alpha
.
From the
4
-stepped-and-semicongruent property, it is clear that
\gamma
is a
4
-bar-core, and in particular is the
4
-bar-core of
\alpha
, so we can write
\gamma=\bar{\kappa}_{a}
for some
a\geqslant 0
.
Meanwhile, using the
4
-stepped property again, we have

\beta=(2m,2m-2,\dots,2)\sqcup(2k-1,2k-3,\dots,1)

for some
m,k\geqslant 0
. If we let

r=m+k
and
s=|m-k+\tfrac{1}{2}|-\tfrac{1}{2}
,
then this becomes

\beta=\kappa_{r}+\kappa_{s}.

Thus the
4
-stepped-and-semicongruent strict partitions are precisely the partitions of the form
\bar{\kappa}_{a}\sqcup 2(\kappa_{r}+\kappa_{s})
for
a\geqslant 0
and
r,s\geqslant 0
.

Definition 1.4
.

Given a
4
-stepped-and-semicongruent partition
\alpha=\bar{\kappa}_{a}\sqcup 2(\kappa_{r}+\kappa_{s})
, we define
{\alpha}_{\circ}
up to conjugacy by

{(\bar{\kappa}_{a}\sqcup 2(\kappa_{r}+\kappa_{s}))}_{\circ}=[\kappa_{a};(\kappa_{r},\kappa_{s})]

where
[\kappa;\underline{\smash{\lambda}}]
denotes the partition with
2
-core
\kappa
and
2
-quotient
\underline{\smash{\lambda}}
.

Note the conjugate is
[\kappa_{a};(\kappa_{r},\kappa_{s})]^{\prime}=[\kappa_{a};(\kappa_{s},\kappa_{r})]
, so indeed this is well-defined up to conjugacy.

With this notation, our main theorem
1.1
claims that

\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{}=2^{\lfloor\frac{\max\{r,s\}}{2}\rfloor}\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{}=2^{\lfloor\frac{\max\{r,s\}}{2}\rfloor}\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{},

and that these are the only such instances of proportionality between linear and spin characters.

Example 1.5
.

Let
\alpha=(12,8,7,4,3,2)
.
Then
\alpha
is a
4
-stepped-and-semicongruent strict partition, and we can write
\alpha=\bar{\kappa}_{4}\sqcup 2(\kappa_{4}+\kappa_{2})
.
Then the partition
{\alpha}_{\circ}
is either
(12,9,6,3^{2},1^{3})
or its conjugate
(8,5^{2},3^{3},2^{3},1^{3})
, having
2
-core
\kappa_{4}
and
2
-quotient
(\kappa_{2},\kappa_{4})
or
(\kappa_{4},\kappa_{2})
respectively, as can be verified from their abacus displays below.

[\kappa_{4};(\kappa_{2},\kappa_{4})]\ =\ \hbox to20.61pt{\vbox to111.57pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 3.98993pt\lower-107.58151pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{{}}{}

Our main theorem claims that
\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{}=4\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{}=4\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{}
.

1.3
Structure of paper

Relevant background is collected in
Section

2
.
The proofs of the “if” and “only if” directions of our main theorem are logically independent, and can be read in either order.

The proof of the “only if” direction (that is, that a spin character proportional to a linear one modulo
2
must be labelled by a
4
-stepped-and-semicongruent strict partition) is contained in
Sections

3
and
4
.
The proof is by induction, using Robinson’s induction and restriction functors and the Murnaghan–Nakayama–Morris rules to constrain the partition labelling a proportional character.

The proof of the “if” direction (that is, that
4
-stepped-and-semicongruent strict partitions do label spin characters proportional to linear ones modulo
2
) is contained in
Sections

5
,
6
and
7
.
We first show the claim for
homogeneous
spin characters, then use certain combinations of Robinson’s induction and restriction functors to propagate the property of being proportional.

Acknowledgements
.

The authors worked on this project during the second author’s visit to Stacey Law at the University of Cambridge; we are grateful to the Heilbronn Institute for supporting that visit and to the Centre for Mathematical Sciences for providing facilities.

2
Background

In this paper,
\mathbb{N}
denotes the set of positive integers.
If
\epsilon\in\{0,1\}
, we write
\bar{\epsilon}=1-\epsilon
.

2.1
Partitions and Young diagrams

We recall some simple combinatorial concepts relating to representations of symmetric groups.

2.1.1
Partitions

A
partition
is an infinite weakly-decreasing sequence
\lambda=(\lambda_{1},\lambda_{2},\dots)
of integers which are eventually zero. The integers
\lambda_{1},\lambda_{2},\dots
are the
parts
of
\lambda
. Given a partition
\lambda
and a natural number
a
, we write
a\in\lambda
to mean that
a
is a part of
\lambda
.

We write
\lvert\lambda\rvert
for the sum
\lambda_{1}+\lambda_{2}+\dots
, and say that
\lambda
is a partition of
\lvert\lambda\rvert
. We write
\mathscr{P}
for the set of all partitions, and
\mathscr{P}(n)
for the set of partitions of
n
. When writing partitions, we omit trailing zeroes and group together equal parts with a superscript. We write
\varnothing
for the unique partition of
0
. For any partition
\lambda
, we write
l(\lambda)
for the number of non-zero parts of
\lambda
, which we call the
length
of
\lambda
.

We use some natural notation for combining partitions.
Given partitions
\lambda
and
\mu
, we write:

\diamond

a\lambda=(a\lambda_{1},a\lambda_{2},\dots)
for
a\in\mathbb{N}
;

\diamond

\lambda+\mu=(\lambda_{1}+\mu_{1},\lambda_{2}+\mu_{2},\dots)
;

\diamond

\lambda\sqcup\mu
for the partition obtained by combining all the parts of
\lambda
and
\mu
and writing them in decreasing order;

\diamond

\lambda\setminus\mu
for the partition obtained by retaining only those parts of
\lambda
which are not parts of
\mu
.

A
bipartition
is an ordered pair
\underline{\smash{\lambda}}=(\lambda^{(0)},\lambda^{(1)})
of partitions, which we call the
components
of
\underline{\smash{\lambda}}
. We write
\lvert\underline{\smash{\lambda}}\rvert=\lvert\lambda^{(0)}\rvert+\lvert\lambda^{(1)}\rvert
, and say that
\underline{\smash{\lambda}}
is a bipartition of
\lvert\lambda\rvert
.

2.1.2
Young diagrams

The
Young diagram
of a partition
\lambda
is the set

[\lambda]=\left\{\left.(r,c)\in\mathbb{N}^{2}\ \right|\ \smash{c\leqslant\lambda_{r}}\right\},

drawn in the plane using the English convention. We call the elements of the Young diagram the
nodes
of
\lambda
. In general, a node means an element of
\mathbb{N}^{2}
.
We may not distinguish between a partition and its Young diagram; for example, given partitions
\lambda
and
\mu
, we write:

\diamond

\lambda\cap\mu
for the partition with Young diagram the intersection of the Young diagrams of
\lambda
and
\mu
;

\diamond

\mu\subseteq\lambda
to mean that every node of
\mu
is also a node of
\lambda
;

\diamond

\lambda/\mu
for the set of nodes of
\lambda
which are not nodes of
\mu
, provided that
\mu\subseteq\lambda
.

The
conjugate
of a partition
\lambda
, denoted
\lambda^{\prime}
, is the partition whose Young diagram is obtained by reflecting that of
\lambda
in the main diagonal.
Thus the parts of
\lambda^{\prime}
are the lengths of the columns of
\lambda
.

A node
(r,c)
of
\lambda
is
removable
if it can be removed from
[\lambda]
to leave the Young diagram of a smaller partition (that is, if
c=\lambda_{r}>\lambda_{r+1}
), while a node not in
\lambda
is an addable node of
\lambda
if it can be added to
[\lambda]
to produce a Young diagram of a larger partition.
The
rim
of a partition
\lambda
is the set of nodes
(r,c)\in[\lambda]
such that
(r+1,c+1)\notin[\lambda]
. All removable nodes lie in the rim, but not all nodes in the rim are removable.
We denote by
\lambda^{-\epsilon}
the partition obtained by removing all removable
\epsilon
-nodes.

If
\lambda,\mu\in\mathscr{P}(n)
, then we say that
\lambda

dominates

\mu
(and write
\lambda\trianglerighteqslant\mu
) if

\lambda_{1}+\dots+\lambda_{r}\geqslant\mu_{1}+\dots+\mu_{r}

for every
r\geqslant 0
. Another way of saying this is that
[\mu]
can be obtained from
[\lambda]
by moving some nodes to lower positions.

2.1.3
Residues, diagonals and ladders

The
residue
of a node
(r,c)
is the residue of
c-r
modulo
2
. For
\epsilon\in\{0,1\}
, an
\epsilon
-node
means a node of residue
\epsilon
.

For
d
in
\mathbb{Z}
, the
d
th
diagonal
in
\mathbb{N}^{2}
is the set of all nodes
(r,c)
for which
c-r=d
(that is, a top-left to bottom-right line of nodes of constant residue).
We write
\mathcal{D}_{d}
for the
d
th diagonal.

For
l\in\mathbb{N}
, the
l
th
ladder
in
\mathbb{N}^{2}
is the set of all nodes
(r,c)
for which
r+c=l+1
(that is, a bottom-left to top-right line of nodes of constant residue).
We write
\mathcal{L}_{l}
for the
l
th ladder, and say that
\mathcal{L}_{m}
is
larger
than
\mathcal{L}_{l}
when
m>l
.

If
\lambda\in\mathscr{P}
, the
2
-regularisation
of
\lambda
is the partition obtained by sliding the nodes of
\lambda
as far up their ladders as they will go. We write this partition as
\lambda^{\mathrm{reg}}
. It is easy to see that if
\lambda,\mu\in\mathscr{P}
then
\lambda^{\mathrm{reg}}=\mu^{\mathrm{reg}}
if and only if
\lvert\lambda\cap\mathcal{L}_{l}\rvert=\lvert\mu\cap\mathcal{L}_{l}\rvert
for every
l
.

2.2
Strict partitions

Now we outline the combinatorial notions relating to spin representations of the double covers of the symmetric group.

A
strict partition
is a partition
\alpha
such that
\alpha_{i}>\alpha_{i+1}
for all
i\leqslant l(\alpha)
.
We write
\mathscr{D}
for the set of all strict partitions, and
\mathscr{D}(n)
for the set of strict partitions of
n
.

As in the introduction, the
double
of a strict partition
\alpha
is the partition

\mathrm{dbl}(\alpha)=\left(\left\lceil\mathord{\raise 0.49991pt\hbox{$\displaystyle\genfrac{}{}{0.4pt}{}{\al_1}{2}$}}\right\rceil,\left\lfloor\mathord{\raise 0.49991pt\hbox{$\displaystyle\genfrac{}{}{0.4pt}{}{\al_1}{2}$}}\right\rfloor,\left\lceil\mathord{\raise 0.49991pt\hbox{$\displaystyle\genfrac{}{}{0.4pt}{}{\al_2}{2}$}}\right\rceil,\left\lfloor\mathord{\raise 0.49991pt\hbox{$\displaystyle\genfrac{}{}{0.4pt}{}{\al_2}{2}$}}\right\rfloor,\dots\right).

The
spin-residue
of a node
(r,c)
is the residue of
\left\lfloor c/2\right\rfloor
modulo
2
. For
\epsilon\in\{0,1\}
, an
\epsilon
-spin-node
means a node of spin-residue
\epsilon
.

A node
(r,c)
of
\alpha
is
spin-removable
if it can be removed, possibly together with other nodes of the same spin-residue, to leave a strict partition.
A node
(r,c)
which is not a node of
\alpha
is
spin-addable
if it can be added, possibly together with other nodes of the same spin-residue, to obtain a strict partition.
An
\epsilon
-spin-removable node
means a spin-removable
\epsilon
-spin-node, and likewise with “addable” in place of “removable”.
We denote by
\alpha^{-\epsilon}
the strict partition obtained by removing from
\alpha
all
\epsilon
-spin-removable nodes.

For example, let
\alpha=(7,5,4,1)
. The spin-residues of the nodes of
\alpha
are illustrated in the following diagram.

\vphantom{1}\smash{0}
\vphantom{1}\smash{1}
\vphantom{1}\smash{1}
\vphantom{1}\smash{0}
\vphantom{1}\smash{0}
\vphantom{1}\smash{1}
\vphantom{1}\smash{1}
\vphantom{1}\smash{0}
\vphantom{1}\smash{1}
\vphantom{1}\smash{1}
\vphantom{1}\smash{0}
\vphantom{1}\smash{0}
\vphantom{1}\smash{0}
\vphantom{1}\smash{1}
\vphantom{1}\smash{1}
\vphantom{1}\smash{0}
\vphantom{1}\smash{0}

The
0
-spin-removable nodes are
(2,5)
,
(3,4)
and
(4,1)
, and
\alpha^{-0}=(7,4,3)
; the
0
-spin-addable nodes are
(1,8)
and
(1,9)
.

2.3
Irreducible characters of the symmetric group and its double cover

For
n\geqslant 1
, let
\hat{\mathfrak{S}}_{n}
denote the group with generators
t_{1},\dots,t_{n-1},z
and defining relations

\displaystyle t_{1}^{2}=\dots=t_{n-1}^{2}

\displaystyle=z^{2}=1,

\displaystyle t_{i}z

\displaystyle=zt_{i}

\displaystyle\text{for }1\leqslant i\leqslant n-1,

\displaystyle t_{i}t_{i+1}t_{i}

\displaystyle=t_{i+1}t_{i}t_{i+1}

\displaystyle\text{for }1\leqslant i\leqslant n-2,

\displaystyle t_{i}t_{j}

\displaystyle=zt_{j}t_{i}

\displaystyle\text{for }1\leqslant i\leqslant j-1\leqslant n-2.

Then
\hat{\mathfrak{S}}_{n}
is a double cover of the symmetric group
\mathfrak{S}_{n}
, and is a Schur cover of
\mathfrak{S}_{n}
provided
n\geqslant 4
. The ordinary representation theory of
\hat{\mathfrak{S}}_{n}
is described in detail in the book by Hoffman and Humphreys
[
HH92
]
, and the modular theory is addressed in Kleshchev’s book
[
Kle05
]
. We will be concerned with representations in characteristic
0
and characteristic
2
. (We remark that there is another double cover of
\mathfrak{S}_{n}
, obtained by replacing the relations
t_{i}^{2}=1
with
t_{i}^{2}=z
; our results apply equally to either double cover.)

The element
z
is a central involution, so acts as a scalar
\pm 1
on any irreducible representation (over any field). The irreducible representations on which
z
acts as
1
are precisely the lifts of the irreducible representations of
\mathfrak{S}_{n}
. Irreducible representations on which
z
does not act as the identity are called
spin representations
, and (in characteristic
0
) their characters are called
spin characters
.
The irreducible characters which are not spin characters are called
linear characters

.
We write
\left(\ :\ \right)
for the usual inner product on ordinary characters, extended bilinearly to generalised characters.

The classification of ordinary irreducible representations of
\mathfrak{S}_{n}
is very well known. For
\lambda\in\mathscr{P}(n)
, let
\operatorname{S}^{\lambda}
denote the
Specht module
defined over a field of characteristic
0
(see
[
Jam78a
, Section 4]
, for example), and let
\chi(\lambda)
denote its character. Then the set
\left\{\left.\chi(\lambda)\ \right|\ \smash{\lambda\in\mathscr{P}(n)}\right\}
is a complete irredundant list of ordinary irreducible characters of
\mathfrak{S}_{n}
. We will treat
\operatorname{S}^{\lambda}
as a module for
\hat{\mathfrak{S}}_{n}
by letting
z
act as the identity, and correspondingly treat
\chi(\lambda)
as a character for
\hat{\mathfrak{S}}_{n}
.

The classification of ordinary irreducible spin characters of
\hat{\mathfrak{S}}_{n}
goes back to Schur
[
Sch11
]
(although, remarkably, a construction for the corresponding representations was not found until 1990, by Nazarov
[
Naz90
]
). Let
\alpha\in\mathscr{D}(n)
, and say that
\alpha
is
even
or
odd
as the number of positive even parts of
\alpha
is even or odd. If
\alpha
is even, then Schur constructs an irreducible character
\langle\alpha\rangle
corresponding to
\alpha
. If
\alpha
is odd, then Schur gives two characters
\langle\alpha\rangle_{+}
and
\langle\alpha\rangle_{-}
corresponding to
\alpha
. These characters together give a complete irredundant list of ordinary irreducible spin characters of
\hat{\mathfrak{S}}_{n}
.

For studying
2
-modular reductions, it will be helpful to adopt some notation introduced in
[
Fay20
]
for spin characters.
For any
\alpha\in\mathscr{D}
, we define the generalised character

\mathopen{\hbox{${\langle}$}\kern-2.72221pt\hbox{${\langle}$}}\alpha\mathclose{\hbox{${\rangle}$}\kern-2.72221pt\hbox{${\rangle}$}}=\begin{cases}\langle\alpha\rangle&\text{if $\alpha$ is even},\\
\frac{1}{\sqrt{2}}(\langle\alpha\rangle_{+}+\langle\alpha\rangle_{-})&\text{if $\alpha$ is odd}.\end{cases}

Using the generalised characters
\mathopen{\hbox{${\langle}$}\kern-2.72221pt\hbox{${\langle}$}}\alpha\mathclose{\hbox{${\rangle}$}\kern-2.72221pt\hbox{${\rangle}$}}
simplifies several results for spin representations, such as Schur’s degree formula
[
Sch11
]
, the Bessenrodt–Olsson regularisation theorem (
2.4
below), and the branching rule (
2.6
below).
Since the
2
-modular reductions of
\langle\alpha\rangle_{+}
and
\langle\alpha\rangle_{-}
are equal (as they differ only on elements of even order), and since we are concerned only with proportionality, there is no harm in working with these generalised characters
\mathopen{\hbox{${\langle}$}\kern-2.72221pt\hbox{${\langle}$}}\alpha\mathclose{\hbox{${\rangle}$}\kern-2.72221pt\hbox{${\rangle}$}}
.

We also need to recall the classification of irreducible
\hat{\mathfrak{S}}_{n}
-modules in characteristic
2
. Obviously in characteristic
2
there are no irreducible spin representations, so the irreducible representations of
\hat{\mathfrak{S}}_{n}
are precisely the lifts of the irreducible representations of
\mathfrak{S}_{n}
. These are afforded by the
James modules

\operatorname{D}^{\mu}
for
\mu\in\mathscr{D}
: the James module
\operatorname{D}^{\mu}
can be constructed as the unique simple quotient of the Specht module
\operatorname{S}^{\mu}
. We write
\varphi(\mu)
for the Brauer character of
\operatorname{D}^{\mu}
, and for an ordinary character
\chi
, we define
[\chi:\varphi(\mu)]
to be the multiplicity of
\varphi(\mu)
as a composition factor of a
2
-modular reduction of
\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{}
. These multiplicities, as
\chi
ranges over the ordinary irreducible characters, are the
decomposition numbers
for
\hat{\mathfrak{S}}_{n}
.

2.4
Hooks, bars and character values

In this section we recall the Murnaghan–Nakayama rule for computing character values for the symmetric group, and its spin version, proved by Morris. In fact for our purposes we can make do with very simplified versions of these rules.

2.4.1
Hooks and the Murnaghan–Nakayama rule

We begin by recalling the well-known concept of rim-hooks of partitions.
Take
k\in\mathbb{N}
and
\lambda
a partition. A
k
-rim-hook
, or rim-hook of length
k
, is a connected subset of the rim of
\lambda
comprising
k
nodes which can be removed to leave the Young diagram of a smaller partition. For a rim-hook
\delta
in
\lambda
, the smaller partition obtained by removing
\delta
is denoted
\lambda\setminus\delta
.

Let
R(\lambda,k)
be the set of partitions that can be obtained by removing a
k
-rim-hook from
\lambda
. Given
\nu\in\mathscr{P}(n)
, let
\chi(\lambda)(\nu)
be the value of the character
\chi(\lambda)
on elements of
\mathfrak{S}_{n}
of cycle type
\nu
. Now a simplified version of the Murnaghan–Nakayama rule (see for example
[
Jam78a
, 21.1]
) can be stated as follows.

Theorem 2.1
.

Suppose
\lambda\in\mathscr{P}(n)
and
k\in\mathbb{N}
. For each
\mu\in R(\lambda,k)
there is a non-zero constant
c_{\mu}
such that for any
\nu\in\mathscr{P}(n-k)
,

\chi(\lambda)(\nu\sqcup(k))=\sum_{\mu\in R(\lambda,k)}c_{\mu}\chi(\mu)(\nu).

2.4.2
Odd bars and Morris’s rule

Now we consider spin characters, and explain Morris’s rule (as given by Hoffman and Humphreys
[
HH92
, Theorem 10.1]
). Because we are concerned with
2
-modular Brauer characters in this paper, we need only consider
2
-regular conjugacy classes in
\hat{\mathfrak{S}}_{n}
. In fact these are in one-to-one correspondence with
2
-regular conjugacy classes in
\mathfrak{S}_{n}
: for each
2
-regular conjugacy class
C
in
\mathfrak{S}_{n}
, there are two conjugacy classes in
\hat{\mathfrak{S}}_{n}
of elements that map to
C
under the quotient map
\hat{\mathfrak{S}}_{n}\to\mathfrak{S}_{n}
, and only one of these consists of elements of odd order. So
2
-regular conjugacy classes in
\hat{\mathfrak{S}}_{n}
can be labelled by partitions of
n
into odd parts. Given
\alpha\in\mathscr{D}
and a partition
\nu
into odd parts, we write
\mathopen{\hbox{${\langle}$}\kern-2.72221pt\hbox{${\langle}$}}\alpha\mathclose{\hbox{${\rangle}$}\kern-2.72221pt\hbox{${\rangle}$}}(\nu)
for the value of
\mathopen{\hbox{${\langle}$}\kern-2.72221pt\hbox{${\langle}$}}\alpha\mathclose{\hbox{${\rangle}$}\kern-2.72221pt\hbox{${\rangle}$}}
on the
2
-regular conjugacy class corresponding to
\nu
.

The appropriate analogue of rim-hooks for strict partitions is
bars
. Take
k\in\mathbb{N}
odd and
\alpha\in\mathscr{D}
. A
k
-bar
in
\alpha
is one of the following:

\diamond

a part of size equal to
k
;

\diamond

a pair of parts whose sizes sum to
k
;

\diamond

the last
k
nodes in a part of size
\alpha_{r}>k
such that
\alpha_{r}-k
is not already a part of
\alpha
.

Removing a
k
-bar from
\alpha
means removing the bar and, if necessary, reordering the remaining parts to form a strict partition.

Let
S(\alpha,k)
denote the set of strict partitions that can be obtained from
\alpha
by removing a
k
-bar. Now a simplified version of Morris’s rule can be stated as follows.

Theorem 2.2
.

Suppose
\alpha\in\mathscr{D}(n)
and
k\in\mathbb{N}
is odd. For each
\beta\in S(\alpha,k)
there is a non-zero constant
d_{\beta}
such that for any partition
\nu
of
n-k
into odd parts,

\mathopen{\hbox{${\langle}$}\kern-2.72221pt\hbox{${\langle}$}}\alpha\mathclose{\hbox{${\rangle}$}\kern-2.72221pt\hbox{${\rangle}$}}(\nu\sqcup(k))=\sum_{\beta\in S(\alpha,k)}d_{\beta}\mathopen{\hbox{${\langle}$}\kern-2.72221pt\hbox{${\langle}$}}\beta\mathclose{\hbox{${\rangle}$}\kern-2.72221pt\hbox{${\rangle}$}}(\nu).

2.5
Blocks

The
2
-block structure of
\hat{\mathfrak{S}}_{n}
was determined by Bessenrodt and Olsson
[
BO97
]
, extending the classical result of Brauer and Robinson (the “Nakayama Conjecture”) for
\mathfrak{S}_{n}
. We summarise the essential points here.

2.5.1
Cores and weights

Suppose
\lambda\in\mathscr{P}
and
k\in\mathbb{N}
. The
k
-core
of
\lambda
is the partition obtained by successively removing
k
-rim-hooks until no more remain. It is well-known that this partition is independent of the choice of
k
-rim-hook removed at each stage. The
k
-weight
of
\lambda
is the number of
k
-rim-hooks removed to reach the
k
-core, or equivalently the number of rim-hooks of
\lambda
of size divisible by
k
. By the Murnaghan–Nakayama rule (
2.1
), the
k
-weight of
\lambda
is the maximum
w
such that the character value
\chi(\lambda)(k^{w},1^{n-kw})
is non-zero.

When
k=2
, it is easy to see that the
2
-cores are precisely the partitions of the form
(r,r-1,\dots,1)
for
r\geqslant 0
. In this paper we will write
\kappa_{r}=(r,r-1,\dots,1)
.

The Brauer–Robinson Theorem (which applies equally well for
\hat{\mathfrak{S}}_{n}
) says that
\chi(\lambda)
and
\chi(\mu)
lie in the same
k
-block if and only if
\lambda
and
\mu
have the same
k
-core. This result also gives the distribution of the irreducible Brauer characters
\varphi(\mu)
into blocks, since
\varphi(\mu)
is a composition factor of
\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{}
.

This means that we can define the
k
-core
and
k
-weight
of a block of
\hat{\mathfrak{S}}_{n}
to be the common
k
-core and
k
-weight of the partitions
\lambda
labelling characters
\chi(\lambda)
in that block.

2.5.2
Bar-cores and bar-weights

Now we come to spin characters. For
\alpha\in\mathscr{D}
and
k
odd, the
k
-bar-core
of
\alpha
is the strict partition obtained by successively removing
k
-bars until none remain, and the
k
-bar-weight
of
\alpha
is the number of
k
-bars removed to reach the
k
-bar-core. By Morris’s rule (
2.2
), the
k
-bar-weight of
\alpha
is the maximum
w
such that the character value
\mathopen{\hbox{${\langle}$}\kern-2.72221pt\hbox{${\langle}$}}\alpha\mathclose{\hbox{${\rangle}$}\kern-2.72221pt\hbox{${\rangle}$}}(k^{w},1^{n-kw})
is non-zero.

To work in characteristic
2
, we require a slightly different definition:
for
\alpha\in\mathscr{D}
, the
4
-bar-core
of
\alpha
is obtained by repeatedly applying moves of the following types:

\diamond

deleting an even part;

\diamond

deleting two parts whose sum is a multiple of
4
;

\diamond

replacing an odd part
\alpha_{r}>4
with
\alpha_{r}-4
, if
\alpha_{r}-4
is not already a part of
\alpha
.

(This permits removing parts of size equal to
2
in addition to “
4
-bars” defined analogously to the odd case.)
The
4
-bar-weight of
\alpha
is the total number of nodes removed in this way divided by
2
.

The possible
4
-bar-cores are the partitions
(4t-1,4t-5,\dots,3)
and
(4t+1,4t-3,\dots,1)
for
t\geqslant 0
. For
r\geqslant 1
, we write
\bar{\kappa}_{r}
for the
4
-bar-core with largest part
2r-1
, and we set
\bar{\kappa}_{0}=\varnothing
.
Note that the function
\tau\mapsto\mathrm{dbl}(\tau)
gives a bijection from the set of
4
-bar-cores to the set of
2
-cores, sending
\bar{\kappa}_{r}
to
\kappa_{r}
for every
r
. (The inverse of this bijection is given by
\nu\mapsto\mathrm{dbl}(\nu)^{\prime}
.)

Now we can describe the distribution of spin characters into blocks
[
BO97
, Theorem 4.1]
: suppose
\alpha\in\mathscr{D}
, and let
\gamma
be the
4
-bar-core of
\alpha
; then the spin character
\mathopen{\hbox{${\langle}$}\kern-2.72221pt\hbox{${\langle}$}}\alpha\mathclose{\hbox{${\rangle}$}\kern-2.72221pt\hbox{${\rangle}$}}
lies in the
2
-block of
\hat{\mathfrak{S}}_{n}
with
2
-core
\mathrm{dbl}(\gamma)
.

2.5.3
RoCK blocks

Say that the block with
2
-core
\kappa_{a}=(a,a-1,\ldots,1)
and
2
-weight
w
is
RoCK
if
a\geqslant w-1
. RoCK blocks, which are often also called
Rouquier blocks
, are particularly well understood, and part of the proof of our main theorem will involve reducing to the case of RoCK blocks.

2.5.4
Regularisation theorems

A very useful tool in the study of decomposition numbers for the symmetric groups is James’s regularisation theorem
[
Jam76
, Theorem A]
. For the double cover
\hat{\mathfrak{S}}_{n}
in characteristic
2
, a useful analogue was proved by Bessenrodt and Olsson
[
BO97
, Theorem 5.2]
.
These results are as follows, where the double of a strict partition is as defined in
Section

2.2
and the regularisation of a partition is as defined in
Section

2.1.3
.

Theorem 2.3
.

Suppose
\lambda\in\mathscr{P}
.
Then
[\chi(\lambda):\varphi(\lambda^{\mathrm{reg}})]=1
, while
[\chi(\lambda):\varphi(\mu)]=0
unless
\mu\trianglerighteqslant\lambda^{\mathrm{reg}}
.

Theorem 2.4
.

Suppose
\alpha\in\mathscr{D}
, and let
e
be the number of even parts of
\alpha
. Then
[\mathopen{\hbox{${\langle}$}\kern-2.72221pt\hbox{${\langle}$}}\alpha\mathclose{\hbox{${\rangle}$}\kern-2.72221pt\hbox{${\rangle}$}}:\varphi(\mathrm{dbl}(\alpha)^{\mathrm{reg}})]=2^{e/2}
, while
[\mathopen{\hbox{${\langle}$}\kern-2.72221pt\hbox{${\langle}$}}\alpha\mathclose{\hbox{${\rangle}$}\kern-2.72221pt\hbox{${\rangle}$}}:\varphi(\mu)]=0
unless
\mu\trianglerighteqslant\mathrm{dbl}(\alpha)^{\mathrm{reg}}
.

2.5.5
Content and spin-content

The block classification can alternatively be expressed in terms of residues of nodes. Define the
content
of a partition
\lambda
to be the multiset of residues of its nodes. It was proved by Littlewood that two partitions of
n
have the same
2
-core if and only if they have the same content
[
Lit51
, p. 347]
. So given a block of
\hat{\mathfrak{S}}_{n}
, we may define its content to be the common contents of the partitions labelling characters
\chi(\lambda)
in the block.

To extend this idea to spin characters, define the
spin-content
of a strict partition
\alpha
to be the multiset of spin-residues of its nodes. Then
\mathopen{\hbox{${\langle}$}\kern-2.72221pt\hbox{${\langle}$}}\alpha\mathclose{\hbox{${\rangle}$}\kern-2.72221pt\hbox{${\rangle}$}}
lies in the block whose content coincides with the spin-content of
\alpha
(as can be shown, for example, by observing that the spin-content of a partition equals the content of its double
[
BO97
, Lemma 3.9]
and using
2.4
above).

2.6
Abacus displays and 2-quotients

Given
\lambda\in\mathscr{P}
, take an integer
r\geqslant l(\lambda)
, and define the
beta-numbers

\beta_{i}=\lambda_{i}+r-i
for
i=1,\dots,r
. Take an abacus with two vertical runners; label these runners
0
and
1
from left to right. Mark positions
0,2,4,\dots
from the top down on runner
0
, and
1,3,5,\dots
from the top down on runner
1
. Now place
r
beads on the abacus, at positions
\beta_{1},\dots,\beta_{r}
. The resulting configuration is the
r
-bead abacus display
for
\lambda
.

The
2
-quotient
of a partition
\lambda
is a bipartition constructed from an abacus display for
\lambda
by viewing each runner in isolation as a
1
-runner abacus and reading off the corresponding partitions. This requires a choice of convention with regard to the number of beads used in the abacus display and which runner corresponds to which component of the bipartition. In this paper we follow a slightly unusual convention: we choose the number of beads
r
so that runner
1
has at least as many beads as runner
0
(or equivalently so that the number of beads is congruent modulo
2
to the length of the
2
-core of
\lambda
), and then define the
\epsilon
th component of the
2
-quotient from the
\epsilon
th runner (explicitly, the
2
-quotient is
(\lambda^{(0)},\lambda^{(1)})
where
\lambda^{(\epsilon)}_{i}
is the number of empty spaces above the
i
th lowest bead on runner
\epsilon
). This can alternatively be described as follows: given any
r
-bead abacus display, let the
1
st component
\lambda^{(1)}
correspond to the runner with strictly more beads if such a runner exists, or to runner
1
if the numbers of beads on the runners are equal.

(The more usual convention for the
2
-quotient (see for example
[
JK81
, Section 2.7]
) is to take
r
always to be even and let the
\epsilon
th component
\lambda^{(\epsilon)}
correspond to runner
\epsilon
; our choice will be more convenient when we consider RoCK partitions. The analogue for odd primes of our convention is used in
[
Fay16
]
, where it is called the
ordered
p
-quotient
.)

It is well-known (and easy to see on the abacus) that a partition is defined by its
2
-core and
2
-quotient. Conversely, given a
2
-core
\kappa
and a bipartition
\underline{\smash{\lambda}}=(\lambda^{(0)},\lambda^{(1)})
, there is a partition with
2
-core
\kappa
and
2
-quotient
\underline{\smash{\lambda}}
. We write this as
[\kappa;(\lambda^{(0)},\lambda^{(1)})]
.

2.7
Induction and restriction

Induction and restriction between symmetric groups of different sizes is one of the most important tools in the representation theory of these groups, and the same applies to the double covers. Robinson’s
i
-induction and -restriction functors, which give more powerful results in positive characteristic, likewise extend to
\hat{\mathfrak{S}}_{n}
. We summarise the results we will need here, following the account in
[
Fay18
]
, where references and further details can be found.

Given a character
\chi
for
\hat{\mathfrak{S}}_{n}
, we write
\chi{}\downarrow_{\hat{\mathfrak{S}}_{n-1}}
for its restriction to
\hat{\mathfrak{S}}_{n-1}
, and
\chi{}\uparrow^{\hat{\mathfrak{S}}_{n+1}}
for the induced character for
\hat{\mathfrak{S}}_{n+1}
. Now suppose
\chi
lies in a single block, and write the content of this block as
\{0^{a},1^{b}\}
. Define
e_{0}\chi
to be the component of
\chi{}\downarrow_{\hat{\mathfrak{S}}_{n-1}}
lying in the block with content
\{0^{a-1},1^{b}\}
if there is such a block, and
e_{0}\chi=0
otherwise. Define
f_{0}\chi
to be the component of
\chi{}\uparrow^{\hat{\mathfrak{S}}_{n+1}}
lying in the block with content
\{0^{a+1},1^{b}\}
if there is such a block, or
0
otherwise. Define the characters
e_{1}\chi
and
f_{1}\chi
similarly. It follows from the branching rules for
\hat{\mathfrak{S}}_{n}
and the block classification that
\chi{}\downarrow_{\hat{\mathfrak{S}}_{n-1}}=e_{0}\chi+e_{1}\chi
and
\chi{}\uparrow^{\hat{\mathfrak{S}}_{n+1}}=f_{0}\chi+f_{1}\chi
for any
\chi
.

Fix
\epsilon\in\{0,1\}
for the rest of this subsection. The functor
e_{\epsilon}
is defined for any
n>0
, so we can define the power
e_{\epsilon}^{r}
and the divided power
e_{\epsilon}^{(r)}=e_{\epsilon}^{r}/r!
, and similarly for
f_{\epsilon}
.
These functors can be applied either to ordinary characters or
2
-modular Brauer characters, and commute with reduction modulo
2
.

The classical branching rule for induction and restriction of ordinary irreducible characters of symmetric groups extends to the double covers in characteristic
2
, and can be phrased in terms of the functors
e_{i}
as follows.

Theorem 2.5
.

Suppose
\lambda\in\mathscr{P}(n)
and
\mu\in\mathscr{P}(n-r)
, and
\epsilon\in\{0,1\}
. If
\mu
is obtained from
\lambda
by removing
r

\epsilon
-nodes, then
\left(e_{\epsilon}^{(r)}\chi(\lambda):\chi(\mu)\right)=\left(f_{\epsilon}^{(r)}\chi(\mu):\chi(\lambda)\right)=1
; otherwise
\left(e_{\epsilon}^{(r)}\chi(\lambda):\chi(\mu)\right)=\left(f_{\epsilon}^{(r)}\chi(\mu):\chi(\lambda)\right)=0
.

The branching rule for spin characters is given as follows (slightly rephrased from
[
Fay20
]
).

Theorem 2.6

(
[
Fay20
, Proposition 4.11]
)
.

Suppose
\alpha\in\mathscr{D}(n)
and
\beta\in\mathscr{D}(n-r)
, and
\epsilon\in\{0,1\}
. Then
\left(e_{\epsilon}^{(r)}\mathopen{\hbox{${\langle}$}\kern-2.72221pt\hbox{${\langle}$}}\alpha\mathclose{\hbox{${\rangle}$}\kern-2.72221pt\hbox{${\rangle}$}}:\mathopen{\hbox{${\langle}$}\kern-2.72221pt\hbox{${\langle}$}}\beta\mathclose{\hbox{${\rangle}$}\kern-2.72221pt\hbox{${\rangle}$}}\right)
and
\left(f_{\epsilon}^{(r)}\mathopen{\hbox{${\langle}$}\kern-2.72221pt\hbox{${\langle}$}}\beta\mathclose{\hbox{${\rangle}$}\kern-2.72221pt\hbox{${\rangle}$}}:\mathopen{\hbox{${\langle}$}\kern-2.72221pt\hbox{${\langle}$}}\alpha\mathclose{\hbox{${\rangle}$}\kern-2.72221pt\hbox{${\rangle}$}}\right)
are non-zero if and only if
\beta
is obtained by removing
r

\epsilon
-spin-nodes from
\alpha
.
If this is the case, let
b
be the number of values
c\geqslant 2
such that
\alpha/\beta
contains a node in column
c
but does not contain any nodes in columns
c-1
and
c+1
. Then

\left(e_{\epsilon}^{(r)}\mathopen{\hbox{${\langle}$}\kern-2.72221pt\hbox{${\langle}$}}\alpha\mathclose{\hbox{${\rangle}$}\kern-2.72221pt\hbox{${\rangle}$}}:\mathopen{\hbox{${\langle}$}\kern-2.72221pt\hbox{${\langle}$}}\beta\mathclose{\hbox{${\rangle}$}\kern-2.72221pt\hbox{${\rangle}$}}\right)=\left(f_{\epsilon}^{(r)}\mathopen{\hbox{${\langle}$}\kern-2.72221pt\hbox{${\langle}$}}\beta\mathclose{\hbox{${\rangle}$}\kern-2.72221pt\hbox{${\rangle}$}}:\mathopen{\hbox{${\langle}$}\kern-2.72221pt\hbox{${\langle}$}}\alpha\mathclose{\hbox{${\rangle}$}\kern-2.72221pt\hbox{${\rangle}$}}\right)=2^{b/2}.

We remark that the integer
b
in
2.6
can also be characterised as the number of even integers which are parts of
\alpha
or
\beta
but not both.

3
First results on proportionality

Our first step towards proving the “only if” direction of the main theorem, as well as establishing the claimed constant of proportionality, is to
examine the consequences of the regularisation theorems
2.3
and
2.4
.
To say that
\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{}
is proportional to
\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{}
is to say that there is a scalar
a
such that
[\mathopen{\hbox{${\langle}$}\kern-2.72221pt\hbox{${\langle}$}}\alpha\mathclose{\hbox{${\rangle}$}\kern-2.72221pt\hbox{${\rangle}$}}:\varphi(\mu)]=a[\chi(\lambda):\varphi(\mu)]
for every
\mu\in\mathscr{D}
.
The regularisation theorems substantially restrict the possible pairs
(\lambda,\alpha)
for which this can happen, as well as determining the constant
a
.
The following result is immediate from
2.3
and
2.4
.

Lemma 3.1
.

Suppose
\lambda\in\mathscr{P}
and
\alpha\in\mathscr{D}
, with
\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{}\propto\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{}
. Then:

1.

\lambda^{\mathrm{reg}}=\mathrm{dbl}(\alpha)^{\mathrm{reg}}

(that is,
\lambda
can be obtained from
\mathrm{dbl}(\alpha)
by sliding nodes along the
2
-ladders);

2.

\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{}=2^{e/2}\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{}
, where
e
is the number of even parts of
\alpha
.

Part
Item

2
of this lemma establishes the constant of proportionality claimed in our main theorem
1.1
.

Next we examine the consequences of the Murnaghan–Nakayama–Morris rules for character values given in
Section

2.4
. First we compare
k
-weight and
k
-bar-weight.

Lemma 3.2
.

Let
\lambda\in\mathscr{P}
and
\alpha\in\mathscr{D}
, with
\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{}\propto\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{}
. Then for any odd integer
k
, the
k
-weight of
\lambda
equals the
k
-bar-weight of
\alpha
.

The characters
\mathopen{\hbox{${\langle}$}\kern-2.72221pt\hbox{${\langle}$}}\alpha\mathclose{\hbox{${\rangle}$}\kern-2.72221pt\hbox{${\rangle}$}}
and
\chi(\lambda)
vanish on precisely the same
2
-regular classes. In particular, the
k
-weight of
\lambda
and the
k
-bar-weight of
\alpha
are both equal to the largest
w
such that
\mathopen{\hbox{${\langle}$}\kern-2.72221pt\hbox{${\langle}$}}\alpha\mathclose{\hbox{${\rangle}$}\kern-2.72221pt\hbox{${\rangle}$}}
and
\chi(\lambda)
are non-zero on elements of cycle type
(k^{w},1^{n-kw})
.
∎

Observe that any strict partition has a unique largest bar of odd length (if all parts are odd, it is the largest part; if all parts are even, it is the largest part minus one node; otherwise, it is the largest odd part together with the largest even part).

Proposition 3.3
.

Suppose
\alpha\in\mathscr{D}
has at least one odd part.
Let
\pi
be the unique largest bar of odd length in
\alpha
, and let
k
be its length.
If
\lambda\in\mathscr{P}
with
\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{}\propto\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{}
, then
\lambda
has a unique
k
-rim-hook
\delta
, and
\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{}\propto\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{}
.

It is easy to see that
\alpha\!\setminus\!\pi
has no
k
-bars, since the largest bar of odd length in
\alpha\!\setminus\!\pi
is at most the sum of the largest odd part and largest even part of
\alpha\!\setminus\!\pi
(taking these to be zero if no such parts exist), which is strictly less than the sum of those for
\alpha
.
So
\alpha
has
k
-bar-weight
1
, and therefore
Lemma

3.2
gives that
\lambda
has
k
-weight
1
.
This implies in particular that
\lambda
has a unique rim-hook of length
k
, which we denote
\delta
.
Then
R(\lambda,k)=\{\lambda\setminus\delta\}
, while
S(\alpha,k)=\{\alpha\!\setminus\!\pi\}
.

Let
a
be such that
\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{}=a\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{}
. Then
2.1
and
2.2
give

\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{}(\nu)=\frac{1}{d_{\alpha\!\setminus\!\pi}}\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{}(\nu\sqcup(k))=\frac{a}{d_{\alpha\!\setminus\!\pi}}\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{}(\nu\sqcup(k))=\frac{ac_{\lambda\setminus\delta}}{d_{\alpha\!\setminus\!\pi}}\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{}(\nu)

for any partition
\nu
of
n-k
into odd parts, and therefore

\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{}=\frac{ac_{\lambda\setminus\delta}}{d_{\alpha\!\setminus\!\pi}}\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{}.\qed

Finally we derive some consequences of the modular branching rules.
Recall, given
\lambda\in\mathscr{P}
,
\alpha\in\mathscr{D}
and
\epsilon\in\{0,1\}
, that
\lambda^{-\epsilon}
denotes the partition obtained by removing all the removable
\epsilon
-nodes from
\lambda
, and
\alpha^{-\epsilon}
denotes the strict partition obtained by removing all the
\epsilon
-spin-removable nodes from
\alpha
.

Proposition 3.4
.

Let
\lambda\in\mathscr{P}
and
\alpha\in\mathscr{D}
with
\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{}\propto\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{}
.
Let
\epsilon\in\left\{0,1\right\}
.
Then
\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{}\propto\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{}
.

Let
r
be the number of
\epsilon
-spin-removable nodes of
\alpha
.
2.6
shows that
r
is maximal such that
e_{\epsilon}^{(r)}\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{}\neq 0
, and so
r
is also maximal such that
e_{\epsilon}^{(r)}\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{}\neq 0
.

2.5
then shows that
r
is the number of removable
\epsilon
-nodes of
\lambda
, and that
e_{\epsilon}^{(r)}\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{}=\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{}
. At the same time,
2.6
shows that
e_{\epsilon}^{(r)}\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{}
is a non-zero multiple of
\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{}
. So

\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{}\propto e_{\epsilon}^{(r)}\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{}\propto e_{\epsilon}^{(r)}\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{}=\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{}.\qed

4
Proportional implies 4-stepped-and-semicongruent

Our aim in this section is to prove the following statement.
We say that a strict partition
\alpha
is
proportional
if there exists a partition
\lambda
such that
\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{}\propto\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{}
; the definition of
4
-stepped-and-semicongruent was given at the start of the Introduction.

Theorem 4.1
.

Suppose
\alpha\in\mathscr{D}
. If
\alpha
is proportional, then
\alpha
is
4
-stepped-and-semicongruent.

This is enough to prove the “only if” claim of
1.1
when given also the “if” direction (
5.1
).
Indeed, if
\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{}
is proportional to
\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{}
, then
4.1
says that
\alpha
is
4
-stepped-and-semicongruent, and we must furthermore deduce that
\lambda\in\{{\alpha}_{\circ},{\alpha}_{\circ}^{\prime}\}
;
having that
\alpha
is
4
-stepped-and-semicongruent,
5.1
will tell us that
\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{}
is proportional to
\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{}
; since the constants of proportionality are the same (by
Lemma

3.1
Item

2
), we find that
\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{}=\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{}
.
Then a theorem of Wildon
[
Wil08
, Theorem 1.1.1(ii)]
gives
\lambda\in\{{\alpha}_{\circ},{\alpha}_{\circ}^{\prime}\}
.

The remainder of this section comprises the proof of
4.1
. The proof is by induction on the size of
\alpha
: let
\alpha
be a proportional strict partition; suppose that all smaller proportional strict partitions are
4
-stepped-and-semicongruent; and our goal is to show that
\alpha
is
4
-stepped-and-semicongruent.

We achieve our goal principally by applying the inductive hypothesis to three strict partitions:
\alpha^{-0}
,
\alpha^{-1}
and
\alpha\!\setminus\!\pi
, where
\pi
denotes the unique largest bar of odd length in
\alpha
.
We also make use of requirements on
\mathrm{dbl}(\alpha)^{\mathrm{reg}}
(
Lemma

3.1
Item

1
) and the bar-weights of
\alpha
(
Lemma

3.2
).

The strict partitions
\alpha^{-0}
and
\alpha^{-1}
are proportional by
3.4
, and so whenever they are smaller than
\alpha
the inductive hypothesis applies. Meanwhile
\alpha\!\setminus\!\pi
is proportional whenever
\alpha
contains an odd part by
3.3
(whilst we quickly deal with the case of
\alpha
having all parts even in the first subsection below).

Throughout, let
\lambda
be a partition such that
\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{}
is proportional to
\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{}
.
We summarise below the assumptions just made.

Assumptions in force for the rest of
Section

4
:

\diamond

\alpha\in\mathscr{D}
is proportional;

\diamond

\pi
is the unique largest odd bar in
\alpha
;

\diamond

\lambda\in\mathscr{P}
is such that
\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{}
is proportional to
\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{}
;

\diamond

any proportional strict partition
\beta
with
|\beta|<|\alpha|
is
4
-stepped-and-semicongruent.

4.1
Case of all parts having same parity

First we consider the case where the parts of
\alpha
all have the same parity.

Lemma 4.2
.

Suppose
\alpha
consists of a single odd part.
Then
\alpha
is
4
-stepped-and-semicongruent (that is,
\alpha\in\left\{(1),(3)\right\}
).

Write
\alpha_{1}=2k+1
. Then
\alpha
has a
(2k+1)
-bar, so by
Lemma

3.2

\lambda
has a
(2k+1)
-rim-hook. But
\lambda
is a partition of
2k+1
, so
\lambda
must itself be a rim-hook. Meanwhile
\lambda^{\mathrm{reg}}=\mathrm{dbl}(\alpha)^{\mathrm{reg}}=(k+1,k)
by
Lemma

3.1
Item

1
, so the only possibility is
\lambda=(k+1,1^{k})
.

If
k\geqslant 1
then
\alpha
has also a
(2k-1)
-bar, so
\lambda
has a
(2k-1)
-rim-hook (by
Lemma

3.2
). But the second-largest rim-hook of
\lambda=(k+1,k)
is of length
k
, so we must have
k\geqslant 2k-1
. This gives
k\leqslant 1
, and so
\alpha_{1}=2k+1\leqslant 3
as required.
∎

Proposition 4.3
.

Suppose
\alpha
has all parts odd.
Then
\alpha
is
4
-stepped-and-semicongruent (that is, it is a
4
-bar-core).

The case
l(\alpha)=1
is
Lemma

4.2
, so suppose
l(\alpha)>1
. The unique largest odd bar
\pi
is the largest part of
\alpha
.
By
3.3
and the inductive hypothesis,
\alpha\!\setminus\!\pi
is
4
-stepped-and-semicongruent, and since it has all parts odd
\alpha\!\setminus\!\pi
is
4
-bar-core. We can write
\alpha_{1}=\alpha_{2}+2k
for some positive integer
k
; we are required to show
k=2
. Let
\epsilon
be the spin-residue of the node at the end of row
2
of
\alpha
(and hence the spin-residue of the nodes at the end of every row except possibly row
1
).

Suppose towards a contradiction
k\geqslant 3
.
Then
\alpha^{-\epsilon}=(\alpha_{2}+r,\alpha_{2}-2,\alpha_{3}-2,\ldots)
where
r\in\left\{2k,2k-2\right\}
.
Then
\alpha_{2}+r>4
but
\alpha_{2}+r-4\not\in\alpha^{-\epsilon}
, so
\alpha^{-\epsilon}
is not
4
-stepped, a contradiction.

Now suppose towards a contradiction
k=1
. Then the node at the end of row
1
of
\alpha
is removable and has spin-residue
\bar{\epsilon}
, giving
\alpha^{-\bar{\epsilon}}=(\alpha_{2}+1,\alpha_{2},\alpha_{3},\ldots)
. If
\alpha_{2}>3
, then
\alpha^{-\bar{\epsilon}}_{1}>4
but
\alpha^{-\bar{\epsilon}}-4\not\in\alpha^{-\bar{\epsilon}}
, contradicting the assumption that
\alpha^{-\bar{\epsilon}}
is
4
-stepped. If
\alpha_{2}\in\left\{1,3\right\}
, then
\alpha\in\left\{(3,1),(5,3)\right\}
and it is easily checked that these strict partitions are not proportional (for example by inspecting the character tables in
[
JK81
]
and
[
HH92
]
, or by identifying differences in odd weights and removable nodes between
\alpha
and candidate partitions).
∎

Proposition 4.4
.

Suppose
\alpha
has all parts even.
Then
\alpha
is
4
-stepped-and-semicongruent.

Trivially any strict partition with all parts even is
4
-semicongruent; it remains to verify the
4
-stepped property.

Let
i
be such that
\alpha_{i}>4
(hence
\alpha_{i}\geqslant 6
).
Let
\epsilon
be the spin-residue of the node at the end of row
i
of
\alpha
. Then
\alpha_{i}^{-\epsilon}=\alpha_{i}-1>4
and
\alpha^{-\epsilon}
is
4
-stepped-and-semicongruent by the inductive hypothesis. Thus
\alpha_{i}-5\in\alpha^{-\epsilon}
. But since
\alpha_{i}-5
is odd, it cannot be a part of
\alpha
, and likewise nor can
\alpha_{i}-3
; thus a part of size
\alpha_{i}-5
must have been obtained by removing a single
\epsilon
-spin-node from a part of size
\alpha_{i}-4\in\alpha
.
∎

Given the above two propositions, we assume for the remainder of this section that
\alpha
has both odd and even parts.
In particular, the unique largest odd bar
\pi
comprises the largest even part and the largest odd part, and by
3.3
the partition
\lambda
has a unique rim-hook
\delta
of length
\lvert\pi\rvert
, and
\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{}
is proportional to
\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{}
.
Then by the inductive hypothesis
\alpha\!\setminus\!\pi
is
4
-stepped-and-semicongruent.

Additional assumption in force for the rest of
Section

4
:

\diamond

\alpha
has both odd and even parts;

\diamond

\delta
is the unique rim-hook of length
\lvert\pi\rvert
in
\lambda
, satisfying
\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{}\propto\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{}
.

We deal with one additional configuration here.

Lemma 4.5
.

Suppose
1
is the only odd part of
\alpha
and that
2\not\in\alpha\!\setminus\!\pi
.
Then
\alpha
is
4
-stepped-and-semicongruent.

The hypotheses imply that
\alpha\!\setminus\!\pi
does not have
1
,
2
or
3
as a part.
Then since
\alpha\!\setminus\!\pi
is
4
-stepped-and-semicongruent, it is of the form
(4k,4k-4,\dots,4)
for some nonnegative integer
k
.
So
\alpha=(\alpha_{1},4k,4k-4,\ldots,4,1)
for some even integer
\alpha_{1}>4k
.
If
\alpha_{1}=4k+4
, or if
k=0
and
\alpha_{1}=4k+2
, then
\alpha
is
4
-stepped-and-semicongruent, as required.

Suppose towards a contradiction either
\alpha_{1}\geqslant 4k+6
or
k>0
and
\alpha_{1}=4k+2
.
In either case,
\alpha
has at least one
0
-spin-removable node, and
\alpha^{-0}=(\alpha^{-0}_{1},4k-1,4k-5,\ldots,3)
, where
\alpha^{-0}_{1}
is either equal to
\alpha_{1}
or to
\alpha_{1}-1
; in either case
\alpha^{-0}_{1}>4
but
\alpha^{-0}_{1}-4
is not a part of
\alpha^{-0}
.
Then
\alpha^{-0}
is not
4
-stepped, a contradiction.
∎

4.2
Initial bounds on differences between parts of
\alpha

We begin the proof for general
\alpha
by showing that consecutive parts of
\alpha
cannot be too far apart. Clearly the parts except the largest odd and even parts cannot differ from their neighbouring parts by more than
4
, since
\alpha\!\setminus\!\pi
is
4
-stepped.
But furthermore the largest odd and even parts cannot be too much larger than the others, or else we could remove nodes from these parts and find
\alpha^{-0}
or
\alpha^{-1}
to have significantly differing parts, and hence not be
4
-stepped.
This is made precise by the following lemma.

Lemma 4.6
.

Let
1\leqslant i\leqslant l(\alpha)
.

1.

\alpha_{i}-\alpha_{i+1}\leqslant 5
.

2.

\alpha_{i}-\alpha_{i+1}\leqslant 4
unless
\alpha_{1}\not\equiv\alpha_{2}\ (\operatorname{mod}\,2)
and either
i=1
, or
i=2
,
\alpha_{2}
is odd and
\alpha_{1}-\alpha_{2}=3
.

Let
\epsilon
be the spin-residue of the node
(i,\alpha_{i})
(the node at the end of row
i
).

1.

Suppose towards a contradiction that
\alpha_{i}-\alpha_{i+1}>5
.

First consider the case where
\alpha_{i}
is even.
Then the
\epsilon
-spin-node at the end of row
i
of
\alpha
is spin-removable, and thus
\alpha^{-\epsilon}_{i}=\alpha_{i}-1
. But then
\alpha^{-\epsilon}_{i}-4=\alpha_{i}-5>\alpha_{i+1}\geqslant\alpha^{-\epsilon}_{i+1}
, contradicting the assumption that
\alpha^{-\epsilon}
is
4
-stepped.

Now consider the case where
\alpha_{i}
is odd. If
\alpha_{i}-\alpha_{i+1}>6
, then the two
\epsilon
-spin-nodes at the end of row
i
of
\alpha
are spin-removable, and thus
\alpha^{-\epsilon}_{i}=\alpha_{i}-2
.
But then
\alpha^{-\epsilon}_{i}-4=\alpha_{i}-6>\alpha_{i+1}\geqslant\alpha^{-\epsilon}_{i+1}
, contradicting the assumption that
\alpha^{-\epsilon}
is
4
-stepped.

If
\alpha_{i}-\alpha_{i+1}=6
, then the node at the end of row
i+1
has residue
\bar{\epsilon}
distinct from that of row
i
, and
\alpha_{i}^{-\bar{\epsilon}}-\alpha_{i+1}^{-\bar{\epsilon}}\geqslant\alpha_{i}-\alpha_{i+1}>4
. Thus
\alpha^{-\bar{\epsilon}}
is not
4
-stepped, so to avoid a contradiction with the inductive hypothesis we have
\alpha^{-\bar{\epsilon}}=\alpha
(that is,
\alpha
has no
\bar{\epsilon}
-spin-removable nodes). In order for row
i+1
to have no
\bar{\epsilon}
-spin-removable nodes, we must have
\alpha_{i+1}\geqslant 3
and
\alpha_{i+1}-1,\alpha_{i+1}-2\in\alpha
. But then
\alpha_{i+1},\alpha_{i+1}-2\in\alpha\!\setminus\!\pi
(with
\alpha_{i+1}
odd), contradicting the assumption that
\alpha\!\setminus\!\pi
is
4
-semicongruent.

2.

Since
\alpha\!\setminus\!\pi
is
4
-stepped, we have
\alpha_{i}-\alpha_{i+1}\leqslant 4
for all
i
except possibly when
\alpha_{i}
is the largest part of its parity.
If
\alpha_{i}
is largest of its parity but
i\geqslant 3
, then
\alpha_{i-1}
is not largest of its parity,
and so
\alpha_{i-1}\in\alpha\!\setminus\!\pi
but
\alpha_{i}\not\in\alpha\!\setminus\!\pi
.
Then
\alpha_{i-1}-\alpha_{i+1}\leqslant 4
and hence
\alpha_{i}-\alpha_{i+1}\leqslant 4
.
So we may assume
i\in\left\{1,2\right\}
.

If
\alpha_{1}\equiv\alpha_{2}\ (\operatorname{mod}\,2)
, then by part (i) we have
\alpha_{1}-\alpha_{2}\leqslant 4
(resolving the case
i=1
), while
\alpha_{2}
is not the largest part of its parity (resolving the case
i=2
).
So we may assume
\alpha_{1}\not\equiv\alpha_{2}\ (\operatorname{mod}\,2)
.

Consider the case
i=2
,
\alpha_{1}
odd and
\alpha_{2}
even.
We have
\alpha_{2}-\alpha_{3}\leqslant 5
by part (i).
Suppose towards a contradiction that
\alpha_{3}=\alpha_{2}-5
.
We argue as in the final paragraph of part (i): the node at the end of row
3
has residue
\bar{\epsilon}
, and thus
\alpha_{2}^{-\bar{\epsilon}}-\alpha_{3}^{-\bar{\epsilon}}\geqslant\alpha_{2}-\alpha_{3}>4
;
therefore
\alpha^{-\bar{\epsilon}}
is not
4
-stepped, and so
\alpha^{-\bar{\epsilon}}=\alpha
; in order for row
3
to have no
\bar{\epsilon}
-spin-removable nodes, we have
\alpha_{3}\geqslant 3
and
\alpha_{3}-1,\alpha_{3}-2\in\alpha
; but then
\alpha_{3},\alpha_{3}-2\in\alpha\!\setminus\!\pi
, contradicting the assumption that
\alpha\!\setminus\!\pi
is
4
-semicongruent.
So
\alpha_{2}-\alpha_{3}\leqslant 4
in this case.

The only remaining case to consider is when
i=2
,
\alpha_{1}
even and
\alpha_{2}
odd, where we must show that if
\alpha_{2}-\alpha_{3}=5
then
\alpha_{1}-\alpha_{2}=3
.
Indeed, if
\alpha_{2}-\alpha_{3}=5
then the nodes at the ends of rows
2
and
3
both have spin-residue
\epsilon
, and so
\alpha_{2}^{-\bar{\epsilon}}-\alpha_{3}^{-\bar{\epsilon}}=\alpha_{2}-\alpha_{3}>4
.
Thus
\alpha^{-\bar{\epsilon}}
is not
4
-stepped, so to avoid a contradiction with the inductive hypothesis we have
\alpha^{-\bar{\epsilon}}=\alpha
(that is,
\alpha
has no
\bar{\epsilon}
-spin-removable nodes).
In particular, we cannot have
\alpha_{1}=\alpha_{2}+5
(for then row
1
would have a
\bar{\epsilon}
-spin-removable node).
So
\alpha_{1}\in\left\{\alpha_{2}+1,\alpha_{2}+3\right\}
.
But if
\alpha_{1}=\alpha_{2}+1
, then
\alpha^{-\epsilon}_{1}=\alpha_{1}=\alpha_{2}+1>4
, but
\alpha^{-\epsilon}_{2}=\alpha_{2}-2
and
\alpha^{-\epsilon}_{3}\leqslant\alpha_{3}=\alpha_{2}-5
, contradicting the assumption that
\alpha^{-\epsilon}
is
4
-stepped.
So
\alpha_{1}-\alpha_{2}=3
as required.
∎

4.3
Improving bounds by considering the last fully-occupied ladder

We now tighten the bounds from the previous section by considering restrictions on the
fully-occupied ladders
in
\mathrm{dbl}(\alpha)
imposed by the regularisation requirement (
Lemma

3.1
Item

1
).

Recall from
Section

2.1.3
that
\mathcal{L}_{l}
denotes the
l
th ladder in
\mathbb{N}^{2}
.
For a partition
\mu
, we say a ladder
\mathcal{L}_{l}
is
fully-occupied
in
\mu
if
\mathcal{L}_{l}\subseteq\mu
.
For a strict partition
\beta
, let
f(\beta)
be maximal such that
\mathcal{L}_{f(\beta)}
is fully-occupied in
\mathrm{dbl}(\beta)
, and set
f(\varnothing)=0
.
Trivially we find
f(\beta)\leqslant\mathrm{dbl}(\beta)_{1}=\lceil\beta_{1}/2\rceil
.
We will show that for
\alpha
and
\alpha\!\setminus\!\pi
, this bound is in fact attained.

Lemma 4.7
.

Suppose
\beta\in\mathscr{D}
, and let
1\leqslant i\leqslant l(\beta)-1
.

1.

Suppose either
\beta_{i}
is odd and
\beta_{i}-\beta_{i+1}\leqslant 4
, or
\beta_{i}
is even and
\beta_{i}-\beta_{i+1}\leqslant 5
.
Then the ladder that contains the node
(2i-1,\lceil\beta_{i}/2\rceil)
(that is, the final node in row
2i-1
of
\mathrm{dbl}(\beta)
) intersects
\mathrm{dbl}(\beta)
also in rows
2i
,
2i+1
and
2i+2
(ignoring the last if
\beta_{i+1}=1
).

2.

Suppose
\beta_{i}
is odd and
\beta_{i}-\beta_{i+1}\leqslant 6
.
Then the ladder that contains the node
(2i-1,\lfloor\beta_{i}/2\rfloor)
(that is, the penultimate node in row
2i-1
of
\mathrm{dbl}(\beta)
) intersects
\mathrm{dbl}(\beta)
also in rows
2i
,
2i+1
and
2i+2
(ignoring the last if
\beta_{i+1}=1
).

1.

The ladder that contains
(2i-1,\lceil\beta_{i}/2\rceil)
is
\mathcal{L}_{\lceil\beta_{i}/2\rceil+2i-2}
, so
\mathrm{dbl}(\beta)
meets this ladder in row
j>2i-1
if and only if
\mathrm{dbl}(\beta)_{j}\geqslant\lceil\beta_{i}/2\rceil+2i-1-j
.
Clearly

\displaystyle\mathrm{dbl}(\beta)_{2i}

\displaystyle=\lfloor\beta_{i}/2\rfloor\geqslant\lceil\beta_{i}/2\rceil-1,

while as illustrated in
Figures

1(a)
and
1(b)
the assumptions imply that

\displaystyle\mathrm{dbl}(\beta)_{2i+1}

\displaystyle=\lceil\beta_{i+1}/2\rceil\geqslant\lceil\beta_{i}/2\rceil-2,

\displaystyle\mathrm{dbl}(\beta)_{2i+2}

\displaystyle=\lfloor\beta_{i+1}/2\rfloor\geqslant\lceil\beta_{i}/2\rceil-3.

2.

The ladder that contains
(2i-1,\lfloor\beta_{i}/2\rfloor)
is
\mathcal{L}_{\lfloor\beta_{i}/2\rfloor+2i-2}
, so
\mathrm{dbl}(\beta)
meets this ladder in row
j>2i-1
if and only if
\mathrm{dbl}(\beta)_{j}\geqslant\lfloor\beta_{i}/2\rfloor+2i-1-j
.
Clearly

\displaystyle\mathrm{dbl}(\beta)_{2i}

\displaystyle=\lfloor\beta_{i}/2\rfloor\geqslant\lfloor\beta_{i}/2\rfloor-1,

while as illustrated in
Figure

1(c)
the assumptions imply that

\displaystyle\mathrm{dbl}(\beta)_{2i+1}

\displaystyle=\lceil\beta_{i+1}/2\rceil\geqslant\lfloor\beta_{i}/2\rfloor-2,

\displaystyle\mathrm{dbl}(\beta)_{2i+2}

\displaystyle=\lfloor\beta_{i+1}/2\rfloor\geqslant\lfloor\beta_{i}/2\rfloor-3.\qed

\vphantom{1}\smash{\scriptstyle\lceil\beta_{i}/2\rceil}
\vphantom{1}\smash{\scriptstyle\lfloor\beta_{i}/2\rfloor}
\vphantom{1}\smash{\scriptstyle\lceil\beta_{i+1}/2\rceil}
\vphantom{1}\smash{\scriptstyle\lfloor\beta_{i+1}/2\rfloor}
\mathcal{L}_{\lceil\beta_{i}/2\rceil+2i-1}

(a)

\beta_{i}
even,
\beta_{i}-\beta_{i+1}\leqslant 5

\vphantom{1}\smash{\scriptstyle\lceil\beta_{i}/2\rceil}
\vphantom{1}\smash{\scriptstyle\lfloor\beta_{i}/2\rfloor}
\vphantom{1}\smash{\scriptstyle\lceil\beta_{i+1}/2\rceil}
\vphantom{1}\smash{\scriptstyle\lfloor\beta_{i+1}/2\rfloor}
\mathcal{L}_{\lceil\beta_{i}/2\rceil+2i-1}

(b)

\beta_{i}
odd,
\beta_{i}-\beta_{i+1}\leqslant 4

\vphantom{1}\smash{\scriptstyle\lceil\beta_{i}/2\rceil}
\vphantom{1}\smash{\scriptstyle\lfloor\beta_{i}/2\rfloor}
\vphantom{1}\smash{\scriptstyle\lceil\beta_{i+1}/2\rceil}
\vphantom{1}\smash{\scriptstyle\lfloor\beta_{i+1}/2\rfloor}
\mathcal{L}_{\lfloor\beta_{i}/2\rfloor+2i-1}

(c)

\beta_{i}
odd,
\beta_{i}-\beta_{i+1}\leqslant 6

Figure 1
:
An illustration of the ladder considered in
Lemma

4.7
hitting the following three rows. The dashed boxes indicate nodes that may or may not be present in the Young diagram.

The next
Lemma
gives some basic results about
f(\alpha)
and
f(\alpha\!\setminus\!\pi)
.

Lemma 4.8
.

1.

f(\alpha\!\setminus\!\pi)=\mathrm{dbl}(\alpha\!\setminus\!\pi)_{1}=\left\lceil\frac{1}{2}(\alpha\!\setminus\!\pi)_{1}\right\rceil
.

2.

f(\alpha)\in\left\{\mathrm{dbl}(\alpha)_{1},\,\mathrm{dbl}(\alpha)_{1}-1\right\}
.

3.

If
\alpha_{1}
is even, then
f(\alpha)=\mathrm{dbl}(\alpha)_{1}=\frac{1}{2}\alpha_{1}
.

The strict partition
\alpha\!\setminus\!\pi
is
4
-stepped and hence satisfies the conditions of
Lemma

4.7
Item

1
for every
i
.
Thus the ladder
\mathcal{L}_{\mathrm{dbl}(\alpha\!\setminus\!\pi)_{1}}
intersects
\mathrm{dbl}(\alpha\!\setminus\!\pi)
in every row, and so
f(\alpha\!\setminus\!\pi)=\mathrm{dbl}(\alpha\!\setminus\!\pi)_{1}
as required.

By
Lemma

4.6
, the strict partition
\alpha
also satisfies the conditions of
Lemma

4.7
Item

1
, except possibly for a single value of
i\in\left\{1,2\right\}
with
\alpha_{i}
odd,
\alpha_{1}\not\equiv\alpha_{2}\ (\operatorname{mod}\,2)
, and
\alpha_{1}-\alpha_{2}=3
if
i=2
.

If the condition fails for
i=1
, then
\alpha_{1}
is odd and
\alpha_{1}-\alpha_{2}=5
, and so by
Lemma

4.7
Item

2
the ladder
\mathcal{L}_{\mathrm{dbl}(\alpha)_{1}-1}
intersects rows
2
,
3
and
4
of
\mathrm{dbl}(\alpha)
, and hence all rows of
\mathrm{dbl}(\alpha)
by
Lemma

4.7
Item

1
.

If the condition fails for
i=2
, then
\alpha_{1}
is even and
\alpha_{1}-\alpha_{2}=3
.
So by
Lemma

4.7
Item

1
, the ladder
\mathcal{L}_{\mathrm{dbl}(\alpha)}
intersects rows
2
,
3
and
4
of
\mathrm{dbl}(\alpha)
, and moreover since
\alpha_{1}-\alpha_{2}=3
it is easily seen that it intersects nodes in these rows strictly before the last.
Then by
Lemma

4.7
Item

2
, this ladder intersects also rows
5
and
6
of
\mathrm{dbl}(\alpha)
, and hence all rows of
\mathrm{dbl}(\alpha)
by
Lemma

4.7
Item

1
.

Thus in either case
f(\alpha)\geqslant\mathrm{dbl}(\alpha)_{1}-1
, and if
\alpha_{1}
is even then
f(\alpha)=\mathrm{dbl}(\alpha)_{1}
.
∎

The following is the key observation that, combined with the values of
f(\alpha)
and
f(\alpha\!\setminus\!\pi)
identified above, allows us to improve the bounds on differences between parts of
\alpha
.

Lemma 4.9
.

f(\alpha)\leqslant f(\alpha\!\setminus\!\pi)+2
.

By
Lemma

3.1
Item

1
, the partitions
\lambda
and
\mathrm{dbl}(\alpha)
have the same
2
-regularisation, so
f(\alpha)
is maximal such that
\mathcal{L}_{f(\alpha)}\subseteq\lambda
.
Similarly
f(\alpha\!\setminus\!\pi)
is maximal such that
\mathcal{L}_{f(\alpha\!\setminus\!\pi)}\subseteq\lambda\setminus\delta
.
Thus we can find a node
(r,c)\in\mathcal{L}_{f(\alpha\!\setminus\!\pi)+1}
which is not contained in
\lambda\setminus\delta
.
Then the node
(r+1,c+1)
cannot be contained in
\lambda
, because
\delta
contains at most one node on any diagonal, and hence cannot contain both
(r,c)
and
(r+1,c+1)
. The node
(r+1,c+1)
lies in
\mathcal{L}_{f(\alpha\!\setminus\!\pi)+3}
, and so we deduce
\mathcal{L}_{f(\alpha\!\setminus\!\pi)+3}\nsubseteq\lambda
, and therefore
f(\alpha)\leqslant f(\alpha\!\setminus\!\pi)+2
.
∎

We can now deduce tighter bounds on the differences between parts of
\alpha
.

Lemma 4.10
.

1.

If
\alpha_{1}
is even and
\alpha_{2}
is odd, then
\alpha_{1}-\alpha_{3}\leqslant 5
.

2.

If
\alpha_{1}
is odd and
\alpha_{2}
is even, then
\alpha_{1}-\alpha_{3}\leqslant 6
.

In both cases,
\alpha_{1}\not\equiv\alpha_{2}\ (\operatorname{mod}\,2)
and so
(\alpha\!\setminus\!\pi)_{1}=\alpha_{3}
.
Then combining
Lemma

4.8
1
and
Lemma

4.9
gives
f(\alpha)\leqslant\frac{1}{2}(\alpha_{3}+1)+2
.

1.

Suppose
\alpha_{1}
is even and
\alpha_{2}
is odd.
Then
Lemma

4.8
3
gives
f(\alpha)=\frac{1}{2}\alpha_{1}
.
Thus
\frac{1}{2}\alpha_{1}\leqslant\frac{1}{2}(\alpha_{3}+1)+2
, which gives the claim.

2.

Suppose
\alpha_{1}
is odd and
\alpha_{2}
is even.
Then
Lemma

4.8
2
gives
f(\alpha)\geqslant\mathrm{dbl}(\alpha)_{1}-1=\frac{1}{2}(\alpha_{1}-1)
.
Thus
\frac{1}{2}(\alpha_{1}-1)\leqslant\frac{1}{2}(\alpha_{3}+1)+2
, which gives the claim.
∎

Proposition 4.11
.

1.

Let
1\leqslant i\leqslant l(\alpha)
.
Then
\alpha_{i}-\alpha_{i+1}\leqslant 4
.

2.

f(\alpha)=\mathrm{dbl}(\alpha)_{1}=\left\lceil\frac{1}{2}\alpha_{1}\right\rceil
.

3.

If
\alpha_{1}
is odd and
\alpha_{2}
is even, then
\alpha_{1}-\alpha_{3}\leqslant 4
.

1.

The only cases not covered by
Lemma

4.6
are as follows.

\alpha_{1}
is even,
\alpha_{2}
is odd,
i\in\left\{1,2\right\}
:

By
Lemma

4.10
Item

1
we have
\alpha_{1}-\alpha_{3}\leqslant 5
.
Then
\alpha_{1}-\alpha_{2}\leqslant 4
and
\alpha_{2}-\alpha_{3}\leqslant 4
as required.

\alpha_{1}
is odd,
\alpha_{2}
is even,
i=1
:

By
Lemma

4.10
Item

2
we have
\alpha_{1}-\alpha_{3}\leqslant 6
.
Suppose towards a contradiction
\alpha_{1}-\alpha_{2}=5
; then
\alpha_{3}=\alpha_{1}-6
.
The nodes at the end of rows
1
and
2
have the same spin-residue, say
\epsilon
, and the node at the end of row
3
has distinct spin-residue
\bar{\epsilon}
.
If the node at the end of row
3
is spin-removable, then
\alpha^{-\bar{\epsilon}}
is not
4
-stepped, contradicting the inductive hypothesis.
So we must have
\alpha_{3}\geqslant 3
and
\alpha_{3}-1,\alpha_{3}-2\in\alpha
.
But then
\alpha_{3},\alpha_{3}-2\in\alpha\!\setminus\!\pi
, contradicting the assumption that
\alpha\!\setminus\!\pi
is
4
-semicongruent.

2.

By part
Item

1
, we now have that the conditions of
Lemma

4.7
Item

1
are satisfied for
\alpha
for every
i
, and so as in the proof of
Lemma

4.8
the ladder
\mathcal{L}_{\mathrm{dbl}(\alpha)_{1}}
intersects every row of
\mathrm{dbl}(\alpha)
.

3.

Suppose
\alpha_{1}
is odd and
\alpha_{2}
is even.
As in the proof of
Lemma

4.10
, combining
Lemma

4.8
1
and
Lemma

4.9
gives
f(\alpha)\leqslant\frac{1}{2}(\alpha_{3}+1)+2
.
Part
Item

2
gives
f(\alpha)=\mathrm{dbl}(\alpha)_{1}=\frac{1}{2}(\alpha_{1}+1)
.
Thus
\frac{1}{2}(\alpha_{1}+1)\leqslant\frac{1}{2}(\alpha_{3}+1)+2
, which gives the claim.
∎

4.4
Deducing 4-semicongruence by considering the last partially-occupied ladder

We also gain information on
\alpha
by considering the ladders which non-trivially intersect
\mathrm{dbl}(\alpha)
.

For a strict partition
\beta
, let
t(\beta)
be maximal such that
\mathrm{dbl}(\beta)\cap\mathcal{L}_{t(\beta)}
is nonempty, and set
t(\varnothing)=0
.
Trivially

t(\beta)\geqslant l(\mathrm{dbl}(\beta))=\begin{cases}2l(\beta)&\text{ if $1\not\in\beta$,}\\
2l(\beta)-1&\text{ if $1\in\beta$.}\end{cases}

We will show that for
\alpha
and
\alpha\!\setminus\!\pi
this bound is attained or nearly attained.

Lemma 4.12
.

Let
\beta
be any strict partition, and let
1\leqslant i\leqslant l(\beta)-1
. Suppose either
\beta_{i}
is even and
\beta_{i}-\beta_{i+1}\leqslant 4
, or
\beta_{i}
is odd and
\beta_{i}-\beta_{i+1}\leqslant 5
. Then the largest ladder that meets row
2i-1
or
2i
of
\mathrm{dbl}(\beta)
is smaller than or equal to the largest ladder that meets row
2i+1
or
2i+2
of
\mathrm{dbl}(\beta)
.

The largest ladder that meets row
2i-1
or
2i
is ladder
\mathcal{L}_{\lfloor\beta_{i}/2\rfloor+2i-1}
, while the largest ladder that meets rows
2i+1
or
2i+2
is ladder
\mathcal{L}_{\lfloor\beta_{i+1}/2\rfloor+2i+1}
.
The assumptions on
\beta_{i}
and
\beta_{i+1}
imply that
\lfloor\beta_{i}/2\rfloor\leqslant\lfloor\beta_{i+1}/2\rfloor+2
, which is what we need, as illustrated in
Figure

2
.
∎

\vphantom{1}\smash{\scriptstyle\lceil\beta_{i}/2\rceil}
\vphantom{1}\smash{\scriptstyle\lfloor\beta_{i}/2\rfloor}
\vphantom{1}\smash{\scriptstyle\lceil\beta_{i+1}/2\rceil}
\vphantom{1}\smash{\scriptstyle\lfloor\beta_{i+1}/2\rfloor}
\mathcal{L}_{\lfloor\beta_{i}/2\rfloor+2i-1}

(a)

\beta_{i}
even,
\beta_{i}-\beta_{i+1}\leqslant 4

\vphantom{1}\smash{\scriptstyle\lceil\beta_{i}/2\rceil}
\vphantom{1}\smash{\scriptstyle\lfloor\beta_{i}/2\rfloor}
\vphantom{1}\smash{\scriptstyle\lceil\beta_{i+1}/2\rceil}
\vphantom{1}\smash{\scriptstyle\lfloor\beta_{i+1}/2\rfloor}
\mathcal{L}_{\lfloor\beta_{i}/2\rfloor+2i-1}

(b)

\beta_{i}
odd,
\beta_{i}-\beta_{i+1}\leqslant 5
