Figure 2
:
An illustration of the largest ladder meeting row
2i-1
or
2i
, considered in
Lemma

4.12
, being met and possibly exceeded by row
2i+1
or
2i+2
.
The dashed boxes indicate nodes that may or may not be present in the Young diagram.

Proposition 4.13
.

Let
\beta\in\left\{\alpha,\alpha\!\setminus\!\pi\right\}
.
Then

t(\beta)=\begin{cases}l(\mathrm{dbl}(\beta))&\text{ if $1$, $2$ or $3$ is a part of $\beta$;}\\
l(\mathrm{dbl}(\beta))+1&\text{ otherwise.}\end{cases}

In particular, if
\alpha
has at least two odd parts, then
t(\alpha)=l(\mathrm{dbl}(\alpha))
.

Both
\alpha\!\setminus\!\pi
and
\alpha
satisfy the conditions of
Lemma

4.12
for every
i
:
\alpha\!\setminus\!\pi
does so since it is
4
-stepped, and
\alpha
does so by
4.11
Item

1
. Thus the largest ladder that meets
\beta
meets it in its final row. If the final part of
\beta
is
1
,
2
or
3
then this is the
l(\mathrm{dbl}(\beta))
ladder, while if the final part of
\beta
is
4
then this is the
l(\mathrm{dbl}(\beta))+1
ladder. The
4
-stepped property of
\alpha\!\setminus\!\pi
and the bounds on
\alpha
imply that the final part of
\beta
is at most
4
, so these are all possible cases.

The “in particular” follows since if
\alpha
has at least two odd parts then
\alpha\!\setminus\!\pi
has at least one odd part, and since it is
4
-stepped therefore has
1
or
3
as a part, and hence so does
\alpha
.
∎

Lemma 4.14
.

t(\alpha)\geqslant t(\alpha\!\setminus\!\pi)+3
.

It suffices to show
l(\mathrm{dbl}(\alpha))\geqslant t(\alpha\!\setminus\!\pi)+3
.
If
\alpha
has an odd part greater than
1
, then
l(\mathrm{dbl}(\alpha))={l(\mathrm{dbl}(\alpha\!\setminus\!\pi))+4}
, and

4.13
gives
l(\mathrm{dbl}(\alpha\!\setminus\!\pi))+1\geqslant t(\alpha\!\setminus\!\pi)
, yielding the result.
Otherwise, the only odd part of
\alpha
is
1
, and
l(\mathrm{dbl}(\alpha))=l(\mathrm{dbl}(\alpha\!\setminus\!\pi))+3
.
By
Lemma

4.5
, we may assume
2\in\alpha\!\setminus\!\pi
in this case.
Then
l(\mathrm{dbl}(\alpha\!\setminus\!\pi))=t(\alpha\!\setminus\!\pi)
by
4.13
, and the result follows.
∎

Now recall the rim-hook
\delta
. Because
|\delta|
is odd, we can write
|\delta|=2a+1
. The residues of the nodes in a rim-hook alternate between
0
and
1
from one end of the rim-hook to the other, so the multiset of residues of the nodes of
\delta
is either
\{0^{a+1},1^{a}\}
or
\{0^{a},1^{a+1}\}
.

Lemma 4.15
.

The rim-hook
\delta
meets
\mathcal{L}_{t(\alpha)}
in the first row or column.
In particular, the multiset of residues of
\delta
is
\{0^{a+1},1^{a}\}
if
t(\alpha)
is odd, or
\{0^{a},1^{a+1}\}
if
t(\alpha)
is even.

That
\delta
meets the ladder
\mathcal{L}_{t(\alpha)}
is clear from the fact that
t(\alpha)>t(\alpha\!\setminus\!\pi)
. Since furthermore
t(\alpha)\geqslant t(\alpha\!\setminus\!\pi)+3
by
Lemma

4.14
, we deduce that
\lambda\setminus\delta
does not contain any nodes in the ladder
\mathcal{L}_{t(\alpha)-2}
, and hence the only possible nodes of
\mathcal{L}_{t(\alpha)}
that can belong to
\delta
are
(1,t(\alpha))
and
(t(\alpha),1)
. So
\delta
meets
\mathcal{L}_{t(\alpha)}
in the first row or column.
∎

Proposition 4.16
.

The odd parts of
\alpha
are congruent modulo
4
(that is,
\alpha
is
4
-semicongruent).

If
\alpha
has only one odd part, this is trivially true. So suppose
\alpha
has at least two odd parts. Since
\alpha\!\setminus\!\pi
is
4
-semicongruent, all the odd parts of
\alpha
except possibly the largest are congruent modulo
4
, so it suffices to show that the largest odd part of
\alpha
is congruent to the smallest odd part of
\alpha
.
Write
2k+1
for the largest odd part of
\alpha
. Note that the smallest odd part of
\alpha
is the smallest odd part of
\alpha\!\setminus\!\pi
, and so (since
\alpha\!\setminus\!\pi
is
4
-stepped) is equal to
1
or
3
.

Recall that (spin-)contents (i.e. multisets of (spin-)residues) of partitions characterise blocks (
Section

2.5
). Since
\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{}
and
\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{}
are proportional, they lie in the same block, so the spin-content of
\alpha
is equal to the content of
\lambda
; likewise the spin-content of
\alpha\!\setminus\!\pi
is equal to the content of
\lambda\setminus\delta
. It follows that the multiset of spin-residues of
\pi
is equal to the multiset of residues of
\delta
. Write
2a+1
for the size of
\delta
.

Observe that an even part of
\alpha
has equal numbers of nodes of spin-residue
0
and
1
, a part congruent to
1\ (\operatorname{mod}\,4)
has one more node of spin-residue
0
than
1
, and a part congruent to
3\ (\operatorname{mod}\,4)
has one more node of spin-residue
1
than
0
.
Thus we have

\displaystyle 2k+1\equiv 1\ (\operatorname{mod}\,4)

\displaystyle\;\Longleftrightarrow\;\text{$\pi$ has multiset of spin-residue{}s $\{0^{a+1},1^{a}\}$}

(by the above observation)

\displaystyle\;\Longleftrightarrow\;\text{$\delta$ has multiset of residues $\{0^{a+1},1^{a}\}$}

(as contents characterise blocks)

\displaystyle\;\Longleftrightarrow\;\text{$t(\alpha)$ is odd}

(by
Lemma

4.15
)

\displaystyle\;\Longleftrightarrow\;\text{$l(\mathrm{dbl}(\alpha))$ is odd}

(by
4.13
)

\displaystyle\;\Longleftrightarrow\;\text{$1$ is the smallest odd part of $\alpha$}.

Therefore the largest odd part of
\alpha
is congruent modulo
4
to the smallest, as required.
∎

The congruence of the odd parts of
\alpha
also allows us to make the following useful deductions.

Corollary 4.17
.

1.

Suppose
1\leqslant i\leqslant l(\alpha)
. If
\alpha_{i}
is odd, then the node
(i,\alpha_{i})
at the end of row
i
is spin-removable.

2.

Let
\epsilon\in\left\{0,1\right\}
and let
m\geqslant 2
be even.
If
m\in\alpha^{-\epsilon}
, then
m\in\alpha
.

1.

The only way the node
(i,\alpha_{i})
can fail to be spin-removable is if
\alpha_{i}\geqslant 3
and both
\alpha_{i}-1
and
\alpha_{i}-2
are parts of
\alpha
. But this contradicts
4.16
.

2.

Let
j
be such that
\alpha^{-\epsilon}_{j}=m
.
If the node
(j,\alpha_{j})
at the end of row
j
in
\alpha
has spin-residue
\bar{\epsilon}
, then
\alpha_{j}=m
also.
Otherwise,
(j,\alpha_{j})
has spin-residue
\epsilon
, and either
\alpha_{j}=m
, in which case the claim holds, or
\alpha_{j}=m+1
.
In this remaining case, the penultimate node in row
j
, the node
(j,m)
, is not spin-removable, and so either
\alpha_{j+1}=m
or
\alpha_{j+1}=m-1
.
But if
m
is even then
m+1
and
m-1
cannot both be parts of
\alpha
by
4.16
, so
\alpha_{j+1}=m
.
∎

4.5
Ruling out configurations using hook lengths

Before we can complete the proof of
4.1
by showing that
\alpha
is
4
-stepped, we first rule out several tricky configurations by considering restrictions on hook lengths in
\lambda
.

Recall that by assumption
\lambda
has a unique rim-hook
\delta
of length
|\pi|
.

Lemma 4.18
.

If
\lvert\delta\rvert\geqslant t(\alpha)+f(\alpha\!\setminus\!\pi)+1
,
then
f(\alpha)=f(\alpha\!\setminus\!\pi)+2
.

Observe that if
\delta
meets all of the diagonals intersecting the ladders
\mathcal{L}_{f(\alpha\!\setminus\!\pi)+1}
and
\mathcal{L}_{f(\alpha\!\setminus\!\pi)+2}
, then these ladders are both fully-occupied in
\lambda
(using the fact that
\mathcal{L}_{f(\alpha\!\setminus\!\pi)}
is already fully-occupied in
\lambda\setminus\delta
). Thus if
\delta
meets these diagonals, then
f(\alpha)\leqslant f(\alpha\!\setminus\!\pi)+2
, which must then hold with equality by
Lemma

4.9
.

So suppose
\lvert\delta\rvert\geqslant t(\alpha)+f(\alpha\!\setminus\!\pi)+1
, and it suffices to show that
\delta
meets the diagonals intersecting the ladders
\mathcal{L}_{f(\alpha\!\setminus\!\pi)+1}
and
\mathcal{L}_{f(\alpha\!\setminus\!\pi)+2}
, which are the diagonals

-(f(\alpha\!\setminus\!\pi)+1),\ldots,-1,0,1,\ldots,f(\alpha\!\setminus\!\pi)+1.

We know from
Lemma

4.15
that
\delta
meets the ladder
\mathcal{L}_{t(\alpha)}
in either the first column or first row. Without loss of generality, suppose it is the first column; then the bottom-most node (the “foot node”) of
\delta
is
(t(\alpha),1)
, which lies on the
1-t(\alpha)
diagonal.
Furthermore, since a rim-hook meets each diagonal at most once,
\delta
meets all diagonals from
1-t(\alpha)
to
\lvert\delta\rvert-t(\alpha)
inclusive.
So provided
\lvert\delta\rvert\geqslant t(\alpha)+f(\alpha\!\setminus\!\pi)+1
as assumed (and also
t(\alpha)-1\geqslant f(\alpha\!\setminus\!\pi)+1
, which holds since
t(\alpha)\geqslant t(\alpha\!\setminus\!\pi)+3\geqslant f(\alpha\!\setminus\!\pi)+3
), the desired diagonals are met.
∎

Proposition 4.19
.

\alpha
is not of the form:

1.

(4k+2)\sqcup\gamma\sqcup(4k,4k-1,4k-4,4k-5,\ldots,4,3)
where
k\geqslant 1
and
\gamma
is a strict partition with all parts congruent to
2\ (\operatorname{mod}\,4)
and strictly less than
4k-2
; or

2.

(4k+4)\sqcup\gamma\sqcup(4k+2,4k+1,4k-2,4k-3,\ldots,2,1)
where
k\geqslant 1
and
\gamma
is a strict partition with all parts congruent to
0\ (\operatorname{mod}\,4)
and strictly less than
4k
.

In both cases we suppose that
\alpha
is of the given form and find a contradiction with
Lemma

4.18
.

1.

We compute (using
Lemmas

4.8
and
4.13
if desired)

\displaystyle t(\alpha)

\displaystyle=l(\mathrm{dbl}(\alpha))=2(2k+1+l(\gamma))\leqslant 6k,

\displaystyle f(\alpha)

\displaystyle=\mathrm{dbl}(\alpha)_{1}=2k+1,

\displaystyle f(\alpha\!\setminus\!\pi)

\displaystyle=\mathrm{dbl}(\alpha\!\setminus\!\pi)_{1}=2k,

\displaystyle\lvert\delta\rvert

\displaystyle=\lvert\pi\rvert=8k+1.

But then
t(\alpha)+f(\alpha\!\setminus\!\pi)+1\leqslant 8k+1=\lvert\delta\rvert
but
f(\alpha)\neq f(\alpha\!\setminus\!\pi)+2
, contradicting
Lemma

4.18
.

2.

We compute (using
Lemmas

4.8
and
4.13
if desired)

\displaystyle t(\alpha)

\displaystyle=l(\mathrm{dbl}(\alpha))-1=2(2k+3+l(\gamma))-1\leqslant 6k+3,

\displaystyle f(\alpha)

\displaystyle=\mathrm{dbl}(\alpha)_{1}=2k+2,

\displaystyle f(\alpha\!\setminus\!\pi)

\displaystyle=\mathrm{dbl}(\alpha\!\setminus\!\pi)_{1}=2k+1,

\displaystyle\lvert\delta\rvert

\displaystyle=\lvert\pi\rvert=8k+5.

But then
t(\alpha)+f(\alpha\!\setminus\!\pi)+1\leqslant 8k+5=\lvert\delta\rvert
but
f(\alpha)\neq f(\alpha\!\setminus\!\pi)+2
, contradicting
Lemma

4.18
. ∎

The following is in fact a statement about the longest odd hook length in any partition
\lambda
with given
2
-regularisation.

Lemma 4.20
.

In all cases,
\lvert\delta\rvert\leqslant 2t(\alpha)-1
.
Moreover:

1.

if
\mathrm{dbl}(\alpha)
contains only a single node in
\mathcal{L}_{t(\alpha)}
, then
\lvert\delta\rvert<2t(\alpha)-1
;

2.

if
\mathrm{dbl}(\alpha)
contains only a single node in
\mathcal{L}_{t(\alpha)}
and exactly two nodes in each of
\mathcal{L}_{t(\alpha)-1}
and
\mathcal{L}_{t(\alpha)-2}
, then
\lvert\delta\rvert<2t(\alpha)-3
.

The diagonals that intersect the first
t(\alpha)
ladders are the diagonals

-(t(\alpha)-1),\ldots,-1,0,1,\ldots,t(\alpha)-1.

Thus
\lambda
meets at most
2t(\alpha)-1
diagonals. Since
\delta
meets each diagonal at most once, we have
\lvert\delta\rvert\leqslant 2t(\alpha)-1
. In order for equality to be achieved,
\delta
must meet both the
-(t(\alpha)-1)
and
t(\alpha)-1
diagonals, but their intersection with the first
t(\alpha)
ladders is with only
\mathcal{L}_{t(\alpha)}
; thus
\mathcal{L}_{t(\alpha)}
must contains at least two nodes of
\lambda
, proving (i).

Now suppose
\mathrm{dbl}(\alpha)
is as described in (ii).
By part (i), we have
\lvert\delta\rvert<2t(\alpha)-1
; suppose towards a contradiction
\lambda
contains a rim-hook
\rho
of length
2t(\alpha)-3
(because
\lvert\delta\rvert
is necessarily odd, this will suffice).
Then
\rho
must meet both the diagonals
t(\alpha)-3
and
-(t(\alpha)-3)
, and hence
\lambda
must contain the nodes at start of each of these diagonals:
(1,t(\alpha)-2)
and
(t(\alpha)-2,1)
.
These nodes lie in the ladder
\mathcal{L}_{t(\alpha)-2}
, which by assumption has exactly two nodes in
\mathrm{dbl}(\alpha)
; thus the nodes
(1,t(\alpha)-2)
and
(t(\alpha)-2,1)
are the only nodes in
\lambda\cap\mathcal{L}_{t(\alpha)-1}
and furthermore are contained in
\rho
, and thus
\rho
is the largest rim-hook in
\lambda
.
It follows that the nodes in
\lambda
in the ladders
\mathcal{L}_{t(\alpha)-1}
and
\mathcal{L}_{t(\alpha)}
must also be at the start of their diagonals (that is, in the first row or the first column), and
\rho
has length
2t(\alpha)-2
, a contradiction.
∎

Proposition 4.21
.

\alpha
is not of the form:

1.

(4k,4k-4,\ldots,4)\sqcup(5)
where
k\geqslant 1
; or

2.

(4k,4k-4,\ldots,4)\sqcup(5,2)
where
k\geqslant 1
.

In both cases we suppose that
\alpha
is of the given form and find a contradiction with
Lemma

4.20
.
In both cases
\lvert\delta\rvert=\lvert\pi\rvert=4k+5
.

1.

Observe that
t(\alpha)=l(\mathrm{dbl}(\alpha))+1=2k+3
and that
\mathrm{dbl}(\alpha)
contains only a single node of
\mathcal{L}_{t(\alpha)}
. But also
\lvert\delta\rvert=2t(\alpha)-1
, contradicting
Lemma

4.20
Item

1
.

2.

Observe that
t(\alpha)=l(\mathrm{dbl}(\alpha))=2k+4
and that
\mathrm{dbl}(\alpha)
contains only a single node of
\mathcal{L}_{t(\alpha)}
. Furthermore
\mathcal{L}_{t(\alpha)-1}
and
\mathcal{L}_{t(\alpha)-2}
contain exactly two nodes of
\mathrm{dbl}(\alpha)
each. But also
\lvert\delta\rvert=2t(\alpha)-3
, contradicting
Lemma

4.20
Item

2
. ∎

4.6
4-stepped property

To complete our proof of
4.1
, we show that
\alpha_{i}-4\in\alpha
whenever
\alpha_{i}>4
.

Lemma 4.22
.

Let
\alpha_{i}
be the largest odd part of
\alpha
, and suppose
\alpha_{i}>4
and
\alpha_{i}-4\not\in\alpha
.
Then
\alpha_{i+1}=\alpha_{i}-1
, and if
\alpha_{i}-3\in\alpha
then
\alpha_{i}+1\not\in\alpha
.

Our bound on consecutive parts
\alpha_{i}-\alpha_{i+1}\leqslant 4
from
4.11
Item

1
, together with the assumption
\alpha_{i}-4\not\in\alpha
and that
\alpha_{i}-2\not\in\alpha
by
4.16
, gives
\alpha_{i+1}\in\left\{\alpha_{i}-1,\alpha_{i}-3\right\}
.

First suppose
i=1
.
Clearly
\alpha_{1}+1\not\in\alpha
.
Meanwhile if
\alpha_{1}-1\not\in\alpha
we have
\alpha_{2}=\alpha_{1}-3
;
then by
4.11
Item

3
we have
\alpha_{3}=\alpha_{1}-4
, contradicting the assumption on
\alpha
.

So suppose
i>1
, and let
\epsilon
be the spin-residue of the node at the end of row
i
in
\alpha
.
If
\alpha_{i}-3\not\in\alpha
, then
\alpha_{i+1}=\alpha_{i}-1
; it remains to suppose
\alpha_{i}-3\in\alpha
and show that
\alpha_{i}-1\in\alpha
and
\alpha_{i}+1\not\in\alpha
.
Observe that, since
\alpha_{i}-4\not\in\alpha
, there is a
\bar{\epsilon}
-spin-removable node in column
\alpha_{i}-3
(and so the inductive hypothesis applies to
\alpha^{-\bar{\epsilon}}
).
Since also
\alpha_{i}-2\not\in\alpha
,
we deduce that
\alpha_{i}-3\not\in\alpha^{-\bar{\epsilon}}
.
Now,
\alpha_{i-1}
is even and
\alpha_{i-1}-\alpha_{i}\leqslant 4
,
so
\alpha_{i-1}\in\left\{\alpha_{i}+1,\alpha_{i}+3\right\}
.
In either case, the node at the end of row
i-1
is not a
\bar{\epsilon}
-spin-removable node, and thus
\alpha_{i-1}^{-\bar{\epsilon}}=\alpha_{i-1}
.
So if
\alpha_{i-1}=\alpha_{i}+1
, then
\alpha_{i-1}^{-\bar{\epsilon}}=\alpha_{i}+1
but
\alpha_{i}-3\not\in\alpha^{-\bar{\epsilon}}
, contradicting the assumption that
\alpha^{-\bar{\epsilon}}
is
4
-stepped; thus
\alpha_{i}+1\not\in\alpha
, and
\alpha_{i-1}=\alpha_{i}+3
.
Then
\alpha^{-\bar{\epsilon}}_{i-1}=\alpha_{i}+3
and hence
\alpha_{i}-1\in\alpha^{-\bar{\epsilon}}
, and so
\alpha_{i}-1\in\alpha
by
4.17
Item

2
.
∎

Proposition 4.23
.

If
\alpha_{i}
is odd and
\alpha_{i}>4
, then
\alpha_{i}-4\in\alpha
.

Since
\alpha\!\setminus\!\pi
is
4
-stepped, the
Section
holds for all odd parts except possibly the largest. So suppose
\alpha_{i}
is the largest odd part of
\alpha
.
Suppose towards a contradiction
\alpha_{i}-4\not\in\alpha
.
By
Lemma

4.22
we have
\alpha_{i+1}=\alpha_{i}-1
.
Let
\epsilon
be the spin-residue of the nodes
(i,\alpha_{i})
and
(i+1,\alpha_{i}-1)
at the ends of rows
i
and
i+1
; note that these nodes are spin-removable, so
\alpha^{-\epsilon}
is
4
-stepped-and-semicongruent by the inductive hypothesis.

Consider first the case
\alpha_{i}\geqslant 7
.
We have
\alpha^{-\epsilon}_{i}=\alpha_{i}-1
and
\alpha^{-\epsilon}_{i+1}=\alpha_{i}-2
.
Then since
\alpha^{-\epsilon}
is
4
-stepped and
\alpha_{i}\geqslant 7
, we have
\alpha_{i}-5,\alpha_{i}-6\in\alpha^{-\epsilon}
.
So
\alpha
contains at least two of the integers
\alpha_{i}-4
,
\alpha_{i}-5
and
\alpha_{i}-6
.
But
\alpha_{i}-6\not\in\alpha
by
4.16
, so
\alpha_{i}-4\in\alpha
as required.

Now consider the case
\alpha_{i}=5
(and hence
\epsilon=0
).
Since any part congruent to
2
or
3
modulo
4
has final node of spin-residue
1
, the parts of
\alpha
congruent to
2
modulo
4
are precisely the same as the parts of
\alpha^{-0}
congruent to
2
modulo
4
.
Since
\alpha^{-0}
is
4
-stepped, these parts must therefore be
2,6,\dots,4r-2
for some
r\geqslant 0
.
But by
Lemma

4.22
we have
r\leqslant 1
.
Thus
\alpha
is of the form
\alpha=(4k,4k-4,\ldots,8,5,4)
or
\alpha=(4k,4k-4,\ldots,8,5,4,2)
for some
k\geqslant 1
, contradicting
4.21
.
∎

Proposition 4.24
.

If
\alpha_{i}
is even and
\alpha_{i}>4
, then
\alpha_{i}-4\in\alpha
.

Since
\alpha\!\setminus\!\pi
is
4
-stepped, the claim holds for all even parts except possibly the largest. So assume
\alpha_{i}
is the largest even part of
\alpha
. Let
\epsilon
be the spin-residue of the node
(i,\alpha_{i})
.

By
4.17
Item

2
, it suffices to show that either
\alpha^{-\epsilon}
or
\alpha^{-\bar{\epsilon}}
has a part equal to
\alpha_{i}-4
.
Since
\alpha_{i}\in\alpha^{-\bar{\epsilon}}
, it also suffices to show
\alpha
has a
\bar{\epsilon}
-spin-removable node, for then the inductive hypothesis implies that
\alpha^{-\bar{\epsilon}}
is
4
-stepped and hence
\alpha_{i}-4\in\alpha^{-\bar{\epsilon}}
.

If
\alpha
has any part congruent to
\alpha_{i}-1\ (\operatorname{mod}\,4)
, then
\alpha
has a
\bar{\epsilon}
-spin-removable node by
4.17
Item

1
, and we are done.
Meanwhile, if
\alpha
has any part
\alpha_{j}\equiv\alpha_{i}-2\ (\operatorname{mod}\,4)
with
\alpha_{j}-1\not\in\alpha
, then
\alpha
has a
\bar{\epsilon}
-spin-removable node, so again we are done.

Consider the case
i>1
.
Then
\alpha_{i-1}
is odd and
\alpha_{i-1}-\alpha_{i}\leqslant 4
(by
4.11
Item

1
), so
\alpha_{i-1}\in\left\{\alpha_{i}+1,\alpha_{i}+3\right\}
. If
\alpha_{i-1}=\alpha_{i}+3
then we can use the argument from the last paragraph, so assume
\alpha_{i-1}=\alpha_{i}+1
. Then by
4.17
Item

1

\alpha
has a
\epsilon
-spin-removable node in row
i-1
, so the inductive hypothesis implies that
\alpha^{-\epsilon}
is
4
-stepped.
Since both
\alpha_{i},\alpha_{i}+1\in\alpha
we have
\alpha_{i}\in\alpha^{-\epsilon}
, so
\alpha_{i}-4\in\alpha^{-\epsilon}
and we are done.

So we may assume
i=1
.

4.11
Item

1
gives
\alpha_{1}-\alpha_{2}\leqslant 4
.
Clearly if
\alpha_{2}=\alpha_{1}-4
the lemma holds, while if
\alpha_{2}=\alpha_{1}-1
we can use the argument from earlier in the proof. So assume
\alpha_{2}\in\left\{\alpha_{1}-2,\alpha_{1}-3\right\}
.

Suppose
\alpha_{2}=\alpha_{1}-3
.
Then
\alpha_{2}
is odd and, using
Lemma

4.10
1
, we have
\alpha_{3}\in\left\{\alpha_{1}-4,\alpha_{1}-5\right\}
.
But we cannot have
\alpha_{1}-3,\alpha_{1}-5\in\alpha
by
4.16
, so
\alpha_{3}=\alpha_{1}-4
and we are done.

We are left with the case
\alpha_{2}=\alpha_{1}-2
.
Then
\alpha_{1}-2\in\alpha\!\setminus\!\pi
, and hence
\alpha_{1}-2,\alpha_{1}-6,\ldots\in\alpha
. From the third paragraph of the proof, we can also assume
\alpha_{1}-3,\alpha_{1}-7,\ldots\in\alpha
.
Thus if the
Section
is not true, then
\alpha
is of the form

\displaystyle\alpha

\displaystyle=(4k+2,4k,4k-1,4k-4,4k-5,\ldots,4,3)\sqcup\gamma

where
k\geqslant 1
, and
\gamma
is a strict partition with all parts congruent to
2\ (\operatorname{mod}\,4)
and strictly less than
4k-2
, or

\displaystyle\alpha

\displaystyle=(4k+4,4k+2,4k+1,4k-2,4k-3,\ldots,2,1)\sqcup\gamma

where
k\geqslant 1
, and
\gamma
is a strict partition with all parts congruent to
0\ (\operatorname{mod}\,4)
and strictly less than
4k
.
But these are the cases ruled out by
4.19
.
∎

This completes the proof of
4.1
.

5
4-stepped-and-semicongruent implies proportional

We now turn towards proving the “if” direction of our main theorem, stated below.
We remind the reader that the construction of the partition
{\alpha}_{\circ}
was given in
Section

1.2
.

Theorem 5.1

((“If” direction of
1.1
))
.

Let
\alpha
be a
4
-stepped-and-semicongruent strict partition, and let
\lambda\in\{{\alpha}_{\circ},{\alpha}_{\circ}^{\prime}\}
.
Then
\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{}
is proportional to
\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{}
.

Our strategy is to first show the result for spin characters which are
homogeneous
, meaning that their composition factors in characteristic
2
are all isomorphic.
We then use (linear combinations of) induction and restriction functors to propagate the property of being proportional.
For this second step, in this section we only state the actions of our functions on relevant characters and show how they allow us to deduce
5.1
, deferring the proofs of these actions to the following sections.

5.1
Homogeneous case

Our proof starts with homogeneous characters.
We recall some notation from
Section

2
:

\kappa_{a}
is the
2
-core partition
(a,a-1,\ldots,1)
;
\bar{\kappa}_{a}
is the
4
-bar-core whose double is
\kappa_{a}
; and given two partitions
\sigma
and
\tau
, we write
[\kappa_{a};(\sigma,\tau)]
for the partition with
2
-core
\kappa_{a}
and
2
-quotient
(\sigma,\tau)
.

Proposition 5.2
.

Let
\alpha=\bar{\kappa}_{a}\sqcup 2\kappa_{r}
for some integers
a,r\geqslant 0
with
a\geqslant r-1
.
Then:

1.

\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{}
is homogeneous;

2.

\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{}
is irreducible;

3.

{\alpha}_{\circ}=\mathrm{dbl}(\alpha)^{\mathrm{reg}}
;

4.

\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{}\propto\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{}
.

1.

The homogeneous spin characters labelled by
separated
partitions were classified in
[
Fay20
, Theorem 5.11]
; in fact since the even parts of
\alpha
are twice a
2
-core, we need only use
[
Fay20
, Proposition 5.8]
to deduce that
\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{}
is homogeneous.

2.

The irreducible Specht modules were classified in
[
JM99
]
; in fact since
{\alpha}_{\circ}=[\kappa_{a};(\varnothing,\kappa_{r})]=\kappa_{a}+\kappa_{r}
is
2
-regular, we need only use
[
Jam78b
, Theorem 2.13]
to deduce that
\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{}
is irreducible.

3.

It is easily seen that
\mathrm{dbl}(\alpha)=\kappa_{a}\sqcup\kappa_{r}\sqcup\kappa_{r}
.
Observe that this partition is
2
-restricted (i.e. consecutive parts differ by at most
1
), and hence its regularisation is equal to its conjugate.
Note also for any partitions
\lambda,\mu
we have
(\lambda\sqcup\mu)^{\prime}=\lambda^{\prime}+\mu^{\prime}
.
Thus

\mathrm{dbl}(\alpha)^{\mathrm{reg}}=(\kappa_{a}\sqcup\kappa_{r}\sqcup\kappa_{r})^{\prime}=\kappa_{a}^{\prime}+\kappa_{r}^{\prime}+\kappa_{r}^{\prime}=\kappa_{a}+2\kappa_{r}={\alpha}_{\circ}.

4.

By
2.4
and part
Item

3
of the present
Section
,
\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{}
has least dominant composition factor given by
\varphi(\mathrm{dbl}(\alpha)^{\mathrm{reg}})=\varphi({\alpha}_{\circ})
.
Then since
\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{}
is homogeneous by
Item

1
, all its composition factors are isomorphic to
\varphi({\alpha}_{\circ})
.
Finally by
Item

2
we have
\varphi({\alpha}_{\circ})=\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{}
, giving the result. ∎

Remark 5.3
.

In fact, the spin characters
\mathopen{\hbox{${\langle}$}\kern-2.72221pt\hbox{${\langle}$}}\alpha\mathclose{\hbox{${\rangle}$}\kern-2.72221pt\hbox{${\rangle}$}}
appearing in the above
Section
are the only homogeneous spin characters proportional to a linear character in characteristic
2
, with the exception of
\alpha=(4)
(which satisfies
\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{}\propto\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{}=\varphi(3,1)
).
Indeed, suppose
\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{}
is homogeneous and proportional to
\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{}
.
Then
\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{}
is also homogeneous, and hence
\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{}=\varphi(\lambda^{\mathrm{reg}})
is irreducible (since we know
\varphi(\lambda^{\mathrm{reg}})
occurs with multiplicity
1
).
James and Mathas’s classification of irreducible linear characters
[
JM99
]
says that
\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{}
is irreducible if and only if
\lambda
or
\lambda^{\prime}
is
2
-Carter
(a property which implies
2
-regular) or
\lambda=(2,2)
.

On the other hand, our main theorem gives
\lambda\in\left\{{\alpha}_{\circ},{\alpha}_{\circ}^{\prime}\right\}
and hence both components of the
2
-quotient of
\lambda
are
2
-cores.
If both of these
2
-cores are nonempty, then in an abacus display for
\lambda
or
\lambda^{\prime}
there are beads in adjacent positions, and so
\lambda
and
\lambda^{\prime}
are both
2
-singular (that is, having repeated parts) and hence not
2
-Carter.
Furthermore if the nonempty component of the
2
-quotient has length greater than the length of the
2
-core by
2
or more, then again an abacus display for
\lambda
or
\lambda^{\prime}
has beads in adjacent positions.
Thus the only possibility (up to conjugacy) is

\lambda=[\kappa_{a};(\varnothing,\kappa_{r})]
for some
a\geqslant r-1
(which is precisely the situation of the lemma) or
\lambda=(2,2)
(in which case
\alpha=(4)
).

5.2
Application of runner-swapping and quotient-redistributing functions

In order to prove
5.1
from
5.2
, we apply certain combinations of the induction and restriction functors
e_{i},f_{i}
.
They are both functions on the Grothendieck group, or equivalently on generalised characters.

Definition 5.4
.

For
c\in\mathbb{Z}
and
\epsilon\in\left\{0,1\right\}
, define the
runner-swapping function
to be

S_{\epsilon}^{(c)}=\sum_{a\geqslant\max\{0,-c\}}(-1)^{a+c}f_{\epsilon}^{(a+c)}e_{\epsilon}^{(a)}.

Definition 5.5
.

For
d\in\mathbb{Z}
and
\epsilon\in\{0,1\}
, define the
quotient-redistributing function
to be

R_{\epsilon}^{(d)}=\sum_{a\geqslant\max\{0,-d\}}(-1)^{a+d}f^{(a+d)}_{\epsilon}f^{(a+d)}_{\bar{\epsilon}}e^{(a)}_{\bar{\epsilon}}e^{(a)}_{\epsilon}.

The actions of these functions on certain partitions of interest are given by the next four propositions, whose proofs are deferred to subsequent sections.

We begin with the action of the runner-swapping function. The following proposition is proved in a much more general form in
Section

6.1
.

Proposition 5.6

((Action of runner-swapping function on certain characters))
.

Let
r,s\geqslant 0
and let
a\geqslant 1
. Let
\epsilon
be the residue of
a+1
modulo
2
.
Then

S_{\epsilon}^{(-a)}\chi([\kappa_{a};(\kappa_{r},\kappa_{s})])=\pm\chi([\kappa_{a-1};(\kappa_{r},\kappa_{s})]).

Example 5.7
.

Take
r=1
,
s=2
,
a=2
and hence
\epsilon=1
.
Then
[\kappa_{2};(\kappa_{1},\kappa_{2})]
is the partition
\lambda=(6,3,1^{2})
.
The maximum
d
for which
e_{1}^{(d)}\chi(\lambda)\neq 0
is
3
, so that

\displaystyle S_{1}^{(-2)}\chi(\lambda)

\displaystyle=e_{1}^{(2)}\chi(\lambda)-f_{1}e_{1}^{(3)}\chi(\lambda)

\displaystyle=\chi(6,2,1)+\chi(5,3,1)+\chi(5,2,1^{2})-f_{1}\chi(5,2,1)

\displaystyle=-\chi(5,2^{2})

and indeed
(5,2^{2})=[\kappa_{1};(\kappa_{1},\kappa_{2})]
.
This is illustrated with Young diagrams and abacus displays below; the removed and added nodes are coloured.

We have a corresponding statement for spin characters, proved in
Section

6.2
.

Proposition 5.8

((Action of runner-swapping function on certain spin characters))
.

Let
r,s\geqslant 0
and let
a\geqslant 1
. Let
\epsilon
be the residue of
a+1
modulo
2
.
Then

S_{\epsilon}^{(-a)}\mathopen{\hbox{${\langle}$}\kern-2.72221pt\hbox{${\langle}$}}\bar{\kappa}_{a}\sqcup 2(\kappa_{r}+\kappa_{s})\mathclose{\hbox{${\rangle}$}\kern-2.72221pt\hbox{${\rangle}$}}=\pm\mathopen{\hbox{${\langle}$}\kern-2.72221pt\hbox{${\langle}$}}\bar{\kappa}_{a-1}\sqcup 2(\kappa_{r}+\kappa_{s})\mathclose{\hbox{${\rangle}$}\kern-2.72221pt\hbox{${\rangle}$}}.

Example 5.9
.

As in
5.7
, we take
r=1
,
s=2
,
a=2
and hence
\epsilon=1
.
Now
\bar{\kappa}_{2}=(3)
, so that
\bar{\kappa}_{a}\sqcup 2(\kappa_{r}+\kappa_{s})
is the partition
\alpha=(6,3,2)
.
Now we can calculate

\displaystyle S_{1}^{(-2)}\mathopen{\hbox{${\langle}$}\kern-2.72221pt\hbox{${\langle}$}}\alpha\mathclose{\hbox{${\rangle}$}\kern-2.72221pt\hbox{${\rangle}$}}

\displaystyle=e_{1}^{(2)}\chi(\alpha)-f_{1}e_{1}^{(3)}\chi(\alpha)

\displaystyle=2\mathopen{\hbox{${\langle}$}\kern-2.72221pt\hbox{${\langle}$}}5,3,1\mathclose{\hbox{${\rangle}$}\kern-2.72221pt\hbox{${\rangle}$}}+\mathopen{\hbox{${\langle}$}\kern-2.72221pt\hbox{${\langle}$}}6,2,1\mathclose{\hbox{${\rangle}$}\kern-2.72221pt\hbox{${\rangle}$}}-\sqrt{2}f_{1}\mathopen{\hbox{${\langle}$}\kern-2.72221pt\hbox{${\langle}$}}5,2,1\mathclose{\hbox{${\rangle}$}\kern-2.72221pt\hbox{${\rangle}$}}

\displaystyle=2\mathopen{\hbox{${\langle}$}\kern-2.72221pt\hbox{${\langle}$}}5,3,1\mathclose{\hbox{${\rangle}$}\kern-2.72221pt\hbox{${\rangle}$}}+\mathopen{\hbox{${\langle}$}\kern-2.72221pt\hbox{${\langle}$}}6,2,1\mathclose{\hbox{${\rangle}$}\kern-2.72221pt\hbox{${\rangle}$}}-(2\mathopen{\hbox{${\langle}$}\kern-2.72221pt\hbox{${\langle}$}}6,2,1\mathclose{\hbox{${\rangle}$}\kern-2.72221pt\hbox{${\rangle}$}}+2\mathopen{\hbox{${\langle}$}\kern-2.72221pt\hbox{${\langle}$}}5,3,1\mathclose{\hbox{${\rangle}$}\kern-2.72221pt\hbox{${\rangle}$}})

\displaystyle=-\mathopen{\hbox{${\langle}$}\kern-2.72221pt\hbox{${\langle}$}}6,2,1\mathclose{\hbox{${\rangle}$}\kern-2.72221pt\hbox{${\rangle}$}}

and indeed
(6,2,1)=\bar{\kappa}_{1}\sqcup 2(\kappa_{1}+\kappa_{2})
.
This is illustrated with Young diagrams below; the removed nodes are coloured.

\lxSVG@closescope }}}
}
\lxSVG@closescope {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}

For the action of the quotient-redistributing function, we restrict attention to (spin) characters in RoCK blocks.
Recall that the block with
2
-core
\kappa_{a}=(a,a-1,\ldots,1)
and weight
w
is
RoCK
if
a\geqslant w-1
.
The following is proved in
Section

7.1
.

Proposition 5.10

((Action of quotient-redistributing function on certain RoCK characters))
.

Let
s>r\geqslant 0
and let
a\geqslant\frac{1}{2}(r(r+1)+s(s+1))-1
. Let
\epsilon
be the residue of
a+1
modulo
2
.
Then

\displaystyle R_{\epsilon}^{(-(s-r-1))}\chi([\kappa_{a};(\kappa_{r},\kappa_{s})])

\displaystyle=\pm\chi([\kappa_{a};(\kappa_{r+1},\kappa_{s-1})]).

Example 5.11
.

Take
r=0
,
s=2
and
a=2
, and hence
\epsilon=1
.
Then
[\kappa_{2};(\kappa_{0},\kappa_{2})]
is the partition
(6,3)
.
We calculate

\displaystyle R_{\epsilon}^{(-1)}\chi(6,3)

\displaystyle=e_{0}^{(1)}e_{1}^{(1)}\chi(6,3)-f_{1}^{(1)}f_{0}^{(1)}e_{0}^{(2)}e_{1}^{(2)}\chi(6,3)

\displaystyle=\chi(6,1)+\chi(4,3)-f_{1}^{(1)}f_{0}^{(1)}\chi(4,1)

\displaystyle=-\chi(4,1^{3})

and indeed
(4,1^{3})=[\kappa_{2};(\kappa_{1},\kappa_{1})]
.
This is illustrated with Young diagrams and abacus displays below; the removed and added nodes are coloured.

The corresponding statement for spin characters below is proved in
Section

7.2
.

Proposition 5.12

((Action of quotient-redistributing function on certain RoCK spin characters))
.

Let
s>r\geqslant 0
and let
a\geqslant\frac{1}{2}(r(r+1)+s(s+1))-1
. Let
\epsilon
be the residue of
a+1
modulo
2
.
Then

\displaystyle R_{\epsilon}^{(-(s-r-1))}\mathopen{\hbox{${\langle}$}\kern-2.72221pt\hbox{${\langle}$}}\bar{\kappa}_{a}\sqcup 2(\kappa_{r}+\kappa_{s})\mathclose{\hbox{${\rangle}$}\kern-2.72221pt\hbox{${\rangle}$}}

\displaystyle=\pm\sqrt{2}\mathopen{\hbox{${\langle}$}\kern-2.72221pt\hbox{${\langle}$}}\bar{\kappa}_{a}\sqcup 2(\kappa_{r+1}+\kappa_{s-1})\mathclose{\hbox{${\rangle}$}\kern-2.72221pt\hbox{${\rangle}$}}.

Example 5.13
.

As in
5.11
, we take
r=0
,
s=2
,
a=2
and hence
\epsilon=1
.
Now
\bar{\kappa}_{2}\sqcup 2(\kappa_{0}+\kappa_{2})
is the strict partition
(4,3,2)
.
We calculate

\displaystyle R_{1}^{(-1)}\mathopen{\hbox{${\langle}$}\kern-2.72221pt\hbox{${\langle}$}}4,3,2\mathclose{\hbox{${\rangle}$}\kern-2.72221pt\hbox{${\rangle}$}}

\displaystyle=e_{0}^{(1)}e_{1}^{(1)}\mathopen{\hbox{${\langle}$}\kern-2.72221pt\hbox{${\langle}$}}4,3,2\mathclose{\hbox{${\rangle}$}\kern-2.72221pt\hbox{${\rangle}$}}-f_{1}^{(1)}f_{0}^{(1)}e_{0}^{(2)}e_{1}^{(2)}\chi(4,3,2)

\displaystyle=\sqrt{2}\mathopen{\hbox{${\langle}$}\kern-2.72221pt\hbox{${\langle}$}}4,3\mathclose{\hbox{${\rangle}$}\kern-2.72221pt\hbox{${\rangle}$}}-\sqrt{2}f_{1}^{(1)}f_{0}^{(1)}\mathopen{\hbox{${\langle}$}\kern-2.72221pt\hbox{${\langle}$}}3,2\mathclose{\hbox{${\rangle}$}\kern-2.72221pt\hbox{${\rangle}$}}

\displaystyle=\sqrt{2}\mathopen{\hbox{${\langle}$}\kern-2.72221pt\hbox{${\langle}$}}4,3\mathclose{\hbox{${\rangle}$}\kern-2.72221pt\hbox{${\rangle}$}}-2\sqrt{2}\mathopen{\hbox{${\langle}$}\kern-2.72221pt\hbox{${\langle}$}}4,3\mathclose{\hbox{${\rangle}$}\kern-2.72221pt\hbox{${\rangle}$}}

\displaystyle=-\sqrt{2}\mathopen{\hbox{${\langle}$}\kern-2.72221pt\hbox{${\langle}$}}4,3\mathclose{\hbox{${\rangle}$}\kern-2.72221pt\hbox{${\rangle}$}}

and indeed
(4,3)=\bar{\kappa}_{2}\sqcup 2(\kappa_{1}+\kappa_{1})
.
This is illustrated with Young diagrams below; the removed nodes are coloured.

These allow us to complete the goal of this section, restated below.

See
5.1

By assumption there exist non-negative integers
a,r,s
with
s\geqslant r
such that
\alpha=\bar{\kappa}_{a}\sqcup 2(\kappa_{r}+\kappa_{s})
and
\lambda=[\kappa_{a};(\kappa_{r},\kappa_{s})]
.
Choose
b\in\mathbb{N}
such that
b\geqslant a
and
b\geqslant|\kappa_{r+s}|-1
.
Applying
5.2
with
\alpha=\bar{\kappa}_{b}\sqcup 2\kappa_{r+s}
, we obtain

\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{}\propto\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{}.

Applying quotient-redistributing functions to each side
r
times and using
5.10
and
5.12
iteratively – the choice of
b
having been made to satisfy the hypotheses of these
Section
s – we obtain

\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{}\propto\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{}.

Then applying runner-swapping functions to each side
b-a
times and using
5.6
and
5.8
iteratively yields

\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{}\propto\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{}

as required.
∎

Remark 5.14
.

For characters in RoCK blocks, a possible alternative to our application of quotient-redistributing functions is to use the first author’s explicit formulæ for the decomposition numbers of RoCK blocks in terms of symmetric functions
[
Fay20
, Theorem 5.3]
.
Using these formulæ, it can be shown that
5.1
for RoCK blocks is equivalent to the following statement about symmetric functions: if
\sigma
and
\tau
are
2
-cores, then

P_{\sigma+\tau}=s_{\sigma}s_{\tau},

where
P_{\alpha}
denotes the Schur P-function labelled by a strict partition
\alpha
, and
s_{\lambda}
is the Schur function corresponding to a partition
\lambda
(see Macdonald’s book
[
Mac95
]
for basic definitions regarding symmetric functions).
We have not been able to find this result in the literature; our main theorem yields a proof of this identity.

6
Runner-swapping functions

The purpose of this section is to describe the action of the runner-swapping functions
S_{\epsilon}^{(c)}
on certain characters and spin characters. In fact we will establish the action in much more generality than that required in
Section

5.2
: we identify, for all characters and a large class of spin characters, a choice of parameters for which the image is (up to sign) a specified single (spin) character.

We recall the definition of the function from
Section

5
.

See
5.4

6.1
Action on characters

First we consider the effect of
S_{\epsilon}^{(c)}
on characters
\chi(\lambda)
. We prove the following result (recall for
r\geqslant 0
we write
\kappa_{r}
for the
2
-core partition
(r,r-1,\dots,1)
, and given a bipartition
\underline{\smash{\lambda}}
, we write
[\kappa_{r};\underline{\smash{\lambda}}]
for the partition with
2
-core
\kappa_{r}
and
2
-quotient
\underline{\smash{\lambda}}
).

Theorem 6.1
.

Let
\lambda=[\kappa_{a};\underline{\smash{\lambda}}]
be any partition.

1.

Let
\epsilon
be the residue of
a
modulo
2
. Then

\displaystyle S_{\epsilon}^{(a+1)}\chi([\kappa_{a};\underline{\smash{\lambda}}])=\pm\chi([\kappa_{a+1};\underline{\smash{\lambda}}]).

2.

Suppose
a\geqslant 1
, and let
\epsilon
be the residue of
a+1
modulo
2
. Then

\displaystyle S_{\epsilon}^{(-a)}\chi([\kappa_{a};\underline{\smash{\lambda}}])=\pm\chi([\kappa_{a-1};\underline{\smash{\lambda}}]).

The special case of
6.1
Item

2
in which
\underline{\smash{\lambda}}=(\kappa_{r},\kappa_{s})
gives
5.6
required in the proof of our main theorem.

To prepare for the proof, we introduce some notation. Recall that
\mathcal{D}_{d}
is the
d
th diagonal in
\mathbb{N}^{2}
. For a partition
\lambda
and
d\in\mathbb{Z}
, we write

\iota_{d}(\lambda)=\begin{cases}\phantom{-}1&\text{if $\lambda$ has an addable node in $\mathcal{D}_{d}$;}\\
-1&\text{if $\lambda$ has a removable node in $\mathcal{D}_{d}$;}\\
\phantom{-}0&\text{otherwise}.\end{cases}

If
\mu
is another partition, we write
\mu-_{d}\lambda
for the number of nodes of
\mu
in
\mathcal{D}_{d}
minus the number of nodes of
\lambda
in
\mathcal{D}_{d}
.

Lemma 6.2
.

Let
\lambda
be a partition,
c\in\mathbb{Z}
, and
\epsilon\in\{0,1\}
.
If
\mu
is a partition such that
\chi(\mu)
appears with non-zero coefficient in
S_{\epsilon}^{(c)}\chi(\lambda)
, then
\mu-_{d}\lambda\leqslant\iota_{d}(\lambda)
for all
d\in\mathbb{Z}
with
d\equiv\epsilon\ (\operatorname{mod}\,2)
.

It is clear that if
\chi(\mu)
appears with non-zero coefficient in
S_{\epsilon}^{(c)}\chi(\lambda)
then
\mu
is obtained from
\lambda
by removing and then adding
\epsilon
-nodes.
In particular,
\lambda
and
\mu
agree on
\mathcal{D}_{d}
for every
d\not\equiv\epsilon\ (\operatorname{mod}\,2)
.
This implies that
-1\leqslant\mu-_{d}\lambda\leqslant 1
, and
\mu-_{d}\lambda=0
if
\iota_{d}(\lambda)=0
.
This automatically gives the desired result for all
d
for which
\iota_{d}(\lambda)\geqslant 0
.
So we take
d
with
\iota_{d}(\lambda)=-1
, and assume for a contradiction that
\mu-_{d}\lambda=0
; that is, the removable node of
\lambda
in
\mathcal{D}_{d}
is also present in
\mu
.

Let
\Lambda
be the set of partitions
\nu
such that
\lambda
and
\mu
can each be obtained from
\nu
by adding
\epsilon
-nodes.
Then
\Lambda
is partitioned into pairs
\{\nu^{0},\nu^{1}\}
, where the removable node of
\lambda
in
\mathcal{D}_{d}
is also a removable node of
\nu^{0}
, and
\nu^{1}
is obtained from
\nu^{0}
by removing this node.
Each such pair satisfies
|\nu^{1}|=|\nu^{0}|-1
, so the contributions to
\left(S_{\epsilon}^{(c)}\chi(\lambda):\chi(\mu)\right)
from
\nu^{0}
and
\nu^{1}
cancel out. Summing over all such pairs, we find that
\left(S_{\epsilon}^{(c)}\chi(\lambda):\chi(\mu)\right)=0
, contrary to assumption.
∎

Given a partition
\lambda
, let
\lambda^{\ast\epsilon}
be the partition obtained from
\lambda
by simultaneously adding all the addable
\epsilon
-nodes and removing all the removable
\epsilon
-nodes.
We can equivalently characterise
\lambda^{\ast\epsilon}
as the unique partition such that:

\diamond

\lambda^{\ast\epsilon}
is obtained from
\lambda
by adding and/or removing
\epsilon
-nodes, and

\diamond

\lambda^{\ast\epsilon}-_{d}\lambda=\iota_{d}(\lambda)
for every
d\equiv\epsilon\ (\operatorname{mod}\,2)
.

Let
\Delta_{\epsilon}\lambda
be the number of addable
\epsilon
-nodes of
\lambda
minus the number of removable
\epsilon
-nodes (that is,
\Delta_{\epsilon}\lambda=\sum_{d\equiv\epsilon\ (\operatorname{mod}\,2)}\iota_{d}(\lambda)=|\lambda^{\ast\epsilon}|-|\lambda|
).

Proposition 6.3
.

Let
\lambda
be a partition and
\epsilon\in\{0,1\}
.
Then
S_{\epsilon}^{(\Delta_{\epsilon}\lambda)}\chi(\lambda)=\pm\chi(\lambda^{\ast\epsilon})
.

Suppose
\chi(\mu)
appears with non-zero coefficient in
S_{\epsilon}^{(\Delta_{\epsilon}\lambda)}\chi(\lambda)
.
In particular,
\mu
can be obtained from
\lambda
by removing then adding
\epsilon
-nodes, with a net gain of
\Delta_{\epsilon}\lambda
-many nodes, and so
|\mu|-|\lambda|=\Delta_{\epsilon}\lambda
.
By definition of
\Delta_{\epsilon}\lambda
, we therefore have

\displaystyle|\mu|-|\lambda|

\displaystyle=\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}d\equiv\epsilon\\
\scriptscriptstyle{(\operatorname{mod}\,2)}\end{subarray}}\iota_{d}(\lambda).

Meanwhile, by considering the net number of nodes added to each diagonal individually, we have

\displaystyle|\mu|-|\lambda|

\displaystyle=\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}d\equiv\epsilon\\
\scriptscriptstyle{(\operatorname{mod}\,2)}\end{subarray}}\mu-_{d}\lambda.

Equating these two expressions for
|\mu|-|\lambda|
gives

\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}d\equiv\epsilon\\
\scriptscriptstyle{(\operatorname{mod}\,2)}\end{subarray}}\mu-_{d}\lambda\ \ =\ \sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}d\equiv\epsilon\\
\scriptscriptstyle{(\operatorname{mod}\,2)}\end{subarray}}\iota_{d}(\lambda).

(
\dagger
)

But by
Lemma

6.2
, we have
{\mu-_{d}\lambda}\leqslant\iota_{d}(\lambda)
for every
d\equiv\epsilon\ (\operatorname{mod}\,2)
.
Thus for (

†

‣
6.1
) to hold, the equality
{\mu-_{d}\lambda}=\iota_{d}(\lambda)
must hold for every
d\equiv\epsilon\ (\operatorname{mod}\,2)
.
Then
\mu
fulfils the characterisation of
\lambda^{\ast\epsilon}
given directly above the statement of this proposition, so
\mu=\lambda^{\ast\epsilon}
.

If
\mu
is any partition obtained from
\lambda
by removing then adding
\epsilon
-nodes, then the net number of nodes added (i.e. the number of nodes added, minus the number of nodes removed) is

|\mu|-|\lambda|=\sum_{d\equiv\epsilon}\mu-_{d}\lambda.

Now suppose
\chi(\mu)
appears with non-zero coefficient in
S_{\epsilon}^{(\Delta_{\epsilon}\lambda)}\chi(\lambda)
.
Then
|\mu|-|\lambda|=\Delta_{\epsilon}\lambda=\sum_{d\equiv\epsilon}\iota_{d}(\lambda)
(the latter equality being by definition).
Also,
Lemma

6.2
gives
{\mu-_{d}\lambda}\leqslant\iota_{d}(\lambda)
for every
d\equiv\epsilon\ (\operatorname{mod}\,2)
, and so we have

\sum_{d\equiv\epsilon}\iota_{d}(\lambda)\leqslant\sum_{d\equiv\epsilon}\iota_{d}(\lambda).

But by definition
\Delta_{\epsilon}\lambda=\sum_{d\equiv\epsilon}\iota_{d}(\lambda)
, so the
But the sum over all
d
of
\mu-_{d}\lambda
is
|\mu|-|\lambda|
; this is the net number of nodes added by
S_{\epsilon}^{(\Delta_{\epsilon}\lambda)}
, which is
\Delta_{\epsilon}\lambda=\sum_{d\equiv\epsilon\ (\operatorname{mod}\,2)}\iota_{d}(\lambda)
.
Thus we have equality for every
d
, which forces
\mu=\lambda^{\ast\epsilon}
.

It remains to determine the coefficient of
\chi(\lambda^{\ast\epsilon})
in
S_{\epsilon}^{(\Delta_{\epsilon}\lambda)}\chi(\lambda)
.
It is easy to see that the only partition
\nu
such that
\lambda
and
\lambda^{\ast\epsilon}
are both obtained from
\nu
by adding
\epsilon
-nodes is
\lambda\cap\lambda^{\ast\epsilon}
, the partition obtained from
\lambda
by removing all removable
\epsilon
-nodes.
Thus

\left(S_{\epsilon}^{(\Delta_{\epsilon}\lambda)}\chi(\lambda):\chi(\lambda^{\ast\epsilon})\right)=(-1)^{|\lambda^{\ast\epsilon}|-|\lambda\cap\lambda^{\ast\epsilon}|}.\qed

In view of
6.3
, we would like to understand the partition
\lambda^{\ast\epsilon}
.
Another equivalent characterisation of
\lambda^{\ast\epsilon}
is via the following standard abacus combinatorics, motivating the name “runner-swapping function” for
S_{\epsilon}^{(c)}
.

Proposition 6.4
.

Let
\lambda
be a partition and let
\epsilon\in\left\{0,1\right\}
.
An abacus display for
\lambda^{\ast\epsilon}
can be obtained by choosing an
r
-bead abacus display for
\lambda
where
r\equiv\bar{\epsilon}\ (\operatorname{mod}\,2)
and swapping the runners.

It is well-known (and easy to see) how to identify addable and removable nodes in an abacus display: with the number of beads being congruent to
\bar{\epsilon}
modulo
2
, addable
\epsilon
-nodes correspond to beads on runner
0
with no bead immediately to the right, and adding these nodes corresponds to moving these beads to the right; dually, removable
\epsilon
-nodes correspond to beads on runner
1
with no bead immediately to the left, and removing these nodes corresponds to moving these beads to the left.
So adding all the addable
\epsilon
-nodes and removing all the removable
\epsilon
-nodes can be accomplished on the abacus by moving every bead on runner
0
to the right and every bead on runner
1
to the left, which is the same as swapping the two runners.
∎

The abacus interpretation in
6.4
enables us to describe
\lambda^{\ast\epsilon}
in terms of
2
-cores and
2
-quotients as follows.

Lemma 6.5
.

1.

Let
a\geqslant 0
and let
\epsilon\in\left\{0,1\right\}
.
Then

\Delta_{\epsilon}\kappa_{a}=\begin{cases}a+1&\text{ if $\epsilon\equiv a\ (\operatorname{mod}\,2)$;}\\
-a&\text{ if $\epsilon\not\equiv a\ (\operatorname{mod}\,2)$,}\end{cases}\qquad\kappa_{a}^{\ast\epsilon}=\begin{cases}\kappa_{a+1}&\text{ if $\epsilon\equiv a\ (\operatorname{mod}\,2)$;}\\
\kappa_{a-1}&\text{ if $\epsilon\not\equiv a\ (\operatorname{mod}\,2)$,}\end{cases}

unless
a=0
and
\epsilon=1
, in which case
\Delta_{1}\kappa_{0}=0
still holds but
(\kappa_{0})^{\ast 1}=\kappa_{0}
.

2.

Let
\nu
be a
2
-core partition, let
\lambda^{(0)},\lambda^{(1)}
be partitions and let
\epsilon\in\left\{0,1\right\}
.
Then

[\nu;(\lambda^{(0)},\lambda^{(1)})]^{\ast\epsilon}=[\nu^{\ast\epsilon};(\lambda^{(0)},\lambda^{(1)})]

unless
\nu=\varnothing
and
\epsilon=1
, in which case
[\varnothing;(\lambda^{(0)},\lambda^{(1)})]^{\ast 1}=[\varnothing;(\lambda^{(1)},\lambda^{(0)})]
.
In any case, we have
\Delta_{\epsilon}[\nu;(\lambda^{(0)},\lambda^{(1)})]=\Delta_{\epsilon}\nu
.

Part (i) is routine (with or without
6.4
). For part (ii) we apply
6.4
: write
\lambda=[\nu;(\lambda^{(0)},\lambda^{(1)})]
and choose an
r
-bead abacus display for
\lambda
where
r\equiv\bar{\epsilon}\ (\operatorname{mod}\,2)
, so that an
r
-bead abacus display for
\lambda^{\ast\epsilon}
is obtained by swapping the runners. Then clearly the
2
-core of
\lambda^{\ast\epsilon}
has abacus display given by swapping the runners in an
r
-bead abacus display for
\nu
, which by
6.4
again is precisely
\nu^{\ast\epsilon}
.
Meanwhile by our unusual convention for the
2
-quotient (
Section

2.6
), swapping the runners has no effect on the
2
-quotient provided
\nu\neq\varnothing
or
\epsilon=0
, for then one runner has strictly more beads than the other and we take
\lambda^{(1)}
to be given by the runner with more beads.
In the case
\nu=\varnothing
and
\epsilon=1
, however, the runners have equal numbers of beads so
\lambda^{(1)}
is given by runner
1
before and after swapping, so the components of the
2
-quotient are swapped.
∎

6.1
now follows by combining
6.3
and
6.5
.

Remark 6.6
.

1.

Our runner-swapping functions are not universal in the sense that for a given pair
\lambda,\epsilon
, we need to choose a particular value
c
to obtain
S_{\epsilon}^{(c)}\chi(\lambda)=\pm\chi(\lambda^{\ast\epsilon})
.
In fact, one can define a universal runner-swapping function
S_{\epsilon}
such that
S_{\epsilon}\chi(\lambda)=\pm\chi(\lambda^{\ast\epsilon})
for all
\lambda
, by

S_{\epsilon}=\exp(-f_{\epsilon})\exp(e_{\epsilon})\exp(-f_{\epsilon})=\sum_{a,b,c\geqslant 0}(-1)^{a+c}f^{(a)}_{\epsilon}e^{(b)}_{\epsilon}f^{(c)}_{\epsilon}

(the expression
\exp(-f_{\epsilon})\exp(e_{\epsilon})\exp(-f_{\epsilon})
arises from the general theory of
\mathfrak{sl}_{2}
-representations, and is taken from
[
CR08
, §4.2]
). But we prefer our family of runner-swapping functions
S_{\epsilon}^{(c)}
, which afford simpler calculations.

2.

In the case where
\lambda
is RoCK, so that the runner with more beads has a bead on every row that the other runner does, all but one term in
S_{\epsilon}^{(\Delta_{\epsilon}\lambda)}\chi(\lambda)
vanishes and our function becomes a single (divided power of a) induction or restriction functor.
In this case, Scopes showed that the functor in fact defines a Morita equivalence between the relevant blocks
[
Sco91
]
.
Our application in
Section

5.2
, however, is to use runner-swapping functions to reach non-RoCK partitions.

Remark 6.7
.

The proofs of
Lemmas

6.2
,
6.3
and
6.4

apply just as well with residues taken modulo
p
and abacus displays with
p
runners, for
p
an odd prime.
That is, we have constructed a function that, with suitable choice of parameters, adds all addable nodes and removes all removable nodes of a given residue, or equivalently swaps a pair of adjacent runners in an abacus display. With the appropriate convention for the
p
-quotient (called the “ordered
p
-quotient” in
[
Fay16
]
, where the reader can find more background on the appropriate combinatorics when
p
is odd), this function preserves the
p
-quotient – except (as in the exceptional case in
Lemma

6.5
(ii)) where the two runners in question have the same number of beads, in which case the corresponding components of the
p
-quotient are swapped.

For example, let
p=5
and consider the function
S_{2}^{(1)}=\sum_{a\geqslant 0}(-1)^{(a+1)}f_{2}^{(a+1)}e_{2}^{(a)}
applied to the character labelled by the partition
(9,8,5^{2},2,1^{3})
.
We have

\displaystyle S_{2}^{(1)}\chi(9,8,5,1^{5})

\displaystyle=f_{2}^{(1)}\chi(9,8,5,1^{5})-f_{2}^{(2)}e_{2}^{(1)}\chi(9,8,5,1^{5})

\displaystyle=\chi(9,8,5,1^{6})+\chi(9^{2},5,1^{5})-f_{2}^{(2)}\chi(9,8,4,1^{5})

\displaystyle=-\chi(9^{2},4,1^{6}).

The partitions
(9,8,5,1^{5})
and
(9^{2},4,1^{6})
both have
5
-quotient
(\varnothing,\varnothing,(1^{2}),(2),(1))
(with the appropriate convention), and their
5
-cores, namely
(2)
and
(3)
, differ in the addition of a
2
-node.

This is illustrated with Young diagrams and a choice of abacus display below. The removed and added nodes, and the swapped runners, are coloured.
(Here we have chosen a number of beads congruent to
0
modulo
5
, so that the parts with final nodes of residue
2
are given on runner
2
, and it is runners
1
and
2
that are swapped.)

6.2
Action on spin characters

We next prove a corresponding result for spin characters. This is not quite as general as
6.1
(it doesn’t apply to all strict partitions), though still more general than is needed in
Section

5.2
.

Theorem 6.8
.

Let
\alpha=\bar{\kappa}_{a}\sqcup 2\eta
be a strict partition whose odd parts form a
4
-bar-core.

1.

Let
\epsilon
be the residue of
a
modulo
2
. Then

\displaystyle S_{\epsilon}^{(a+1)}\mathopen{\hbox{${\langle}$}\kern-2.72221pt\hbox{${\langle}$}}\bar{\kappa}_{a}\sqcup 2\eta\mathclose{\hbox{${\rangle}$}\kern-2.72221pt\hbox{${\rangle}$}}=\pm\mathopen{\hbox{${\langle}$}\kern-2.72221pt\hbox{${\langle}$}}\bar{\kappa}_{a+1}\sqcup 2\eta\mathclose{\hbox{${\rangle}$}\kern-2.72221pt\hbox{${\rangle}$}}.

2.

Suppose
a\geqslant 1
, and let
\epsilon
be the residue of
a+1
modulo
2
. Then

\displaystyle S_{\epsilon}^{(-a)}\mathopen{\hbox{${\langle}$}\kern-2.72221pt\hbox{${\langle}$}}\bar{\kappa}_{a}\sqcup 2\eta\mathclose{\hbox{${\rangle}$}\kern-2.72221pt\hbox{${\rangle}$}}=\pm\mathopen{\hbox{${\langle}$}\kern-2.72221pt\hbox{${\langle}$}}\bar{\kappa}_{a-1}\sqcup 2\eta\mathclose{\hbox{${\rangle}$}\kern-2.72221pt\hbox{${\rangle}$}}.

The special case of
6.8
in which
\eta=\kappa_{r}+\kappa_{s}
gives
5.8
(required in the proof of our main theorem).

Our proof is similar to that of
6.1
, but (because we need to consider spin-residues rather than residues) we consider the effect of the functor on pairs of adjacent columns
rather than individual diagonals.

For
\alpha\in\mathscr{D}
and an even non-negative integer
d
, let
\mathring{\iota}_{d}(\alpha)
denote the number of spin-addable nodes in columns
d
and
d+1
minus the number of spin-removable nodes in columns
d
and
d+1
. (When
d=0
, we ignore any reference to column
d
.) Note that any particular column can contain at most one spin-addable node and at most one spin-removable node, and cannot contain both.
Column
1
must contain either a spin-addable or a spin-removable node; meanwhile, for any other column of residue
\epsilon
, if there is a spin-addable or spin-removable node in that column, then the adjacent column of residue
\epsilon
contains either a spin-addable or spin-removable node (not necessarily of the same type).
Thus
\mathring{\iota}_{d}(\alpha)\in\{-2,0,2\}
if
d>0
, and
\mathring{\iota}_{d}(\alpha)\in\{-1,1\}
if
d=0
.

If
\beta
is another strict partition, let
\beta\ominus_{d}\alpha
be the number of nodes of
\beta
in columns
d
and
d+1
minus the number of nodes of
\alpha
in these columns, i.e.
\beta\ominus_{d}\alpha=\beta^{\prime}_{d}+\beta^{\prime}_{d+1}-\alpha^{\prime}_{d}-\alpha^{\prime}_{d+1}
(ignoring
\alpha^{\prime}_{d}
and
\beta^{\prime}_{d}
if
d=0
).

Lemma 6.9
.

Suppose
\alpha\in\mathscr{D}
,
c\in\mathbb{Z}
, and
\epsilon\in\{0,1\}
. If
\beta\in\mathscr{D}
and
\mathopen{\hbox{${\langle}$}\kern-2.72221pt\hbox{${\langle}$}}\beta\mathclose{\hbox{${\rangle}$}\kern-2.72221pt\hbox{${\rangle}$}}
appears with non-zero coefficient in
S_{\epsilon}^{(c)}\mathopen{\hbox{${\langle}$}\kern-2.72221pt\hbox{${\langle}$}}\alpha\mathclose{\hbox{${\rangle}$}\kern-2.72221pt\hbox{${\rangle}$}}
, then
\beta\ominus_{d}\alpha\leqslant\mathring{\iota}_{d}(\alpha)
for all
d\geqslant 0
with
d\equiv 2\epsilon\ (\operatorname{mod}\,4)
.

The fact that
\mathopen{\hbox{${\langle}$}\kern-2.72221pt\hbox{${\langle}$}}\beta\mathclose{\hbox{${\rangle}$}\kern-2.72221pt\hbox{${\rangle}$}}
appears in
S_{\epsilon}^{(c)}\mathopen{\hbox{${\langle}$}\kern-2.72221pt\hbox{${\langle}$}}\alpha\mathclose{\hbox{${\rangle}$}\kern-2.72221pt\hbox{${\rangle}$}}
means that
\beta
is obtained from
\alpha
by removing and then adding
\epsilon
-spin-nodes.
This implies in particular that
-2\leqslant\beta\ominus_{d}\alpha\leqslant 2
for every
d
, and
-1\leqslant\beta\ominus_{0}\alpha\leqslant 1
. Now it is immediate that
\beta\ominus_{d}\alpha\leqslant\mathring{\iota}_{d}(\alpha)
if
\mathring{\iota}_{d}(\alpha)>0
.
So we consider
d
such that
\mathring{\iota}_{d}(\alpha)\leqslant 0
.
We assume for a contradiction that
\beta\ominus_{d}\alpha>\mathring{\iota}_{d}(\alpha)
.

Let
\Gamma
be the set of strict partitions
\gamma
such that
\alpha
and
\beta
can both be obtained from
\gamma
by adding
\epsilon
-spin-nodes. Say that two partitions
\gamma^{0},\gamma^{1}\in\Gamma
are
equivalent
if they agree outside columns
d,d+1
, i.e.
(\gamma^{0})^{\prime}_{k}=(\gamma^{1})^{\prime}_{k}
for all
k\neq d,d+1
.

Now we consider the possible values of
\mathring{\iota}_{d}(\alpha)
and
\beta\ominus_{d}\alpha
satisfying our assumptions. There are four cases.

\mathring{\iota}_{d}(\alpha)=-2
and
\beta\ominus_{d}\alpha=0
:

In this case
\alpha
and
\beta
agree in columns
d,d+1
, both having spin-removable nodes in both columns.
Then each equivalence class in
\Gamma
has the form
\{\gamma^{0},\gamma^{1},\gamma^{2}\}
, where
\gamma^{i}\ominus_{d}\beta=-i
for each
i
.
Take such a class, and let
a=|\alpha|-|\gamma^{0}|
.
We claim that

\sum_{i=0}^{2}(-1)^{a+i+c}\left(e_{\epsilon}^{(a+i)}\mathopen{\hbox{${\langle}$}\kern-2.72221pt\hbox{${\langle}$}}\alpha\mathclose{\hbox{${\rangle}$}\kern-2.72221pt\hbox{${\rangle}$}}:\mathopen{\hbox{${\langle}$}\kern-2.72221pt\hbox{${\langle}$}}\gamma^{i}\mathclose{\hbox{${\rangle}$}\kern-2.72221pt\hbox{${\rangle}$}}\right)\left(f_{\epsilon}^{(a+i+c)}\mathopen{\hbox{${\langle}$}\kern-2.72221pt\hbox{${\langle}$}}\gamma^{i}\mathclose{\hbox{${\rangle}$}\kern-2.72221pt\hbox{${\rangle}$}}:\mathopen{\hbox{${\langle}$}\kern-2.72221pt\hbox{${\langle}$}}\beta\mathclose{\hbox{${\rangle}$}\kern-2.72221pt\hbox{${\rangle}$}}\right)=0.

Using
2.6
, we find that there are positive real numbers
s,t
such that

\displaystyle\left(e_{\epsilon}^{(a)}\mathopen{\hbox{${\langle}$}\kern-2.72221pt\hbox{${\langle}$}}\alpha\mathclose{\hbox{${\rangle}$}\kern-2.72221pt\hbox{${\rangle}$}}:\mathopen{\hbox{${\langle}$}\kern-2.72221pt\hbox{${\langle}$}}\gamma^{0}\mathclose{\hbox{${\rangle}$}\kern-2.72221pt\hbox{${\rangle}$}}\right)

\displaystyle=s,

\displaystyle\qquad\left(f_{\epsilon}^{(a+c)}\mathopen{\hbox{${\langle}$}\kern-2.72221pt\hbox{${\langle}$}}\gamma^{0}\mathclose{\hbox{${\rangle}$}\kern-2.72221pt\hbox{${\rangle}$}}:\mathopen{\hbox{${\langle}$}\kern-2.72221pt\hbox{${\langle}$}}\beta\mathclose{\hbox{${\rangle}$}\kern-2.72221pt\hbox{${\rangle}$}}\right)

\displaystyle=t,

\displaystyle\left(e_{\epsilon}^{(a+1)}\mathopen{\hbox{${\langle}$}\kern-2.72221pt\hbox{${\langle}$}}\alpha\mathclose{\hbox{${\rangle}$}\kern-2.72221pt\hbox{${\rangle}$}}:\mathopen{\hbox{${\langle}$}\kern-2.72221pt\hbox{${\langle}$}}\gamma^{1}\mathclose{\hbox{${\rangle}$}\kern-2.72221pt\hbox{${\rangle}$}}\right)

\displaystyle=\sqrt{2}s,

\displaystyle\qquad\left(f_{\epsilon}^{(a+1+c)}\mathopen{\hbox{${\langle}$}\kern-2.72221pt\hbox{${\langle}$}}\gamma^{1}\mathclose{\hbox{${\rangle}$}\kern-2.72221pt\hbox{${\rangle}$}}:\mathopen{\hbox{${\langle}$}\kern-2.72221pt\hbox{${\langle}$}}\beta\mathclose{\hbox{${\rangle}$}\kern-2.72221pt\hbox{${\rangle}$}}\right)

\displaystyle=\sqrt{2}t,

\displaystyle\left(e_{\epsilon}^{(a+2)}\mathopen{\hbox{${\langle}$}\kern-2.72221pt\hbox{${\langle}$}}\alpha\mathclose{\hbox{${\rangle}$}\kern-2.72221pt\hbox{${\rangle}$}}:\mathopen{\hbox{${\langle}$}\kern-2.72221pt\hbox{${\langle}$}}\gamma^{2}\mathclose{\hbox{${\rangle}$}\kern-2.72221pt\hbox{${\rangle}$}}\right)

\displaystyle=s,

\displaystyle\qquad\left(f_{\epsilon}^{(a+2+c)}\mathopen{\hbox{${\langle}$}\kern-2.72221pt\hbox{${\langle}$}}\gamma^{2}\mathclose{\hbox{${\rangle}$}\kern-2.72221pt\hbox{${\rangle}$}}:\mathopen{\hbox{${\langle}$}\kern-2.72221pt\hbox{${\langle}$}}\beta\mathclose{\hbox{${\rangle}$}\kern-2.72221pt\hbox{${\rangle}$}}\right)

\displaystyle=t,

which yields the claim. Now summing over all equivalence classes gives
\left(S_{\epsilon}^{(c)}\mathopen{\hbox{${\langle}$}\kern-2.72221pt\hbox{${\langle}$}}\alpha\mathclose{\hbox{${\rangle}$}\kern-2.72221pt\hbox{${\rangle}$}}:\mathopen{\hbox{${\langle}$}\kern-2.72221pt\hbox{${\langle}$}}\beta\mathclose{\hbox{${\rangle}$}\kern-2.72221pt\hbox{${\rangle}$}}\right)=0
, contrary to hypothesis.

\mathring{\iota}_{d}(\alpha)=-2
and
\beta\ominus_{d}\alpha=-1
:

In this case
\alpha
has spin-removable nodes in both columns
d
and
d+1
, one of which has been removed in
\beta
.
Then each equivalence class in
\Gamma
has the form
\{\gamma^{0},\gamma^{1}\}
, where again
\gamma^{i}\ominus_{d}\beta=-i
for each
i
, and
\gamma^{1}
is obtained from
\gamma^{0}
by removing a node in column
d
or
d+1
.
Now, letting
a=|\alpha|-|\gamma^{0}|
, there are
s,t
such that

\displaystyle\left(e_{\epsilon}^{(a)}\mathopen{\hbox{${\langle}$}\kern-2.72221pt\hbox{${\langle}$}}\alpha\mathclose{\hbox{${\rangle}$}\kern-2.72221pt\hbox{${\rangle}$}}:\mathopen{\hbox{${\langle}$}\kern-2.72221pt\hbox{${\langle}$}}\gamma^{0}\mathclose{\hbox{${\rangle}$}\kern-2.72221pt\hbox{${\rangle}$}}\right)

\displaystyle=\sqrt{2}s,

\displaystyle\qquad\left(f_{\epsilon}^{(a+c)}\mathopen{\hbox{${\langle}$}\kern-2.72221pt\hbox{${\langle}$}}\gamma^{0}\mathclose{\hbox{${\rangle}$}\kern-2.72221pt\hbox{${\rangle}$}}:\mathopen{\hbox{${\langle}$}\kern-2.72221pt\hbox{${\langle}$}}\beta\mathclose{\hbox{${\rangle}$}\kern-2.72221pt\hbox{${\rangle}$}}\right)

\displaystyle=t,

\displaystyle\left(e_{\epsilon}^{(a+1)}\mathopen{\hbox{${\langle}$}\kern-2.72221pt\hbox{${\langle}$}}\alpha\mathclose{\hbox{${\rangle}$}\kern-2.72221pt\hbox{${\rangle}$}}:\mathopen{\hbox{${\langle}$}\kern-2.72221pt\hbox{${\langle}$}}\gamma^{1}\mathclose{\hbox{${\rangle}$}\kern-2.72221pt\hbox{${\rangle}$}}\right)

\displaystyle=s,

\displaystyle\qquad\left(f_{\epsilon}^{(a+1+c)}\mathopen{\hbox{${\langle}$}\kern-2.72221pt\hbox{${\langle}$}}\gamma^{1}\mathclose{\hbox{${\rangle}$}\kern-2.72221pt\hbox{${\rangle}$}}:\mathopen{\hbox{${\langle}$}\kern-2.72221pt\hbox{${\langle}$}}\beta\mathclose{\hbox{${\rangle}$}\kern-2.72221pt\hbox{${\rangle}$}}\right)

\displaystyle=\sqrt{2}t,

and again we get
\left(S_{\epsilon}^{(c)}\mathopen{\hbox{${\langle}$}\kern-2.72221pt\hbox{${\langle}$}}\alpha\mathclose{\hbox{${\rangle}$}\kern-2.72221pt\hbox{${\rangle}$}}:\mathopen{\hbox{${\langle}$}\kern-2.72221pt\hbox{${\langle}$}}\beta\mathclose{\hbox{${\rangle}$}\kern-2.72221pt\hbox{${\rangle}$}}\right)=0
and a contradiction.

\mathring{\iota}_{d}(\alpha)=-1
and
\beta\ominus_{d}\alpha>-1
:

In this case
d=\epsilon=0
and
\alpha
has a spin-removable node in column
1
.
This node cannot have been removed in
\beta
, or else then
\beta\ominus_{d}\alpha=-1
; so
\beta
also has a spin-removable node in column
1
, and
\beta\ominus_{d}\alpha=0
.
Then each equivalence classes in
\Gamma
has the form
\{\gamma^{0},\gamma^{1}\}
, where
\gamma^{1}
is obtained from
\gamma^{0}
by removing a node in column
1
.
Now there are
s,t
such that

\displaystyle\left(e_{\epsilon}^{(a)}\mathopen{\hbox{${\langle}$}\kern-2.72221pt\hbox{${\langle}$}}\alpha\mathclose{\hbox{${\rangle}$}\kern-2.72221pt\hbox{${\rangle}$}}:\mathopen{\hbox{${\langle}$}\kern-2.72221pt\hbox{${\langle}$}}\gamma^{0}\mathclose{\hbox{${\rangle}$}\kern-2.72221pt\hbox{${\rangle}$}}\right)

\displaystyle=s,

\displaystyle\qquad\left(f_{\epsilon}^{(a+c)}\mathopen{\hbox{${\langle}$}\kern-2.72221pt\hbox{${\langle}$}}\gamma^{0}\mathclose{\hbox{${\rangle}$}\kern-2.72221pt\hbox{${\rangle}$}}:\mathopen{\hbox{${\langle}$}\kern-2.72221pt\hbox{${\langle}$}}\beta\mathclose{\hbox{${\rangle}$}\kern-2.72221pt\hbox{${\rangle}$}}\right)

\displaystyle=t,

\displaystyle\left(e_{\epsilon}^{(a+1)}\mathopen{\hbox{${\langle}$}\kern-2.72221pt\hbox{${\langle}$}}\alpha\mathclose{\hbox{${\rangle}$}\kern-2.72221pt\hbox{${\rangle}$}}:\mathopen{\hbox{${\langle}$}\kern-2.72221pt\hbox{${\langle}$}}\gamma^{1}\mathclose{\hbox{${\rangle}$}\kern-2.72221pt\hbox{${\rangle}$}}\right)

\displaystyle=s,

\displaystyle\qquad\left(f_{\epsilon}^{(a+1+c)}\mathopen{\hbox{${\langle}$}\kern-2.72221pt\hbox{${\langle}$}}\gamma^{1}\mathclose{\hbox{${\rangle}$}\kern-2.72221pt\hbox{${\rangle}$}}:\mathopen{\hbox{${\langle}$}\kern-2.72221pt\hbox{${\langle}$}}\beta\mathclose{\hbox{${\rangle}$}\kern-2.72221pt\hbox{${\rangle}$}}\right)

\displaystyle=t,

and we reach a contradiction as in the previous case.

\mathring{\iota}_{d}(\alpha)=0
and
\beta\ominus_{d}\alpha>0
:

In this case, columns
d
and
d+1
of
\alpha
contain a spin-addable node and a spin-removable node (if neither column contains a spin-removable or spin-addable node, then necessarily
\beta\ominus_{d}\alpha=0
), and the spin-addable node has been added in
\beta
.
Then each equivalence class in
\Gamma
has the form
\{\gamma^{1},\gamma^{2}\}
, where
\gamma^{i}\ominus_{d}\beta=-i
for each
i
, and
\gamma^{2}
is obtained from
\gamma^{1}
by removing a node in column
d
or
d+1
. Now there are
s,t
such that

\displaystyle\left(e_{\epsilon}^{(a)}\mathopen{\hbox{${\langle}$}\kern-2.72221pt\hbox{${\langle}$}}\alpha\mathclose{\hbox{${\rangle}$}\kern-2.72221pt\hbox{${\rangle}$}}:\mathopen{\hbox{${\langle}$}\kern-2.72221pt\hbox{${\langle}$}}\gamma^{1}\mathclose{\hbox{${\rangle}$}\kern-2.72221pt\hbox{${\rangle}$}}\right)

\displaystyle=s,

\displaystyle\qquad\left(f_{\epsilon}^{(a+c)}\mathopen{\hbox{${\langle}$}\kern-2.72221pt\hbox{${\langle}$}}\gamma^{1}\mathclose{\hbox{${\rangle}$}\kern-2.72221pt\hbox{${\rangle}$}}:\mathopen{\hbox{${\langle}$}\kern-2.72221pt\hbox{${\langle}$}}\beta\mathclose{\hbox{${\rangle}$}\kern-2.72221pt\hbox{${\rangle}$}}\right)

\displaystyle=\sqrt{2}t,

\displaystyle\left(e_{\epsilon}^{(a+1)}\mathopen{\hbox{${\langle}$}\kern-2.72221pt\hbox{${\langle}$}}\alpha\mathclose{\hbox{${\rangle}$}\kern-2.72221pt\hbox{${\rangle}$}}:\mathopen{\hbox{${\langle}$}\kern-2.72221pt\hbox{${\langle}$}}\gamma^{2}\mathclose{\hbox{${\rangle}$}\kern-2.72221pt\hbox{${\rangle}$}}\right)

\displaystyle=\sqrt{2}s,

\displaystyle\qquad\left(f_{\epsilon}^{(a+1+c)}\mathopen{\hbox{${\langle}$}\kern-2.72221pt\hbox{${\langle}$}}\gamma^{2}\mathclose{\hbox{${\rangle}$}\kern-2.72221pt\hbox{${\rangle}$}}:\mathopen{\hbox{${\langle}$}\kern-2.72221pt\hbox{${\langle}$}}\beta\mathclose{\hbox{${\rangle}$}\kern-2.72221pt\hbox{${\rangle}$}}\right)

\displaystyle=t,

and again the terms cancel.∎

Given
\alpha\in\mathscr{D}
and
\epsilon\in\{0,1\}
, we construct a strict partition
\alpha^{\oast\epsilon}
as follows. Examine each pair of adjacent columns with spin-residue
\epsilon
, indexed by
d
and
d+1
for some non-negative
d\equiv 2\epsilon\ (\operatorname{mod}\,4)
, in turn: if
\alpha
has spin-addable nodes but not spin-removable nodes in columns
d
and
d+1
, then we add these spin-addable nodes; if
\alpha
has spin-removable nodes but not spin-addable nodes in columns
d
and
d+1
, then we remove the spin-removable nodes; otherwise we do nothing in columns
d
and
d+1
. We can equivalently characterise
\alpha^{\oast\epsilon}
as the unique strict partition such that:

\diamond

\alpha^{\oast\epsilon}
is obtained from
\alpha
by adding and/or removing
\epsilon
-spin-nodes, and

\diamond

\alpha^{\oast\epsilon}\ominus_{d}\alpha=\mathring{\iota}_{d}(\alpha)
for every
d\equiv 2\epsilon\ (\operatorname{mod}\,4)
.

Let
\mathring{\Delta}_{\epsilon}\alpha
be the number of
\epsilon
-spin-addable nodes of
\alpha
minus the number of
\epsilon
-spin-removable nodes (that is,
\mathring{\Delta}_{\epsilon}\alpha=\sum_{d\equiv 2\epsilon\ (\operatorname{mod}\,4)}\mathring{\iota}_{d}(\alpha)
, and also
\mathring{\Delta}_{\epsilon}\alpha=\lvert\alpha^{\oast\epsilon}\rvert-\lvert\alpha\rvert
).

Proposition 6.10
.

Suppose
\alpha\in\mathscr{D}
and
\epsilon\in\{0,1\}
.
Then
S_{\epsilon}^{(\mathring{\Delta}_{\epsilon}\alpha)}\mathopen{\hbox{${\langle}$}\kern-2.72221pt\hbox{${\langle}$}}\alpha\mathclose{\hbox{${\rangle}$}\kern-2.72221pt\hbox{${\rangle}$}}=\pm\mathopen{\hbox{${\langle}$}\kern-2.72221pt\hbox{${\langle}$}}\alpha^{\oast\epsilon}\mathclose{\hbox{${\rangle}$}\kern-2.72221pt\hbox{${\rangle}$}}
.

Suppose
\mathopen{\hbox{${\langle}$}\kern-2.72221pt\hbox{${\langle}$}}\beta\mathclose{\hbox{${\rangle}$}\kern-2.72221pt\hbox{${\rangle}$}}
appears with non-zero coefficient in
S_{\epsilon}^{(\mathring{\Delta}_{\epsilon}\alpha)}\mathopen{\hbox{${\langle}$}\kern-2.72221pt\hbox{${\langle}$}}\alpha\mathclose{\hbox{${\rangle}$}\kern-2.72221pt\hbox{${\rangle}$}}
.

Lemma

6.9
gives
\beta\ominus_{d}\alpha\leqslant\mathring{\iota}_{d}(\alpha)
for every
d\equiv 2\epsilon\ (\operatorname{mod}\,4)
.
But the sum over all
d
of
\beta\ominus_{d}\alpha
is
|\beta|-|\alpha|
; this is the net number of nodes added by
S_{\epsilon}^{(\mathring{\Delta}_{\epsilon}\alpha)}
, which is
\mathring{\Delta}_{\epsilon}\alpha=\sum_{d\equiv 2\epsilon\ (\operatorname{mod}\,4)}\mathring{\iota}_{d}(\alpha)
.
Thus we have equality for every
d
, which forces
\beta=\alpha^{\oast\epsilon}
.

It remains to determine the coefficient of
\mathopen{\hbox{${\langle}$}\kern-2.72221pt\hbox{${\langle}$}}\alpha^{\oast\epsilon}\mathclose{\hbox{${\rangle}$}\kern-2.72221pt\hbox{${\rangle}$}}
in
S_{\epsilon}^{(\mathring{\Delta}_{\epsilon}\alpha)}\mathopen{\hbox{${\langle}$}\kern-2.72221pt\hbox{${\langle}$}}\alpha\mathclose{\hbox{${\rangle}$}\kern-2.72221pt\hbox{${\rangle}$}}
.
To do this, let
\Gamma
be the set of
\gamma\in\mathscr{D}
such that
\alpha
and
\alpha^{\oast\epsilon}
can both be obtained by adding
\epsilon
-spin-nodes to
\gamma
. The strict partition
\alpha\cap\alpha^{\oast\epsilon}
, obtained from
\alpha
by removing the pairs of
\epsilon
-spin-removable nodes in adjacent columns of
\alpha
(and the
\epsilon
-spin-removable node in column 1, if it exists), is one such partition.
Setting
a=\lvert\alpha\rvert-\lvert\alpha\cap\alpha^{\oast\epsilon}\rvert
, we have from
2.6

\left(e_{\epsilon}^{(a)}\mathopen{\hbox{${\langle}$}\kern-2.72221pt\hbox{${\langle}$}}\alpha\mathclose{\hbox{${\rangle}$}\kern-2.72221pt\hbox{${\rangle}$}}:\mathopen{\hbox{${\langle}$}\kern-2.72221pt\hbox{${\langle}$}}\alpha\cap\alpha^{\oast\epsilon}\mathclose{\hbox{${\rangle}$}\kern-2.72221pt\hbox{${\rangle}$}}\right)=\left(f_{\epsilon}^{(a+\mathring{\Delta}_{\epsilon}\alpha)}\mathopen{\hbox{${\langle}$}\kern-2.72221pt\hbox{${\langle}$}}\alpha\cap\alpha^{\oast\epsilon}\mathclose{\hbox{${\rangle}$}\kern-2.72221pt\hbox{${\rangle}$}}:\mathopen{\hbox{${\langle}$}\kern-2.72221pt\hbox{${\langle}$}}\alpha^{\oast\epsilon}\mathclose{\hbox{${\rangle}$}\kern-2.72221pt\hbox{${\rangle}$}}\right)=1.

Thus the contribution from
\alpha\cap\alpha^{\oast\epsilon}
to the coefficient of
\mathopen{\hbox{${\langle}$}\kern-2.72221pt\hbox{${\langle}$}}\alpha^{\oast\epsilon}\mathclose{\hbox{${\rangle}$}\kern-2.72221pt\hbox{${\rangle}$}}
is
(-1)^{a+\mathring{\Delta}_{\epsilon}\alpha}
.

To describe
\Gamma
fully, let
D
be the set of all
d\equiv 2\epsilon\ (\operatorname{mod}\,4)
such that
\alpha
has both a spin-removable node and a spin-addable node in columns
d,d+1
.
For every
E\subseteq D
, define
\gamma_{E}
to be the unique strict partition such that

\diamond

\gamma_{E}
agrees with
\alpha\cap\alpha^{\oast\epsilon}
except in columns
d,d+1
for
d\in E
, and

\diamond

\gamma_{E}\ominus_{d}\alpha=-1
for all
d\in E
(that is, the spin-removable node in column
d
or
d+1
has been removed in
\gamma_{E}
for all
d\in E
).

Then
\Gamma=\left\{\left.\gamma_{E}\ \right|\ \smash{E\subseteq D}\right\}
, with
\gamma_{\varnothing}=\alpha\cap\alpha^{\oast\epsilon}
and
|\gamma_{E}|=|\alpha\cap\alpha^{\oast\epsilon}|-|E|
for each
E
.
Furthermore,
2.6
gives

\left(e_{\epsilon}^{(a+|E|)}\mathopen{\hbox{${\langle}$}\kern-2.72221pt\hbox{${\langle}$}}\alpha\mathclose{\hbox{${\rangle}$}\kern-2.72221pt\hbox{${\rangle}$}}:\mathopen{\hbox{${\langle}$}\kern-2.72221pt\hbox{${\langle}$}}\gamma_{E}\mathclose{\hbox{${\rangle}$}\kern-2.72221pt\hbox{${\rangle}$}}\right)=\left(f_{\epsilon}^{(a+|E|+c)}\mathopen{\hbox{${\langle}$}\kern-2.72221pt\hbox{${\langle}$}}\gamma_{E}\mathclose{\hbox{${\rangle}$}\kern-2.72221pt\hbox{${\rangle}$}}:\mathopen{\hbox{${\langle}$}\kern-2.72221pt\hbox{${\langle}$}}\alpha^{\oast\epsilon}\mathclose{\hbox{${\rangle}$}\kern-2.72221pt\hbox{${\rangle}$}}\right)=\sqrt{2}^{|E|}.

Summing over
E
, we obtain

\displaystyle\left(S_{\epsilon}^{(\mathring{\Delta}_{\epsilon}\alpha)}\mathopen{\hbox{${\langle}$}\kern-2.72221pt\hbox{${\langle}$}}\alpha\mathclose{\hbox{${\rangle}$}\kern-2.72221pt\hbox{${\rangle}$}}:\mathopen{\hbox{${\langle}$}\kern-2.72221pt\hbox{${\langle}$}}\alpha^{\oast\epsilon}\mathclose{\hbox{${\rangle}$}\kern-2.72221pt\hbox{${\rangle}$}}\right)

\displaystyle=(-1)^{a+\mathring{\Delta}_{\epsilon}\alpha}\sum_{E\subseteq D}(-2)^{|E|}

\displaystyle=(-1)^{a+\mathring{\Delta}_{\epsilon}\alpha}\sum_{k=0}^{\lvert D\rvert}\binom{\lvert D\rvert}{k}(-2)^{k}

\displaystyle=(-1)^{a+\mathring{\Delta}_{\epsilon}\alpha}(1-2)^{\lvert D\rvert}

\displaystyle=(-1)^{a+\mathring{\Delta}_{\epsilon}\alpha+|D|}.\qed

In view of
6.10
, we would like to understand the partition
\alpha^{\oast\epsilon}
.

Lemma 6.11
.

Suppose
\alpha\in\mathscr{D}
and
\epsilon\in\{0,1\}
. Then
\alpha^{\oast\epsilon}
is obtained from
\alpha
by:

\diamond

replacing each part
d\equiv 2\epsilon-1\ (\operatorname{mod}\,4)
with
d+2
,

\diamond

replacing each part
d>1
such that
d\equiv 2\epsilon+1\ (\operatorname{mod}\,4)
with
d-2
,

\diamond

inserting the part
1
, if
\epsilon=0
and
1\notin\alpha
,

\diamond

removing the part
1
, if
\epsilon=0
and
1\in\alpha

and then reordering parts into decreasing order.

For each
j>0
with
j\equiv 2\epsilon\ (\operatorname{mod}\,4)
we can consider the two columns
j,j+1
of spin-removable in isolation and verify the result; there are eight cases to check, depending on which of the integers
j-1,j,j+1
are parts of
\alpha
.

When
\epsilon=0
, we additionally need to consider column
1
, and again the result is easy to check.
∎

Remark 6.12
.

Lemma

6.11
can alternatively be described in terms of the
4
-bar-abacus introduced by Bessenrodt and Olsson
[
BO97
]
: it says that the abacus display for
\alpha^{\oast\epsilon}
can be obtained from the abacus display for
\alpha
by swapping runners
1
and
3
. This gives further justification to the name “runner-swapping function”. We refer the reader to
[
BO97
, §3]
for more details on the
4
-bar-abacus.

Now we can give an analogue of
Lemma

6.5
for strict partitions.

Lemma 6.13
.

1.

Let
a\geqslant 0
and let
\epsilon\in\left\{0,1\right\}
.
Then

\mathring{\Delta}_{\epsilon}\bar{\kappa}_{a}=\begin{cases}a+1&\text{ if $\epsilon\equiv a\ (\operatorname{mod}\,2)$;}\\
-a&\text{ if $\epsilon\not\equiv a\ (\operatorname{mod}\,2)$,}\end{cases}\qquad\bar{\kappa}_{a}^{\oast\epsilon}=\begin{cases}\bar{\kappa}_{a+1}&\text{ if $\epsilon\equiv a\ (\operatorname{mod}\,2)$;}\\
\bar{\kappa}_{a-1}&\text{ if $\epsilon\not\equiv a\ (\operatorname{mod}\,2)$,}\end{cases}

unless
a=0
and
\epsilon=1
, in which case
\mathring{\Delta}_{1}\bar{\kappa}_{0}=0
still holds but
(\bar{\kappa}_{0})^{\oast 1}=\bar{\kappa}_{0}
.

2.

Let
\gamma\in\mathscr{D}
with all parts odd, let
\eta\in\mathscr{D}
and let
\epsilon\in\left\{0,1\right\}
.
Then

(\gamma\sqcup 2\eta)^{\oast\epsilon}=\gamma^{\oast\epsilon}\sqcup 2\eta

and hence
\mathring{\Delta}_{\epsilon}(\gamma\sqcup 2\eta)=\mathring{\Delta}_{\epsilon}\gamma
.

Part (i) is routine, and part (ii) follows immediately from
Lemma

6.11
.
∎

6.8
now follows by combining
6.10
and
6.13
.

Remark 6.14
.

It is interesting to ask what we get when we apply the function
S_{\epsilon}^{(c)}
for values of
c
other than
\Delta_{\epsilon}\lambda
or
\mathring{\Delta}_{\epsilon}\alpha
.

First consider a partition
\lambda
.

Lemma

6.2
(and the argument at the start of the proof of
6.3
) shows that if
c>\Delta_{\epsilon}\lambda
, then
S_{\epsilon}^{(c)}\chi(\lambda)=0
.
On the other hand, if
r_{\epsilon}
denotes the number of removable
\epsilon
-nodes of
\lambda
, then clearly
S_{\epsilon}^{(c)}\chi(\lambda)=0
for
c<-r_{\epsilon}
, whilst
S_{\epsilon}^{(-r_{\epsilon})}\chi(\lambda)=\pm\chi(\lambda^{-\epsilon})
, where
\lambda^{-\epsilon}
is the partition obtained by removing all the removable
\epsilon
-nodes from
\lambda
.
Meanwhile if
-r_{\epsilon}<c<\Delta_{\epsilon}\lambda
, it is not hard to show that
S_{\epsilon}^{(c)}\chi(\lambda)
is a linear combination of two or more different characters.

Now consider
\alpha\in\mathscr{D}
. If
c>\mathring{\Delta}_{\epsilon}\alpha
, then
Lemma

6.9
shows that
S_{\epsilon}^{(c)}\mathopen{\hbox{${\langle}$}\kern-2.72221pt\hbox{${\langle}$}}\alpha\mathclose{\hbox{${\rangle}$}\kern-2.72221pt\hbox{${\rangle}$}}=0
. On the other hand, if
r_{\epsilon}
denotes the number of
\epsilon
-spin-removable nodes of
\alpha
, then
S_{\epsilon}^{(c)}\mathopen{\hbox{${\langle}$}\kern-2.72221pt\hbox{${\langle}$}}\alpha\mathclose{\hbox{${\rangle}$}\kern-2.72221pt\hbox{${\rangle}$}}=0
for
c<-r_{\epsilon}
, whilst
S_{\epsilon}^{(-r_{\epsilon})}\mathopen{\hbox{${\langle}$}\kern-2.72221pt\hbox{${\langle}$}}\alpha\mathclose{\hbox{${\rangle}$}\kern-2.72221pt\hbox{${\rangle}$}}
is a non-zero multiple of
\alpha^{-\epsilon}
, where
\alpha^{-\epsilon}
is the partition obtained by removing all the
\epsilon
-spin-removable nodes from
\alpha
.
However, this multiple need not be
\pm 1
; for example,
S_{1}^{(-1)}\mathopen{\hbox{${\langle}$}\kern-2.72221pt\hbox{${\langle}$}}(2)\mathclose{\hbox{${\rangle}$}\kern-2.72221pt\hbox{${\rangle}$}}=\sqrt{2}\mathopen{\hbox{${\langle}$}\kern-2.72221pt\hbox{${\langle}$}}(1)\mathclose{\hbox{${\rangle}$}\kern-2.72221pt\hbox{${\rangle}$}}
.
Meanwhile if
-r_{\epsilon}<c<\mathring{\Delta}_{\epsilon}\alpha
, then
S_{\epsilon}^{(c)}\mathopen{\hbox{${\langle}$}\kern-2.72221pt\hbox{${\langle}$}}\alpha\mathclose{\hbox{${\rangle}$}\kern-2.72221pt\hbox{${\rangle}$}}
is a linear combination of two or more characters, except in some cases where
c=\mathring{\Delta}_{\epsilon}\alpha-1
. We leave the reader to work out the details.

7
Quotient-redistributing functions

The purpose of this section is to describe the action of the quotient-redistributing function on certain RoCK characters and spin characters.
We recall the definition of the function from
Section

5
.

See
5.5

7.1
Action on characters

We will apply the quotient-redistributing function to characters in RoCK blocks. We say that a partition
\lambda
is RoCK if the character
\chi(\lambda)
lies in a RoCK block; that is, if
w\leqslant c+1
, where
\lambda
has
2
-weight
w
and
2
-core
(c,c-1,\dots,1)
. In fact the structure of RoCK partitions is quite easy to understand; they were first analysed by James and Mathas in
[
JM96
]
(where they were called “partitions with enormous
2
-core”). Recall that if
\lambda
is a partition, then we write
\underline{\smash{\lambda}}=(\lambda^{(0)},\lambda^{(1)})
for its
2
-quotient. If
\lambda
is RoCK, then
\lambda
is obtained from its
2
-core by adding
\lambda^{(1)}_{i}
horizontal dominoes (i.e. pairs of orthogonally adjacent nodes) in row
i
, and adding
(\lambda^{(0)})^{\prime}_{i}
vertical dominoes in column
i
, for each
i
. The RoCK condition ensures that the horizontal and vertical dominoes do not meet.
This structure is illustrated in
Figure

3
.
