Figure 3
:
The RoCK partition
(20,19,16,13,10,9,8,7,6,5,4,3,2^{5},1^{3})
, depicted as a Young diagram, on an abacus, and in terms of its
2
-core and
2
-quotient. Observe the correspondence between nodes in the components of the
2
-quotient and “dominoes” in the Young diagram indicated by the colouring.

We introduce some notation to help us describe the action of the quotient-redistributing function on RoCK partitions.

Definition 7.1
.

Given bipartitions
\underline{\smash{\lambda}}
and
\underline{\smash{\mu}}
, write
\underline{\smash{\lambda}}\rightharpoonup\underline{\smash{\mu}}
if
\mu^{(0)}
can be obtained from
\lambda^{(0)}
by adding nodes in distinct columns, and
\mu^{(1)}
can be obtained from
\lambda^{(1)}
by adding nodes in distinct rows.

Now the following result comes from the branching rule (after some translation of notation, part (ii) is effectively a special case of
[
CT02
, Lemma 3.1]
).

Lemma 7.2
.

Let
\lambda
be a partition with
2
-core
\nu
and
2
-weight
w
.
Let
r\geqslant 0
.
Let
\epsilon
be the residue of
l(\nu)+1
modulo
2
.

1.

Suppose
w\leqslant l(\nu)+1
(that is, that
\lambda
is RoCK).

\left(e_{\bar{\epsilon}}^{(r)}e_{\epsilon}^{(r)}\chi(\lambda):\chi(\mu)\right)=\begin{cases}1&\parbox{318.0pt}{if $\mu$ is a partition with $2$-core $\nu$ and $2$-weight $w-r$, and $\underline{\smash{\mu}}\rightharpoonup\underline{\smash{\lambda}}$;}\\
0&\text{otherwise.}\end{cases}

2.

Suppose
w+r\leqslant l(\nu)+1
(that is, that any partition obtained from
\lambda
by adding
r
dominoes is RoCK).

\left(f_{\epsilon}^{(r)}f_{\bar{\epsilon}}^{(r)}\chi(\lambda):\chi(\mu)\right)=\begin{cases}1&\parbox{318.0pt}{if $\mu$ is a partition with $2$-core $\nu$ and $2$-weight $w+r$, and $\underline{\smash{\mu}}\leftharpoonup\underline{\smash{\lambda}}$;}\\
0&\text{otherwise.}\end{cases}

Observe that the functors
e_{\bar{\epsilon}}^{(r)}e_{\epsilon}^{(r)}
and
f_{\epsilon}^{(r)}f_{\bar{\epsilon}}^{(r)}
modify only the
2
-quotient of the partition labelling the character they act on, and not its
2
-core (in fact this is true even if the partition is not RoCK).

We deduce a description of the action of the quotient-redistributing functor.

Definition 7.3
.

Given bipartitions
\underline{\smash{\lambda}}
and
\underline{\smash{\mu}}
, let

\mathcal{I}(\underline{\smash{\lambda}},\underline{\smash{\mu}})=\{\,\underline{\smash{\nu}}\,\mid\,\underline{\smash{\lambda}}\leftharpoonup\underline{\smash{\nu}}\rightharpoonup\underline{\smash{\mu}}\,\}.

The following proposition follows immediately from the definitions and
Lemma

7.2
.

Proposition 7.4
.

Let
\lambda
be a partition with
2
-core
\nu
and
2
-weight
w
.
Let
d\in\mathbb{Z}
.
Let
\epsilon
be the residue of
l(\nu)+1
modulo
2
.
Suppose
\max\{w,w+d\}\leqslant l(\nu)+1
(that is, that
\lambda
, and any partition obtained from
\lambda
by adding
d
dominoes if
d>0
, is RoCK).
Then

R_{\epsilon}^{(d)}\chi(\lambda)=\sum_{\mu}\left(\sum_{\underline{\smash{\nu}}\in\mathcal{I}(\underline{\smash{\lambda}},\underline{\smash{\mu}})}(-1)^{\lvert\underline{\smash{\mu}}/\underline{\smash{\nu}}\rvert}\right)\chi(\mu)

where the sum is over all partitions
\mu
with
2
-core
\nu
and
2
-weight
w-d
.

It remains, then, to consider
\mathcal{I}(\underline{\smash{\lambda}},\underline{\smash{\mu}})
for the desired bipartitions. Given
r,s\geqslant 0
, we write
\underline{\smash{\kappa}}_{r,s}
for the bipartition
(\kappa_{r},\kappa_{s})
.

Lemma 7.5
.

Let
s>r\geqslant 0
.
Let
\underline{\smash{\mu}}
be a bipartition of
\lvert\underline{\smash{\kappa}}_{r+1,s-1}\rvert
.
Then

\sum_{\underline{\smash{\nu}}\in\mathcal{I}(\underline{\smash{\kappa}}_{r,s},\underline{\smash{\mu}})}(-1)^{\lvert\underline{\smash{\mu}}/\underline{\smash{\nu}}\rvert}=\begin{cases}(-1)^{r+1}&\text{ if $\underline{\smash{\mu}}=\underline{\smash{\kappa}}_{r+1,s-1}$;}\\
0&\text{otherwise.}\end{cases}

Let
\underline{\smash{\lambda}}
be any bipartition and let
\underline{\smash{\nu}}\in\mathcal{I}(\underline{\smash{\lambda}},\underline{\smash{\mu}})
.
Suppose there exists a removable node
x
in
\underline{\smash{\lambda}}
which is also present (though not necessarily removable) in
\underline{\smash{\mu}}
, and furthermore that if
x
lies in
\underline{\smash{\mu}}^{(0)}
then there is no node directly below it in
\underline{\smash{\mu}}^{(0)}
, while if
x
lies in
\underline{\smash{\mu}}^{(1)}
then there is no node directly to its right in
\underline{\smash{\mu}}^{(1)}
.
Observe that if
x
is present in
\underline{\smash{\nu}}
, then it is removable in
\underline{\smash{\nu}}
, and furthermore the column or row (according to whether
x
lies in the
0
th or
1
st component) containing
x
is unchanged between
\underline{\smash{\lambda}}
,
\underline{\smash{\nu}}
and
\underline{\smash{\mu}}
, so that
\underline{\smash{\nu}}/x
also lies in
\mathcal{I}(\underline{\smash{\lambda}},\underline{\smash{\mu}})
.
On the other hand, if
x
is not present in
\underline{\smash{\nu}}
, then
x
is addable to
\underline{\smash{\nu}}
, and
\underline{\smash{\nu}}+x
lies in
\mathcal{I}(\underline{\smash{\lambda}},\underline{\smash{\mu}})
.
Thus we can define an involution on the set
\mathcal{I}(\underline{\smash{\lambda}},\underline{\smash{\mu}})
by adding or removing the node
x
. This involution also changes the number of nodes added to reach
\underline{\smash{\mu}}
from
\underline{\smash{\nu}}
by exactly
1
, and therefore reverses the sign
(-1)^{\lvert\underline{\smash{\mu}}/\underline{\smash{\nu}}\rvert}
in the sum
\sum_{\underline{\smash{\nu}}\in\mathcal{I}(\underline{\smash{\lambda}},\underline{\smash{\mu}})}(-1)^{\lvert\underline{\smash{\mu}}/\underline{\smash{\nu}}\rvert}
. Thus the contributions cancel out, and the sum vanishes.

Now consider
\underline{\smash{\lambda}}=\underline{\smash{\kappa}}_{r,s}
, which contains a removable node in every nonempty row and column.
The previous paragraph shows that our sum vanishes unless
\underline{\smash{\mu}}
is obtained from
\underline{\smash{\kappa}}_{r,s}
in such a way that every nonempty column of
\kappa_{r}
and every nonempty row of
\kappa_{s}
has a node either added or removed.
In this case, the number of added and removed nodes amongst the nonempty rows and columns is equal to
r+s
, and so the change in the number of nodes amongst these rows and columns is congruent to
r+s
modulo
2
.
But the total change in the number of nodes going from
\underline{\smash{\kappa}}_{r,s}
to
\mu
is

|\mu|-|\underline{\smash{\kappa}}_{r,s}|=|\underline{\smash{\kappa}}_{r+1,s-1}|-|\underline{\smash{\kappa}}_{r,s}|=-(s-r-1).

So there are more nodes added or removed than in just the nonempty rows or columns.
The only possibilities are the addition of nodes in the first row (i.e. the empty columns) of
\kappa_{r}
and the addition of nodes in the first column (i.e. the empty rows) of
\kappa_{s}
.

If nodes are added in the first column of
\kappa_{s}
, this means the final nonempty row in
\kappa_{s}
cannot have a node removed, so a node must be added; then the row above that cannot have a node removed, and so on; thus every nonempty row of
\kappa_{s}
has a node added.
Then the total change in number of nodes from
\underline{\smash{\kappa}}_{r,s}
to
\underline{\smash{\mu}}
is at least
(s+1)-r>0
, a net gain, contradicting the assumed size of
\underline{\smash{\mu}}
.
Thus nodes must be added in the first row of
\kappa_{r}
, and hence a node must be added in every nonempty column of
\kappa_{r}
; then for the total change in number of nodes to be
-(s-r-1)
there must be exactly
1
node added to the first row of
\kappa_{r}
and a node removed from every nonempty row of
\kappa_{s}
.
This precisely yields
\underline{\smash{\kappa}}_{r+1,s-1}
.

It remains to see that
\sum_{\underline{\smash{\nu}}\in\mathcal{I}(\underline{\smash{\kappa}}_{r,s},\underline{\smash{\kappa}}_{r+1,s-1})}(-1)^{\lvert\underline{\smash{\kappa}}_{r+1,s-1}/\underline{\smash{\nu}}\rvert}
equals
(-1)^{r+1}
.
Indeed,
\underline{\smash{\kappa}}_{r,s-1}
is the unique element of
\mathcal{I}(\underline{\smash{\kappa}}_{r,s},\underline{\smash{\kappa}}_{r+1,s-1})
, and
\lvert\underline{\smash{\kappa}}_{r+1,s-1}/\underline{\smash{\kappa}}_{r,s-1}\rvert=r+1
.
∎

Combining
7.4
and
7.5
allows us to deduce the action on the characters required in
Section

5
, restated below.

See
5.10

7.2
Action on spin characters

Now we come to spin characters, where we also focus on RoCK blocks. We say that
\alpha\in\mathscr{D}
is RoCK if the character
\mathopen{\hbox{${\langle}$}\kern-2.72221pt\hbox{${\langle}$}}\alpha\mathclose{\hbox{${\rangle}$}\kern-2.72221pt\hbox{${\rangle}$}}
lies in a RoCK block.

We begin by looking at the structure of
4
-semicongruent RoCK strict partitions. Part (i) of the following
Lemma
is immediate; part (ii) is given in
[
Fay18
, Corollaries 5.3 & 5.5]
.

Lemma 7.6
.

Let
\alpha\in\mathscr{D}
with
4
-bar-core
\gamma
.

1.

\alpha
is
4
-semicongruent if and only if
\alpha=(\gamma+4\sigma)\sqcup 2\eta
for some
\sigma\in\mathscr{P}
,
\eta\in\mathscr{D}
.

2.

\alpha
is RoCK if and only if
\alpha=(\gamma+4\sigma)\sqcup 2\eta
for some
\sigma\in\mathscr{P}
,
\eta\in\mathscr{D}
with
2\lvert\sigma\rvert+\lvert\eta\rvert\leqslant l(\mathrm{dbl}(\gamma))+1
.

It turns out that the action of the quotient-redistributing function can easily be described in terms of the decomposition given in
Lemma

7.6
.

We begin with a spin analogue of
Lemma

7.2
. We continue to use the notation
\rightharpoonup
from
Section

7.1
, and define the following parameter.

Definition 7.7
.

Given
\eta,\theta\in\mathscr{D}
with
\eta\supseteq\theta
, define
k(\eta/\theta)
to be the number of values
c\geqslant 1
such that exactly one of columns
c
and
c+1
contains a node of
\eta/\theta
.

We note that
k(\eta/\theta)
can also be characterised as the number of integers which are parts of
\eta
or
\theta
but not both.

Proposition 7.8

(
[
Fay20
, Proposition 5.6 and its Frobenius reciprocal]
)
.

Suppose
\alpha\in\mathscr{D}
with
4
-bar-core
\gamma
and
4
-bar-weight
w
.
Write
\alpha=(\gamma+4\sigma)\sqcup 2\eta
, where
\sigma\in\mathscr{P}
,
\eta\in\mathscr{D}
are such that
2\lvert\sigma\rvert+\lvert\eta\rvert=w
.
Let
r\geqslant 0
.
Let
\epsilon
be the residue of
l(\mathrm{dbl}(\gamma))
modulo
2
.

1.

Suppose
w\leqslant l(\mathrm{dbl}(\gamma))+1
.

\left(e_{\bar{\epsilon}}^{(r)}e_{\epsilon}^{(r)}\mathopen{\hbox{${\langle}$}\kern-2.72221pt\hbox{${\langle}$}}\alpha\mathclose{\hbox{${\rangle}$}\kern-2.72221pt\hbox{${\rangle}$}}:\mathopen{\hbox{${\langle}$}\kern-2.72221pt\hbox{${\langle}$}}\beta\mathclose{\hbox{${\rangle}$}\kern-2.72221pt\hbox{${\rangle}$}}\right)=\begin{cases}\sqrt{2}^{\,k(\eta/\theta)}&\parbox{254.0pt}{if $\beta\in\mathscr{D}$ with $4$-bar-core $\gamma$ and $4$-bar-weight $w-r$, expressed as $\beta=(\gamma+4\tau)\sqcup 2\theta$ where $(\theta,\tau)\rightharpoonup(\eta,\sigma)$;}\\
0&\text{otherwise.}\end{cases}

2.

Suppose
w+r\leqslant l(\mathrm{dbl}(\gamma))+1
.

\left(f_{\epsilon}^{(r)}f_{\bar{\epsilon}}^{(r)}\mathopen{\hbox{${\langle}$}\kern-2.72221pt\hbox{${\langle}$}}\alpha\mathclose{\hbox{${\rangle}$}\kern-2.72221pt\hbox{${\rangle}$}}:\mathopen{\hbox{${\langle}$}\kern-2.72221pt\hbox{${\langle}$}}\beta\mathclose{\hbox{${\rangle}$}\kern-2.72221pt\hbox{${\rangle}$}}\right)=\begin{cases}\sqrt{2}^{\,k(\eta/\theta)}&\parbox{254.0pt}{if $\beta\in\mathscr{D}$ with $4$-bar-core $\gamma$ and $4$-bar-weight $w+r$, expressed as $\beta=(\gamma+4\tau)\sqcup 2\theta$ where $(\theta,\tau)\leftharpoonup(\eta,\sigma)$;}\\
0&\text{otherwise.}\end{cases}

In view of this, it is natural to make the following definitions.

Definition 7.9
.

Given
\eta,\theta\in\mathscr{D}
, let

\displaystyle\mathcal{I}_{0}(\eta,\theta)

\displaystyle=\left\{\,\zeta\in\mathscr{D}\,\,\middle|\,\,\parbox{173.0pt}{both $\eta$ and $\theta$ can be obtained from $\zeta$ by adding nodes in distinct columns}\,\right\}

and given
\sigma,\tau\in\mathscr{P}
, let

\displaystyle\mathcal{I}_{1}(\sigma,\tau)

\displaystyle=\left\{\,\varphi\in\mathscr{P}\,\,\middle|\,\,\parbox{173.0pt}{both $\sigma$ and $\tau$ can be obtained from $\varphi$ by adding nodes in distinct rows}\,\right\}.

Let
A_{\sigma,\tau}=\lvert\mathcal{I}_{1}(\sigma,\tau)\rvert
.

Proposition 7.10
.

Suppose
\alpha\in\mathscr{D}
with
4
-bar-core
\gamma
and
4
-bar-weight
w
, and
d\in\mathbb{Z}
. Let
\epsilon
be the residue of
l(\mathrm{dbl}(\gamma))+1
modulo
2
.
Suppose
\max\{w,w+d\}\leqslant l(\mathrm{dbl}(\gamma))+1
.
Write
\alpha=(\gamma+4\sigma)\sqcup 2\eta
, where
\eta\in\mathscr{D}
and
\sigma\in\mathscr{P}
with
2\lvert\sigma\rvert+\lvert\eta\rvert=w
.
Then

R_{\epsilon}^{(d)}\mathopen{\hbox{${\langle}$}\kern-2.72221pt\hbox{${\langle}$}}\alpha\mathclose{\hbox{${\rangle}$}\kern-2.72221pt\hbox{${\rangle}$}}=\sum_{\beta}\left(A_{\sigma,\tau}\sum_{\zeta\in\mathcal{I}_{0}(\eta,\theta)}(-1)^{\lvert\theta/\zeta\rvert}\sqrt{2}^{\,k(\eta/\zeta)+k(\theta/\zeta)}\right)\mathopen{\hbox{${\langle}$}\kern-2.72221pt\hbox{${\langle}$}}\beta\mathclose{\hbox{${\rangle}$}\kern-2.72221pt\hbox{${\rangle}$}}

where the sum is over all
\beta\in\mathscr{D}
with
4
-bar-core
\gamma
and
4
-bar-weight
w+d
expressed as
\beta=(\gamma+4\tau)\sqcup 2\theta
.

It follows from the definitions and
7.8
that

\displaystyle R_{\epsilon}^{(d)}\mathopen{\hbox{${\langle}$}\kern-2.72221pt\hbox{${\langle}$}}\alpha\mathclose{\hbox{${\rangle}$}\kern-2.72221pt\hbox{${\rangle}$}}

\displaystyle=\sum_{\beta}\left(\sum_{(\zeta,\varphi)\in\mathcal{I}_{0}(\eta,\theta)\times\mathcal{I}_{1}(\sigma,\tau)}(-1)^{\lvert\theta/\zeta\rvert+2\lvert\tau/\varphi\rvert}\sqrt{2}^{\,k(\eta/\zeta)+k(\theta/\zeta)}\right)\mathopen{\hbox{${\langle}$}\kern-2.72221pt\hbox{${\langle}$}}\beta\mathclose{\hbox{${\rangle}$}\kern-2.72221pt\hbox{${\rangle}$}}

where the sum is over all
\beta\in\mathscr{D}
with
4
-bar-core
\gamma
and
4
-bar-weight
w+d
expressed as
\beta=(\gamma+4\tau)\sqcup 2\theta
.
This simplifies to the expression in the
Section
by noting that the summands of the inner sum do not depend on
\varphi\in\mathcal{I}_{1}(\sigma,\tau)
.
∎

Our next task is to evaluate the sum over
\mathcal{I}_{0}(\eta,\theta)
appearing in
7.10
, which we denote

B_{\eta,\theta}=\sum_{\zeta\in\mathcal{I}_{0}(\eta,\theta)}(-1)^{\lvert\theta/\zeta\rvert}\sqrt{2}^{\,k(\eta/\zeta)+k(\theta/\zeta)}.

Proposition 7.11
.

Suppose
\eta,\theta\in\mathscr{D}
. Let
d=\lvert\theta\rvert-\lvert\eta\rvert
, and let
r
be the number of parts of
\eta
greater than
|d|
. Then

B_{\eta,\theta}=\begin{cases}(-1)^{r}\sqrt{2}&\text{if $\theta=\eta\setminus(-d)$;}\\
(-1)^{r}&\text{if $\theta=\eta$;}\\
(-1)^{r+d}\sqrt{2}&\text{if $\theta=\eta\sqcup(d)$;}\\
0&\text{otherwise.}\end{cases}

We prove
7.11
by iteratively removing columns from
\eta
and
\theta
.
Fix
\eta
and
\theta
, and for
c\geqslant 1
, let
\Delta_{c}=\eta^{\prime}_{c}-\theta^{\prime}_{c}
be the difference between the length of the
c
th columns in
\eta
and
\theta
.
For any
\zeta\in\mathscr{D}
, let
\check{\zeta}
be the strict partition obtained by deleting the first column.

The iterative step hinges on the following
Lemma
.

Lemma 7.12
.

Suppose
\eta,\theta\in\mathscr{D}
. Then

B_{\eta,\theta}=\begin{cases}\phantom{-\sqrt{2}}B_{\check{\eta},\check{\theta}}&\text{if $\Delta_{1}=\Delta_{2}=1$, or if $\Delta_{1}=\Delta_{2}=0$ and $\eta^{\prime}_{1}-\eta^{\prime}_{2}=0$;}\\
\phantom{\sqrt{2}}{-}B_{\check{\eta},\check{\theta}}&\text{if $\Delta_{1}=\Delta_{2}=-1$, or if $\Delta_{1}=\Delta_{2}=0$ and $\eta^{\prime}_{1}-\eta^{\prime}_{2}=1$;}\\
\phantom{-}\sqrt{2}B_{\check{\eta},\check{\theta}}&\text{if $\Delta_{1}=1$ and $\Delta_{2}=0$;}\\
-\sqrt{2}B_{\check{\eta},\check{\theta}}&\text{if $\Delta_{1}=-1$ and $\Delta_{2}=0$;}\\
\phantom{-\sqrt{2}B}0&\text{if $\Delta_{1}=0$ and $\Delta_{2}\neq 0$, or if $\lvert\Delta_{c}\rvert>1$ for any $c$.}\end{cases}

Observe that for any
\zeta\in\mathcal{I}_{0}(\eta,\theta)
, we must have
\eta^{\prime}_{c},\theta^{\prime}_{c}\in\left\{\zeta^{\prime}_{c},\zeta^{\prime}_{c}+1\right\}
for all
c
.
In particular, if
\lvert\Delta_{c}\rvert>1
for any
c
then
\mathcal{I}_{0}(\eta,\theta)=\varnothing
and
B_{\eta,\theta}=0
as claimed. For the remainder of the proof, assume
\lvert\Delta_{c}\rvert\leqslant 1
for all
c
.

For
\zeta\in\mathcal{I}_{0}(\eta,\theta)
, let
b^{\zeta}_{\eta,\theta}=(-1)^{\lvert\theta/\zeta\rvert}\sqrt{2}^{k(\eta/\zeta)+k(\theta/\zeta)}
denote the contribution to
B_{\eta,\theta}
from
\zeta
.
Our strategy is to compare
b^{\zeta}_{\eta,\theta}
and
b^{\check{\zeta}}_{\check{\eta},\check{\theta}}
.
We claim that
\zeta\mapsto\check{\zeta}
defines a surjective function from
\mathcal{I}_{0}(\eta,\theta)
to
\mathcal{I}_{0}(\check{\eta},\check{\theta})
, so that our strategy does consider all contributions to
B_{\check{\eta},\check{\theta}}
.
Indeed, it is clear that if
\zeta\in\mathcal{I}_{0}(\eta,\theta)
then
\check{\zeta}\in\mathcal{I}_{0}(\check{\eta},\check{\theta})
.
For surjectivity, consider a strict partition
\delta\in\mathcal{I}_{0}(\check{\eta},\check{\theta})
, and observe that
\delta^{\prime}_{1}\leqslant\eta^{\prime}_{2}\leqslant\eta^{\prime}_{1}
and also that
\eta^{\prime}_{1}\leqslant\eta^{\prime}_{2}+1\leqslant\delta^{\prime}_{1}+2
, and likewise for
\theta
, so that

\delta^{\prime}_{1}\leqslant\eta^{\prime}_{1},\theta^{\prime}_{1}\leqslant\delta^{\prime}_{1}+2.

Since we assume
\lvert\Delta_{1}\rvert\leqslant 1
, we then have that either
\{\eta^{\prime}_{1},\theta^{\prime}_{1}\}\subseteq\{\delta^{\prime}_{1},\delta^{\prime}_{1}+1\}
and hence adjoining a column of length
\delta^{\prime}_{1}
to
\delta
yields an element of
\mathcal{I}_{0}(\eta,\theta)
, or
\{\eta^{\prime}_{1},\theta^{\prime}_{1}\}\subseteq\{\delta^{\prime}_{1}+1,\delta^{\prime}_{1}+2\}
and hence adjoining a column of length
\delta^{\prime}_{1}+1
to
\delta
yields an element of
\mathcal{I}_{0}(\eta,\theta)
.
(Note also that these are the only possible column lengths that can be adjoined to
\delta
to obtain a strict partition, and thus every element of
\mathcal{I}_{0}(\check{\eta},\check{\theta})
has a preimage of size
1
or
2
.)

To compare the contributions
b^{\zeta}_{\eta,\theta}
and
b^{\check{\zeta}}_{\check{\eta},\check{\theta}}
, we break down into several cases.

\Delta_{1}=1
:

In this case
\zeta^{\prime}_{1}=\theta^{\prime}_{1}=\eta^{\prime}_{1}-1
for every
\zeta\in\mathcal{I}_{0}(\eta,\theta)
, and it follows that
\zeta\mapsto\check{\zeta}
is a bijection.
Clearly
\lvert\theta/\zeta\rvert=\lvert\check{\theta}/\check{\zeta}\rvert
for every
\zeta\in\mathcal{I}_{0}(\eta,\theta)
.
Note that column
1
contributes to
k(\eta/\zeta)
if and only if
\eta^{\prime}_{2}=\zeta^{\prime}_{2}
, while column
1
contributes to
k(\theta/\zeta)
if and only if
\theta^{\prime}_{2}=\zeta^{\prime}_{2}+1
, and thus

k(\eta/\zeta)=\begin{cases}k(\check{\eta}/\check{\zeta})+1&\text{if }\eta^{\prime}_{2}=\zeta^{\prime}_{2}\\
k(\check{\eta}/\check{\zeta})&\text{if }\eta^{\prime}_{2}=\zeta^{\prime}_{2}+1,\end{cases}\qquad k(\theta/\zeta)=\begin{cases}k(\check{\theta}/\check{\zeta})&\text{if }\theta^{\prime}_{2}=\zeta^{\prime}_{2}\\
k(\check{\theta}/\check{\zeta})+1&\text{if }\theta^{\prime}_{2}=\zeta^{\prime}_{2}+1,\end{cases}

and hence

k(\eta/\zeta)+k(\theta/\zeta)=\begin{cases}k(\check{\eta}/\check{\zeta})+k(\check{\theta}/\check{\zeta})+1&\text{if $\Delta_{2}=0$;}\\
k(\check{\eta}/\check{\zeta})+k(\check{\theta}/\check{\zeta})&\text{if $\Delta_{2}=1$.}\end{cases}

Therefore

b^{\zeta}_{\eta,\theta}=\begin{cases}\sqrt{2}b^{\check{\zeta}}_{\check{\eta},\check{\theta}}&\text{if $\Delta_{2}=0$;}\\[5.0pt]
\phantom{\sqrt{2}}b^{\check{\zeta}}_{\check{\eta},\check{\theta}}&\text{if $\Delta_{2}=1$.}\end{cases}

for every
\zeta\in\mathcal{I}_{0}(\eta,\theta)
. Summing over
\zeta
gives the claimed values when
\Delta_{1}=1
.

\Delta_{1}=-1
:

This is very similar to the previous case; now
\lvert\theta/\zeta\rvert=\lvert\check{\theta}/\check{\zeta}\rvert+1
for every
\zeta
, and the formulæ from the last case apply with
\eta
and
\theta
interchanged and a change of sign in the last formula.

\Delta_{1}=0
and
\Delta_{2}\neq 0
:

We consider the case
\Delta_{2}=1
; the case
\Delta_{2}=-1
is obtained by interchanging
\eta
and
\theta
in what follows.
Having
\Delta_{1}=0
and
\Delta_{2}=1
implies
\zeta^{\prime}_{2}=\theta^{\prime}_{2}=\eta^{\prime}_{2}-1
for every
\zeta\in\mathcal{I}_{0}(\eta,\theta)
, while
\zeta^{\prime}_{1}
can be either
\eta^{\prime}_{1}=\theta^{\prime}_{1}
or
\eta^{\prime}_{1}-1=\theta^{\prime}_{1}-1
.
So for each
\delta\in\mathcal{I}_{0}(\check{\eta},\check{\theta})
there are two partitions
\zeta^{+},\zeta^{-}\in\mathcal{I}_{0}(\eta,\theta)
satisfying
\check{\zeta^{+}}=\check{\zeta^{-}}=\delta
: we obtain
\zeta^{+}
by adjoining a column of length
\eta^{\prime}_{1}
to
\delta
, and
\zeta^{-}
by adjoining a column of length
\eta^{\prime}_{1}-1
.
But now
\lvert\theta/\zeta^{-}\rvert-\lvert\theta/\zeta^{+}\rvert=1
(and also
\lvert\eta/\zeta^{-}\rvert-\lvert\eta/\zeta^{+}\rvert=1
), while

k(\eta/\zeta^{-})=k(\check{\eta}/\delta)+1=k(\eta/\zeta^{+})+1,\qquad k(\theta/\zeta^{+})=k(\check{\theta}/\delta)+1=k(\theta/\zeta^{-})+1,

so that
b^{\zeta^{+}}_{\eta,\theta}+b^{\zeta^{-}}_{\eta,\theta}=0
.
This applies for all
\delta\in\mathcal{I}_{0}(\check{\eta},\check{\theta})
, giving
B_{\eta,\theta}=0
, as required.

\Delta_{1}=\Delta_{2}=0
and
\eta^{\prime}_{1}-\eta^{\prime}_{2}=0
:

For each
\delta\in\mathcal{I}_{0}(\check{\eta},\check{\theta})
with
\delta^{\prime}_{1}=\eta^{\prime}_{2}
, there is a unique
\zeta\in\mathcal{I}_{0}(\eta,\theta)
satisfying
\check{\zeta}=\delta
, obtained by adding a column of length
\eta^{\prime}_{1}
to
\delta
. This
\zeta
satisfies

\lvert\theta/\zeta\rvert=\lvert\check{\theta}/\delta\rvert,\qquad k(\eta/\zeta)=k(\check{\eta}/\delta),\qquad k(\theta/\zeta)=k(\check{\theta}/\delta)

so that
b^{\zeta}_{\eta,\theta}=b^{\delta}_{\check{\eta},\check{\theta}}
.

For each
\delta\in\mathcal{I}_{0}(\check{\eta},\check{\theta})
with
\delta^{\prime}_{1}=\eta^{\prime}_{2}-1
, there are two partitions
\zeta^{+},\zeta^{-}\in\mathcal{I}_{0}(\eta,\theta)
satisfying
\check{\zeta^{+}}=\check{\zeta^{-}}=\delta
, obtained from
\delta
by adding columns of length
\eta^{\prime}_{1}
and
\eta^{\prime}_{1}-1
respectively. These partitions satisfy

\lvert\theta/\zeta^{-}\rvert=\lvert\check{\theta}/\delta\rvert+1=\lvert\theta/\zeta^{+}\rvert+1,

k(\eta/\zeta^{+})=k(\check{\eta}/\delta)+1=k(\eta/\zeta^{-})+1,\qquad k(\theta/\zeta^{+})=k(\check{\theta}/\delta)+1=k(\theta/\zeta^{-})+1,

and therefore
b^{\zeta^{+}}_{\eta,\theta}=2b^{\delta}_{\check{\eta},\check{\theta}}
and
b^{\zeta^{-}}_{\eta,\theta}=-b^{\delta}_{\check{\eta},\check{\theta}}
.
Hence

b^{\delta^{+}}_{\eta,\theta}+b^{\delta^{-}}_{\eta,\theta}=b^{\delta}_{\check{\eta},\check{\theta}}.

Now summing over
\mathcal{I}_{0}(\eta,\theta)
gives
B_{\eta,\theta}=B_{\check{\eta},\check{\theta}}
, as required.

\Delta_{1}=\Delta_{2}=0
and
\eta^{\prime}_{1}-\eta^{\prime}_{2}=1
:

This case is similar to the previous one.
Now for each
\delta\in\mathcal{I}_{0}(\check{\eta},\check{\theta})
with
\delta^{\prime}_{1}=\eta^{\prime}_{2}-1
there is a unique
\zeta\in\mathcal{I}_{0}(\eta,\theta)
such that
\check{\zeta}=\delta
, and this
\zeta
satisfies
b^{\zeta}_{\eta,\theta}=-b^{\delta}_{\check{\eta},\check{\theta}}
.
For each
\delta
with
\delta^{\prime}_{1}=\eta^{\prime}_{2}
, there are two partitions
\zeta^{+},\zeta^{-}\in\mathcal{I}_{0}(\eta,\theta)
such that
\check{\zeta^{+}}=\check{\zeta^{-}}=\delta
, and these partitions satisfy
b^{\zeta^{+}}_{\eta,\theta}=b^{\delta}_{\eta,\theta}
and
b^{\zeta^{-}}_{\eta,\theta}=-2b^{\delta}_{\check{\eta},\check{\theta}}
. Summing over
\mathcal{I}_{0}(\eta,\theta)
then gives
B_{\eta,\theta}=-B_{\check{\eta},\check{\theta}}
.
This case is illustrated
Figure

4
.
∎

Figure 4
:
A comparison of the calculations of
B_{\eta,\theta}
and
B_{\check{\eta},\check{\theta}}
when
\eta=(7,6,2,1)
and
\theta=(6,5,3,1)
(and hence
\check{\eta}=(6,5,1)
and
\check{\theta}=(5,4,2)
).
This example falls into the final case (
\Delta_{1}=\Delta_{2}=0
and
\eta^{\prime}_{1}-\eta^{\prime}_{2}=1
) in the proof of
Lemma

7.12
.
On the left are the partitions in
\mathcal{I}_{0}(\eta,\theta)
, on the right the partitions in
\mathcal{I}_{0}(\check{\eta},\check{\theta})
, matched up according to the function
\zeta\mapsto\check{\zeta}
.
The Young diagrams of the partitions are drawn, with the nodes which must be added to form
\eta
or
\check{\eta}
shaded blue and the nodes which must be added to form
\theta
or
\check{\theta}
shaded yellow (and the nodes which must be added in either case shaded green).

Let
\Delta=(\Delta_{1},\Delta_{2},\ldots)
be the sequence of column differences between
\eta
and
\theta
.
Observe that
\eta=\theta\sqcup(-d)
if and only if
\Delta=(1,\ldots,1,0,\ldots,0)
where the number of
1
s is
-d
, that
\theta=\eta\sqcup(d)
if and only if
\Delta=(-1,\ldots,-1,0,\ldots,0)
, where the number of
-1
s is
d
, and trivially
\eta=\theta
if and only if
\Delta=(0,\ldots 0)
.
So we are required to show
B_{\eta,\theta}
takes the claimed values when
\Delta
is of one of these forms, and is zero otherwise.

We do so using
Lemma

7.12
iteratively.
The fifth case of
Lemma

7.12
tells us that if
\Delta
has any terms strictly greater than
1
in absolute value, or if
\Delta
has any non-zero term following a
0
, then
B_{\eta,\theta}=0
.
Meanwhile
\Delta
cannot contain a
1
followed by a
-1
, for if
\Delta_{c}=1
then
\eta^{\prime}_{c+1}\geqslant\eta^{\prime}_{c}-1=\theta^{\prime}_{c}\geqslant\theta^{\prime}_{c+1}
and hence
\Delta_{c+1}\geqslant 0
, and similarly
\Delta
cannot contain a
-1
followed by a
1
.
Thus if
B_{\eta,\theta}\neq 0
, then
\Delta
is of the form
(1,\ldots,1,0,\ldots,0)
,
(-1,\ldots,-1,0,\ldots,0)
or
(0,\ldots 0)
, where the number of non-zero entries is
\lvert d\rvert
.

If
\Delta=(0,\ldots,0)
, the first and second cases of
Lemma

7.12
tell us that, as we remove columns from
\eta
and
\theta
, we pick up a sign every time a column length decreases (by
1
, the only possibly amount by which a column length can decrease in a strict partition). Thus
B_{\eta,\theta}=(-1)^{l(\eta)}=(-1)^{r}
in this case, as required.

If
\Delta=(1,\ldots,1,0,\ldots,0)
, the first case of
Lemma

7.12
tells us that removing the columns which differ by
1
do not alter the value of
B_{\eta,\theta}
until the last, when the third case tells us that we pick up a factor of
\sqrt{2}
. Then the remaining columns are those meeting parts greater than
\lvert d\rvert
, so arguing as in the previous paragraph gives
B_{\eta,\theta}=(-1)^{r}\sqrt{2}
as required.
If
\Delta=(-1,\ldots,-1,0,\ldots,0)
, a similar argument applies but using the second and fourth cases of
Lemma

7.12
, from which we pick up extra signs for each of the
d
differing columns, yielding
B_{\eta,\theta}=(-1)^{r+d}\sqrt{2}
as required.
∎

Combining
7.10
and
7.11
then yields the following. (Recall that given partitions
\sigma
and
\tau
, we write
A_{\sigma,\tau}=\lvert\mathcal{I}_{1}(\sigma,\tau)\rvert
.)

Theorem 7.13
.

Suppose
\alpha\in\mathscr{D}
has
4
-bar-core
\gamma
and
4
-bar-weight
w
. Let
d\in\mathbb{Z}
. Let
\epsilon
be the residue of
l(\mathrm{dbl}(\gamma))+1
modulo
2
. Suppose
\max\{w,w+d\}\leqslant l(\mathrm{dbl}(\gamma))+1
.
Write
\alpha=(\gamma+4\sigma)\sqcup 2\eta
, where
\eta\in\mathscr{D}
and
\sigma\in\mathscr{P}
with
2\lvert\sigma\rvert+\lvert\eta\rvert=w
. Let
r
be the number of parts of
\eta
greater than
\lvert d\rvert
.
Then

R_{\epsilon}^{(d)}\mathopen{\hbox{${\langle}$}\kern-2.72221pt\hbox{${\langle}$}}\alpha\mathclose{\hbox{${\rangle}$}\kern-2.72221pt\hbox{${\rangle}$}}=\begin{cases}\hphantom{(-1)^{r+d}\sqrt{2}}\mathllap{(-1)^{r}\sqrt{2}}\ \displaystyle\sum_{\tau}A_{\sigma,\tau}\mathopen{\hbox{${\langle}$}\kern-2.72221pt\hbox{${\langle}$}}(\gamma+4\tau)\sqcup 2(\eta\setminus(-d))\mathclose{\hbox{${\rangle}$}\kern-2.72221pt\hbox{${\rangle}$}}&\text{if $d<0$ and $-d\in\eta$;}\\[12.0pt]
\hphantom{(-1)^{r+d}\sqrt{2}}\mathllap{(-1)^{r}}\ \displaystyle\sum_{\tau}A_{\sigma,\tau}\mathopen{\hbox{${\langle}$}\kern-2.72221pt\hbox{${\langle}$}}(\gamma+4\tau)\sqcup 2\eta\mathclose{\hbox{${\rangle}$}\kern-2.72221pt\hbox{${\rangle}$}}&\text{if $d=0$;}\\[12.0pt]
(-1)^{r+d}\sqrt{2}\ \displaystyle\sum_{\tau}A_{\sigma,\tau}\mathopen{\hbox{${\langle}$}\kern-2.72221pt\hbox{${\langle}$}}(\gamma+4\tau)\sqcup 2(\eta\sqcup(d))\mathclose{\hbox{${\rangle}$}\kern-2.72221pt\hbox{${\rangle}$}}&\text{if $d>0$ and $d\not\in\eta$;}\\[10.0pt]
\hphantom{(-1)^{r+d}\sqrt{2}}\ \ \;0&\text{otherwise;}\end{cases}

where in each case the sum is over partitions
\tau
with
\lvert\tau\rvert=\lvert\sigma\rvert
.

Unfortunately it does not seem to be possible to give a simpler expression for
A_{\sigma,\tau}
. Nevertheless, we can deduce the result we need.

Corollary 7.14
.

Suppose
\alpha\in\mathscr{D}
with
4
-bar-core
\gamma
and suppose
\alpha=\gamma\sqcup 2\eta
for some
\eta\in\mathscr{D}
.
Let
d\in\mathbb{Z}
.
Let
\epsilon
be the residue of
l(\mathrm{dbl}(\gamma))+1
modulo
2
.
Suppose
\max\{\lvert\eta\rvert,\lvert\eta\rvert+d\}\leqslant l(\mathrm{dbl}(\gamma))+1
.
Then

R_{\epsilon}^{(d)}\mathopen{\hbox{${\langle}$}\kern-2.72221pt\hbox{${\langle}$}}\alpha\mathclose{\hbox{${\rangle}$}\kern-2.72221pt\hbox{${\rangle}$}}=\begin{cases}\hphantom{(-1)^{r+d}\sqrt{2}}\mathllap{(-1)^{r}\sqrt{2}}\mathopen{\hbox{${\langle}$}\kern-2.72221pt\hbox{${\langle}$}}\gamma\sqcup 2(\eta\setminus(-d))\mathclose{\hbox{${\rangle}$}\kern-2.72221pt\hbox{${\rangle}$}}&\text{if $d<0$ and $-d\in\eta$;}\\[5.0pt]
\hphantom{(-1)^{r+d}\sqrt{2}}\mathllap{(-1)^{r}}\mathopen{\hbox{${\langle}$}\kern-2.72221pt\hbox{${\langle}$}}\gamma\sqcup 2\eta\mathclose{\hbox{${\rangle}$}\kern-2.72221pt\hbox{${\rangle}$}}\par&\text{if $d=0$;}\\[5.0pt]
(-1)^{r+d}\sqrt{2}\mathopen{\hbox{${\langle}$}\kern-2.72221pt\hbox{${\langle}$}}\gamma\sqcup 2(\eta\sqcup(d))\mathclose{\hbox{${\rangle}$}\kern-2.72221pt\hbox{${\rangle}$}}&\text{if $d>0$ and $d\not\in\eta$;}\\[3.0pt]
\hphantom{(-1)^{r+d}\sqrt{2}}\;0&\text{otherwise.}\end{cases}

Set
\sigma=\varnothing
in
7.13
, which forces
\tau=\varnothing
and hence
A_{\sigma,\tau}=1
.
∎

We can now deduce the case required in our application, restated below.

See
5.12

This follows from
7.14
by observing that
(\kappa_{r}+\kappa_{s})\setminus(-(s-r-1))=\kappa_{r+1}+\kappa_{s-1}
.
∎

The results required in
Section

5
are now all proved, and thus our main theorem is established.
